Hydrocarbons — Alkanes, Alkenes, Alkynes, Aromatic Hydrocarbons, Petroleum Refining, Cracking & Combustion

Deep dive into hydrocarbon chemistry — the structure, bonding, and reactivity of alkanes (saturated hydrocarbons with C–C single bonds and sp³ tetrahedral geometry), alkenes (unsaturated with C=C double bonds, π-bonding, and cis–trans isomerism), alkynes (C≡C triple bonds with sp hybridization and linear geometry), aromatic hydrocarbons (benzene and Hückel's (4n+2)π rule), petroleum refining (fractional distillation based on boiling points), cracking (thermal and catalytic cracking, reforming), and combustion chemistry (complete and incomplete combustion, knocking, octane number).

"Hydrocarbons are the simplest organic compounds — yet they form the backbone of the fuels that power civilization and the raw materials for synthetic chemistry."
— August Kekulé
Part 1

🔗 Alkanes and Alkenes: Sigma Bonds, Pi Bonds, and Isomerism

Hydrocarbon Families
Alkanes (CnH2n+2), Alkenes (CnH2n), Bonding, Nomenclature, and Geometric Isomerism
Alkanes are saturated hydrocarbons with general formula CnH2n+2. All C–C and C–H bonds are σ-bonds formed by sp³–sp³ or sp³–s overlap. Each carbon is sp³ hybridized with tetrahedral geometry (109.5° bond angles). Free rotation about C–C single bonds allows conformational isomerism. Structural isomerism (chain and position) arises from different carbon skeletons. IUPAC nomenclature uses the -ane suffix. Alkanes are relatively inert (paraffins = "little affinity") — they undergo only free-radical substitution (halogenation) and combustion. Alkenes are unsaturated with at least one C=C double bond (general formula CnH2n). Each double-bond carbon is sp² hybridized (trigonal planar, 120°). The double bond consists of a strong σ-bond (sp²–sp² overlap) and a weaker π-bond (sideways p–p overlap). Restricted rotation around C=C gives rise to cis–trans (geometric) isomerism when each double-bond carbon has two different substituents. The C=C bond is electron-rich and undergoes electrophilic addition reactions (with HX, X₂, H₂O). Markovnikov's rule predicts the regiochemistry: H adds to the less substituted carbon. Baeyer's test (alkaline KMnO₄) detects unsaturation — the purple permanganate is decolorized. Alkenes also undergo polymerization (addition polymers like polyethylene).
Alkanes have only σ-bonds, making them relatively unreactive; the π-bond in alkenes provides a site for addition reactions and enables geometric isomerism due to restricted rotation.
Part 2

⚗️ Alkynes and Aromatic Hydrocarbons: Triple Bonds and the Benzene Ring

Unsaturation and Aromaticity
Alkynes (CnH2n−2), sp Hybridization, Benzene, Resonance, and Hückel's Rule
Alkynes contain at least one C≡C triple bond (general formula CnH2n−2). Each triple-bond carbon is sp hybridized (linear, 180°). The triple bond comprises one σ-bond (sp–sp) and two π-bonds (from two sets of perpendicular p orbitals). Terminal alkynes (R–C≡C–H) have acidic hydrogen (pKₐ ≈ 25) due to the high s-character of the C–H bond. Alkynes undergo addition reactions similarly to alkenes but can add two equivalents of H₂, X₂, or HX. Aromatic hydrocarbons contain a planar ring of atoms with a conjugated π-system. The prototype is benzene (C₆H₆), proposed by Kekulé (1865) as a six-membered ring with alternating single and double bonds. In reality, benzene is a resonance hybrid with equal C–C bond lengths (140 pm) intermediate between single (154 pm) and double (134 pm). The six p orbitals perpendicular to the ring overlap to form a delocalized π-system above and below the plane. This delocalization provides resonance stabilization energy (~150 kJ/mol), making benzene much more stable than the hypothetical "cyclohexatriene". Hückel's rule: a planar, cyclic, fully conjugated compound is aromatic if it contains (4n+2) π-electrons (n = 0,1,2...). Aromatic compounds undergo electrophilic aromatic substitution (EAS) — nitration, halogenation, sulfonation, Friedel–Crafts alkylation/acylation — rather than addition, preserving the aromatic stabilization.
Alkynes have linear sp-hybridized carbons and acidic terminal hydrogens; aromatic hydrocarbons achieve exceptional stability through delocalized π-electrons as described by Hückel's (4n+2)π rule.
Part 3

🛢️ Petroleum Refining and Cracking: From Crude Oil to Fuels

Industrial Chemistry
Fractional Distillation, Thermal and Catalytic Cracking, Reforming, and Octane Rating
Crude oil (petroleum) is a complex mixture of hydrocarbons (alkanes, cycloalkanes, aromatics) formed from ancient organic matter under high pressure and temperature over millions of years. Fractional distillation separates crude oil into fractions based on boiling point in a fractionating column. The column has a temperature gradient (hot at bottom, cool at top). Lighter fractions with lower boiling points (refinery gas C₁–C₄, gasoline C₅–C₁₀, naphtha C₆–C₁₂) condense higher up; heavier fractions (kerosene C₁₁–C₁₃, diesel C₁₄–C₁₈, fuel oil C₁₉–C₂₅, asphalt >C₂₅) condense lower. Cracking breaks large hydrocarbon molecules into smaller, more valuable ones. Thermal cracking (high temperature ~700°C, high pressure) produces alkenes via free-radical mechanisms. Catalytic cracking (zeolite catalysts, ~500°C, moderate pressure) is more efficient and gives higher gasoline yield with better octane rating. The mechanism involves carbocation intermediates. Hydrocracking combines cracking with hydrogenation to produce saturated products. Reforming (catalytic reforming) converts straight-chain alkanes into branched alkanes, cycloalkanes, and aromatic compounds — all of which have higher octane numbers. The octane scale: iso-octane (2,2,4-trimethylpentane) = 100, n-heptane = 0. Branched and aromatic hydrocarbons resist knocking (auto-ignition) better than straight-chain alkanes.
Cracking transforms heavy, low-demand fractions into lighter, high-demand products, while reforming and isomerization improve fuel quality by producing branched and aromatic hydrocarbons with higher octane numbers.
Part 4

🔥 Combustion and Environmental Impact: Energy, Emissions, and Knock

Energy and Environment
Complete and Incomplete Combustion, Greenhouse Gases, Octane Number, and Catalytic Converters
Combustion (burning) is the rapid exothermic reaction of a hydrocarbon with oxygen. Complete combustion (sufficient O₂): CxHy + (x + y/4)O₂ → xCO₂ + (y/2)H₂O + heat. This is highly exothermic (ΔH ≈ −890 kJ/mol for methane) — the energy that heats homes, powers engines, and generates electricity. Incomplete combustion (limited O₂) produces carbon monoxide (CO) (a poisonous gas that binds hemoglobin 200× more strongly than O₂) and carbon (soot). CO is a major air pollutant. Knocking (engine knock) occurs when the fuel–air mixture auto-ignites prematurely in the cylinder before the spark plug fires, producing damaging pressure waves. The octane number measures a fuel's resistance to knocking. Higher octane fuels allow higher compression ratios (more efficient engines). Lead compounds (tetraethyllead) were historically used as antiknock agents but have been phased out due to toxicity. Modern alternatives include oxygenates (ethanol, MTBE). Catalytic converters in vehicle exhaust systems use platinum, palladium, and rhodium catalysts to convert CO → CO₂, unburnt hydrocarbons → CO₂ + H₂O, and NOₓ → N₂. Environmental impact: CO₂ from hydrocarbon combustion is the primary greenhouse gas driving climate change. Sulfur impurities in crude oil produce SO₂ (acid rain). Cleaner fuels and emissions control technologies continue to evolve.
The exothermic combustion of hydrocarbons provides most of the world's energy, but incomplete combustion produces toxic CO and soot, while CO₂ emissions contribute to climate change — requiring both cleaner combustion and emissions control technologies.

🎯 Practice

← Back to Chemistry

Free‑Radical Halogenation of Alkanes: Mechanism, Selectivity, and Industrial Relevance

Free‑radical halogenation proceeds through three stages: initiation, propagation, and termination. Initiation requires homolytic cleavage of the halogen–halogen bond, typically achieved by UV light or heat (ΔH ≈ +242 kJ mol⁻¹ for Cl₂, +193 kJ mol⁻¹ for Br₂). The resulting halogen radicals abstract hydrogen atoms from the alkane in the first propagation step, forming a carbon‑centered radical and HX. The carbon radical then reacts with a second halogen molecule to give the haloalkane and regenerate a halogen radical, sustaining the chain.

Selectivity is governed by the relative stability of the carbon radicals formed. Tertiary C–H bonds (bond dissociation energy ≈ 380 kJ mol⁻¹) are abstracted fastest, followed by secondary (≈ 395 kJ mol⁻¹) and primary (≈ 410 kJ mol⁻¹). For chlorination at 25 °C the relative rate constants are roughly 5 : 3.8 : 1 (tertiary : secondary : primary), whereas bromination shows a much larger discrimination (≈ 1600 : 80 : 1) because the H‑abstraction step is endothermic for bromine and the transition state resembles the radical product. Fluorination is essentially non‑selective and explosively exothermic; iodination is thermodynamically unfavorable (ΔH > 0) and does not proceed under normal conditions.

In industrial practice, chlorination of methane yields a mixture of CH₃Cl, CH₂Cl₂, CHCl₃, and CCl₄. Control of the Cl₂ : CH₄ ratio and reaction temperature limits polysubstitution. Bromination is used selectively for allylic and benzylic positions where the resulting radical is resonance‑stabilised. The radical chain length can exceed 10⁴ cycles, making the process highly efficient. Termination steps (radical–radical combination) become significant only at very high radical concentrations or in the presence of inhibitors such as oxygen, which forms peroxy radicals that do not propagate the chain.

Side reactions include rearrangements of carbon radicals (rare, because radical rearrangements have higher activation barriers than carbocation shifts) and the formation of minor amounts of alkenes via β‑scission of secondary or tertiary radicals at elevated temperatures. Understanding these nuances allows chemists to predict product distributions and to design selective halogenation routes for synthesis of pharmaceuticals and agrochemicals.

Electrophilic Addition to Alkenes: Regioselectivity, Stereochemistry, and Carbocation Rearrangements

Electrophilic addition to a C=C double bond follows a two‑step pathway: formation of a carbocation intermediate by attack of the electrophile (H⁺, X⁺, or a π‑complex) on the alkene, followed by nucleophilic capture. Markovnikov’s rule states that the electrophile adds to the carbon bearing the greater number of alkyl substituents, generating the more stable carbocation (tertiary > secondary > primary). The stability order reflects hyperconjugation and inductive donation from alkyl groups.

When a more stable carbocation can be accessed by a 1,2‑hydride or 1,2‑alkyl shift, rearrangement occurs before nucleophilic attack. For example, addition of HCl to 3‑methyl‑1‑butene initially forms a secondary carbocation, which rapidly undergoes a hydride shift to give a tertiary carbocation, leading to 2‑chloro‑3‑methylbutane as the major product. Such rearrangements are a classic examiner trap: the product distribution does not follow the simple Markovnikov prediction if a shift is feasible.

Stereochemistry is dictated by the planar sp²‑hybridised carbocation. Nucleophilic attack can occur from either face, giving a racemic mixture when a new stereogenic centre is created. In the presence of a neighbouring chiral centre, diastereomeric transition states may lead to modest diastereoselectivity (Felkin‑Anh model). Halogen addition (Br₂, Cl₂) proceeds via a cyclic halonium ion, enforcing anti‑addition: the two halogen atoms add to opposite faces of the former double bond. This stereospecificity is exploited in synthesis to set defined relative configurations.

Regioselectivity can be reversed under radical conditions (anti‑Markovnikov addition of HBr in the presence of peroxides) or by using hydroboration‑oxidation, which delivers the alcohol at the less substituted carbon with syn‑stereochemistry. Oxymercuration‑demercuration provides Markovnikov hydration without carbocation rearrangements because the mercurinium ion intermediate is not a free carbocation. These complementary methods give chemists a toolbox for controlling both regio‑ and stereochemical outcomes.

Mechanistic Reasoning in Halogen Addition
Predict the major product(s) and stereochemistry when 1‑methylcyclohexene reacts with Br₂ in CCl₄ at 0 °C. Explain any rearrangements.
The reaction proceeds via a bromonium ion intermediate. The π‑bond attacks Br₂, forming a three‑membered ring with a positive charge on bromine. Nucleophilic attack by Br⁻ occurs anti to the bridge, opening the ring at the more substituted carbon (C‑1) because the partial positive charge is greater there. No carbocation rearrangement occurs because the bromonium ion is not a free carbocation. The product is trans‑1,2‑dibromo‑1‑methylcyclohexane, a racemic mixture of enantiomers due to attack from either face of the planar bromonium ion.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for the product of an electrophilic addition, first identify the electrophile and the possible carbocation intermediates. Check for 1,2‑shifts before writing the final structure. For halogen addition, remember the anti‑stereochemistry imposed by the halonium ion; draw both enantiomers if a new chiral centre is formed. If peroxides are mentioned, switch to the radical anti‑Markovnikov pathway for HBr only.

Terminal Alkyne Acidity, Metal Acetylide Formation, and Synthetic Applications

Terminal alkynes (RC≡CH) possess a relatively acidic hydrogen (pKₐ ≈ 25) because the resulting acetylide anion is stabilised by the high s‑character (sp) of the carbon bearing the negative charge. This acidity is significantly greater than that of alkenes (pKₐ ≈ 44) and alkanes (pKₐ ≈ 50). Strong bases such as sodium amide (NaNH₂, pKₐ of conjugate acid ≈ 35), n‑butyllithium (n‑BuLi), or Grignard reagents deprotonate terminal alkynes quantitatively at low temperature (−78 °C to 0 °C) to give metal acetylides (RC≡C⁻ M⁺).

The acetylide anion is a potent nucleophile and a strong base. It undergoes SN2 reactions with primary alkyl halides and tosylates to forge new C–C bonds, extending the carbon chain by two carbons. Reaction with secondary or tertiary halides leads predominantly to elimination (E2) because the acetylide is a strong, sterically hindered base. Coupling with carbonyl compounds (aldehydes, ketones) yields propargylic alcohols after protonation; subsequent reduction (Lindlar catalyst) or oxidation can provide diverse functional groups.

Transition‑metal catalysed cross‑coupling reactions (Sonogashira, Cadiot‑Chodkiewicz, Glaser) exploit the acetylide’s ability to transmetalate to Pd⁰ or Cu⁺ complexes. In the Sonogashira reaction, a terminal alkyne, a palladium catalyst (Pd(PPh₃)₂Cl₂), copper(I) iodide, and a base (Et₃N) couple an aryl or vinyl halide with the alkyne to give a conjugated enyne. The copper co‑catalyst forms a copper acetylide which transmetalates to the palladium centre, facilitating reductive elimination. These reactions tolerate a wide range of functional groups and are central to the synthesis of pharmaceuticals, natural products, and advanced materials.

Protection of the terminal alkyne as a trimethylsilyl (TMS) ether (RC≡C‑SiMe₃) is common when the acidic hydrogen would interfere with other reagents. Deprotection with fluoride (TBAF) or mild acid regenerates the free alkyne. Understanding the acidity, nucleophilicity, and metal‑acetylide chemistry enables strategic planning of multi‑step syntheses involving carbon‑carbon bond formation at the alkyne terminus.

Electrophilic Aromatic Substitution: Directing Effects, Activating/Deactivating Groups, and Poly‑Substitution Patterns

Electrophilic aromatic substitution (EAS) proceeds through a σ‑complex (Wheland intermediate) in which the aromatic π‑system donates electron density to the electrophile, temporarily losing aromaticity. The rate‑determining step is formation of this cationic intermediate; substituents on the ring modulate its stability. Electron‑donating groups (EDGs) such as –OH, –OR, –NH₂, –NHR, –NR₂, –alkyl activate the ring and direct incoming electrophiles to the ortho and para positions because the positive charge in the σ‑complex can be delocalised onto the substituent. Electron‑withdrawing groups (EWGs) such as –NO₂, –SO₃H, –CN, –CO₂R, –CHO, –COR deactivate the ring and are meta‑directing (except halogens, which are deactivating but ortho/para‑directing due to resonance donation competing with inductive withdrawal).

The relative activation/deactivation strength follows: –NH₂ > –OH > –OR > –NHCOR > –R > –Ph > –F > –Cl > –Br > –I > –CO₂R > –SO₃H > –NO₂. Strongly activating groups (–OH, –NH₂) allow substitution under very mild conditions (e.g., phenol nitration with dilute HNO₃ at 0 °C). Strongly deactivating groups (–NO₂) require harsh conditions (conc. HNO₃/H₂SO₄, 100 °C) and often give only mono‑substituted products because the ring becomes too electron‑poor for a second substitution.

Poly‑substitution patterns can be predicted by considering the directing effects of the first substituent. For example, nitration of toluene (–CH₃, ortho/para‑director) yields a mixture of ortho‑ and para‑nitrotoluene; the para isomer predominates due to steric hindrance at the ortho position. Subsequent nitration of p‑nitrotoluene places the second nitro group meta to the methyl (and ortho to the first nitro) because the nitro group is meta‑directing. In contrast, sulfonation of anisole (–OCH₃) gives mainly para‑methoxybenzenesulfonic acid; the sulfonic acid group can be removed later (desulfonation) to serve as a blocking group for selective ortho substitution.

Halogenation (Cl₂/FeCl₃, Br₂/FeBr₃) and Friedel‑Crafts alkylation/acylation follow the same directing principles. Friedel‑Crafts alkylation is prone to polyalkylation and carbocation rearrangements; acylation avoids rearrangement because the acylium ion (R‑C≡O⁺) is resonance‑stabilised and does not rearrange. The carbonyl product can be reduced (Clemmensen or Wolff‑Kishner) to give the alkylbenzene without rearrangement. Mastery of these directing effects and the ability to sequence reactions (protecting groups, blocking groups, reversible sulfonation) is essential for designing synthetic routes to polysubstituted aromatics.

Catalytic Reforming and Zeolite Cracking: Reaction Networks, Catalyst Design, and Octane Enhancement

Catalytic reforming converts low‑octane naphtha (C₆–C₁₀ straight‑chain alkanes) into high‑octane aromatics and branched isomers over a bifunctional Pt/Al₂O₃ catalyst at 480–520 °C and 10–30 atm H₂. The reaction network comprises three main pathways: (1) dehydrogenation of cyclohexanes to aromatics (e.g., methylcyclohexane → toluene + 3 H₂), (2) dehydrocyclisation of n‑paraffins to aromatics (e.g., n‑heptane → toluene + 4 H₂), and (3) isomerisation of n‑paraffins to iso‑paraffins (e.g., n‑heptane → 2‑methylhexane). Platinum sites catalyse dehydrogenation/hydrogenation steps, while the acidic alumina support provides carbocation‑mediated isomerisation and cyclisation. Hydrogen pressure suppresses coke formation and maintains catalyst activity.

Zeolite cracking (fluid catalytic cracking, FCC) uses a solid acid catalyst (typically USY zeolite in a silica‑alumina matrix) at 500–540 °C and near‑atmospheric pressure. The zeolite’s microporous structure (pore diameter ≈ 0.74 nm) imposes shape selectivity: only molecules that fit the pores access the strong Brønsted acid sites. The mechanism proceeds via protonation of a C–C bond to give a carbenium ion, β‑scission to produce a smaller olefin and a new carbenium ion, and subsequent hydride transfer to yield saturated products. The product slate is rich in C₃–C₅ olefins (propylene, butenes) and high‑octane gasoline range iso‑paraffins and aromatics formed by secondary reactions (oligomerisation, cyclisation, dehydrogenation).

Catalyst design balances acidity, pore architecture, and metal function. Rare‑earth exchanged USY (RE‑USY) enhances hydrothermal stability and acid site density. Addition of ZSM‑5 as a co‑catalyst boosts propylene yield by cracking linear olefins within its medium‑pore channels. Metal promoters (Pt, Pd) on the FCC catalyst enable mild hydrogenation of olefins to improve gasoline stability and reduce sulfur via hydrodesulfurisation. In reforming, chloride is added to the alumina to maintain optimal acidity; loss of chloride leads to decreased isomerisation activity.

Octane rating improvements stem from increased branching and aromatic content. Research octane number (RON) and motor octane number (MON) are measured in standard single‑cylinder engines; the anti‑knock index (AKI) = (RON + MON)/2 is displayed at pumps. Typical reformate has RON 95–105, while FCC gasoline ranges 90–95 RON. Blending of reformate, FCC gasoline, alkylate (from isobutane + butenes), and oxygenates (ethanol, MTBE) achieves target AKI while meeting sulfur, benzene, and volatility specifications. Understanding the interplay of reaction networks, catalyst properties, and product blending is crucial for refinery optimisation and for answering exam questions on process chemistry.

  • Assuming that all halogenations of alkanes follow the same selectivity trend; bromination is far more selective than chlorination.
  • Ignoring possible carbocation rearrangements in electrophilic addition to alkenes; a 1,2‑hydride or alkyl shift can change the major product.
  • Treating terminal alkyne acidity as comparable to alkenes; the sp‑hybridised carbon makes the hydrogen ≈10²⁵ times more acidic.
  • Believing that all electron‑withdrawing groups are meta‑directing; halogens are deactivating yet ortho/para‑directing because of resonance donation.
  • Overlooking the role of hydrogen pressure in catalytic reforming; it prevents coke but also shifts equilibria away from aromatisation.
  • Confusing the shape‑selectivity of zeolites with simple acid strength; pore size controls which carbocation intermediates can form.
  • Assuming that octane number is a single measured value; RON, MON, and AKI differ and are used in distinct regulatory contexts.

Hydroboration‑Oxidation and Oxymercuration‑Demercuration: Regioselectivity, Stereochemistry, and Synthetic Utility

Hydroboration‑oxidation converts an alkene into an alcohol with anti‑Markovnikov regioselectivity and syn‑addition stereochemistry. The reaction proceeds through a concerted, four‑center transition state in which the boron atom adds to the less substituted carbon of the double bond while the hydrogen adds to the more substituted carbon. Because the B–C bond forms simultaneously with the C–H bond, no carbocation intermediate is generated, eliminating rearrangements. The resulting trialkylborane is oxidised with alkaline hydrogen peroxide; the boron is replaced by a hydroxyl group with retention of configuration at the carbon bearing boron. This two‑step sequence therefore delivers the less‑substituted alcohol in a single stereochemical sense, making it indispensable for the synthesis of primary alcohols from terminal alkenes.

Oxymercuration‑demercuration, by contrast, follows Markovnikov regioselectivity but also proceeds without carbocation rearrangements. The alkene attacks mercuric acetate to give a cyclic mercurinium ion; water opens the three‑membered ring at the more substituted carbon, delivering a Markovnikov alcohol after reductive demercuration with NaBH₄. The mercurinium ion is a bridged, non‑classical cation that prevents hydride or alkyl shifts. Both methods are complementary: hydroboration‑oxidation gives anti‑Markovnikov products, while oxymercuration‑demercuration provides Markovnikov alcohols under neutral conditions, avoiding the strong acid required for direct hydration.

Stereochemical outcomes differ markedly. Hydroboration‑oxidation is syn‑addition; the newly formed C–H and C–OH bonds end up on the same face of the former double bond. In cyclic alkenes this leads to trans‑relationship between the hydroxyl group and any substituent originally on the same carbon. Oxymercuration‑demercuration proceeds via anti‑addition of water to the mercurinium ion, but the subsequent reduction step removes the mercury without affecting the stereocenter, so the overall addition is also anti. However, because the mercurinium ion is planar at the carbon bearing the positive charge, attack by water can occur from either face, often giving a mixture of diastereomers unless the substrate is rigid.

Industrial relevance: hydroboration‑oxidation is employed in the large‑scale production of 1‑hexanol from 1‑hexene for plasticiser synthesis, while oxymercuration‑demercuration finds use in the manufacture of pharmaceutical intermediates where acid‑sensitive functional groups must survive. Both reactions tolerate a wide range of functional groups (esters, ethers, halides) because they avoid strongly acidic or basic media.

Diels‑Alder Cycloaddition: Orbital Symmetry, Endo Rule, and Substituent Effects

The Diels‑Alder reaction is a [4+2] cycloaddition between a conjugated diene and a dienophile, proceeding through a single, concerted, pericyclic transition state that obeys the Woodward‑Hoffmann rules. The reaction is thermally allowed because the suprafacial‑suprafacial interaction involves six π‑electrons (4n+2, n=1), giving a HOMO‑LUMO overlap that is symmetry‑matched. Electron‑withdrawing groups on the dienophile lower its LUMO, while electron‑donating groups on the diene raise its HOMO, accelerating the reaction. Typical dienophiles include maleic anhydride, acrylates, and quinones; common dienes are cyclopentadiene, 1,3‑butadiene, and Danishefsky’s diene.

The endo rule predicts that substituents on the dienophile prefer to orient themselves under the π‑system of the diene in the transition state, maximising secondary orbital interactions between the dienophile’s π* orbitals and the diene’s HOMO. This leads to the endo product as the kinetic major product, even though the exo product is often thermodynamically more stable. In practice, the endo selectivity is pronounced for cyclic dienes such as cyclopentadiene reacting with maleic anhydride, giving the bicyclic adduct with the anhydride bridge oriented endo. For acyclic dienes, steric factors can diminish endo preference.

Substituent effects extend beyond rate acceleration. Electron‑donating groups at the 1‑position of the diene (e.g., alkoxy, alkyl) increase the HOMO coefficient at C‑1, directing the dienophile to bond at that carbon and giving regioisomeric products predictable by the “ortho/para” rule for normal‑electron‑demand Diels‑Alder reactions. Conversely, electron‑withdrawing groups on the diene (inverse‑electron‑demand) reverse the polarity, and the dienophile’s HOMO interacts with the diene’s LUMO. Lewis‑acid catalysis (e.g., AlCl₃, TiCl₄) coordinates to carbonyl dienophiles, further lowering the LUMO and enhancing both rate and endo selectivity.

Stereochemical fidelity is a hallmark: the reaction is suprafacial on both components, so the relative stereochemistry of substituents on the diene and dienophile is preserved in the cyclohexene ring. This makes the Diels‑Alder reaction a powerful tool for constructing up to four contiguous stereocenters in a single step, widely exploited in natural‑product synthesis (e.g., the synthesis of cantharidin, endiandric acids, and the core of taxol).

Worked Mechanism Question
Predict the major product (including stereochemistry) of the Diels‑Alder reaction between (E,E)-2,4-hexadiene and methyl acrylate under thermal conditions. Indicate the regiochemical orientation and whether the product is endo or exo.
The diene is electron‑rich (alkyl substituents) and the dienophile bears an electron‑withdrawing ester. Normal‑electron‑demand rules apply: the larger HOMO coefficient on the diene resides at C‑1 and C‑4; the LUMO coefficient on methyl acrylate is larger at the β‑carbon. Thus the new σ‑bonds form between diene C‑1 and dienophile β‑carbon, and between diene C‑4 and dienophile α‑carbon, giving a para‑relationship between the ester group and the methyl substituent at C‑1 of the diene. The reaction proceeds via the endo transition state because the ester carbonyl can engage in secondary orbital overlap with the diene π‑system. The product is a cyclohexene bearing a methyl at C‑1 (from the diene) and a CO₂Me at C‑4 (from the dienophile) on the same face (endo). The (E,E) geometry of the diene forces the two newly formed substituents at C‑1 and C‑4 to be trans to each other in the ring. Therefore the major product is the endo‑adduct with trans‑relationship between the methyl and ester substituents.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a Diels‑Alder question appears, first identify the electronic nature of diene and dienophile (normal vs inverse demand). Sketch the HOMO/LUMO coefficients to predict regioisomer. Then apply the endo rule: draw the transition state with the dienophile’s π‑system under the diene. Finally, transfer the stereochemistry of the starting materials (cis/trans on the diene, E/Z on the dienophile) to the cyclohexene product. This systematic approach avoids guesswork and earns full marks for both regio‑ and stereochemical description.

Catalytic Hydrogenation: Heterogeneous vs Homogeneous Systems, Catalyst Poisoning, and Selective Reduction

Catalytic hydrogenation adds H₂ across π‑bonds (C=C, C≡C, C=O, N=O) under mild conditions. Heterogeneous catalysts — typically Pd, Pt, Ni, or Ru supported on carbon, alumina, or silica — operate by adsorbing H₂ and the substrate onto metal surfaces. The reaction proceeds via dissociative adsorption of H₂ to give surface hydrides, followed by stepwise hydrogen atom transfer to the adsorbed unsaturated bond. Because the catalyst surface is non‑selective, heterogeneous hydrogenation reduces most reducible functionalities present, including alkenes, alkynes, nitro groups, and benzyl ethers. Reaction rates depend on metal dispersion, support acidity, and hydrogen pressure (typically 1–50 atm).

Homogeneous catalysts, exemplified by Wilkinson’s catalyst (RhCl(PPh₃)₃) and Crabtree’s catalyst ([Ir(cod)(py)(PCy₃)]PF₆), operate in solution via well‑defined organometallic cycles. Oxidative addition of H₂ to a low‑valent metal centre gives a dihydride complex; substrate coordination and migratory insertion deliver the reduced product, followed by reductive elimination to regenerate the catalyst. Ligand electronics and sterics tune selectivity: bulky phosphines hinder approach of hindered alkenes, enabling chemoselective reduction of less hindered double bonds in the presence of more substituted ones. Homogeneous systems also allow asymmetric hydrogenation when chiral ligands (e.g., BINAP, DIOP) are employed, delivering enantiomerically enriched products — a cornerstone of industrial chiral synthesis (e.g., L‑DOPA production via Rh‑BINAP).

Catalyst poisoning is a critical practical concern. Heterogeneous catalysts are deactivated by sulfur‑containing compounds (thiols, sulfides), nitrogen heterocycles (pyridine, quinoline), and heavy metals (lead, mercury) that bind irreversibly to active sites. Even trace amounts (ppm) can shut down a reactor. Homogeneous catalysts are sensitive to oxygen, water, and coordinating anions (Cl⁻, CN⁻) that displace phosphine ligands. In both cases, rigorous substrate purification and inert‑atmosphere techniques are mandatory for reproducible results.

Selective reduction strategies exploit differences in adsorption strength or ligand control. For example, Lindlar’s catalyst (Pd/CaCO₃ poisoned with lead acetate and quinoline) stops alkyne hydrogenation at the cis‑alkene stage. Similarly, Rosenmund‑von Braun reduction uses Pd/BaSO₄ poisoned with sulfur to reduce acid chlorides to aldehydes without over‑reduction to alcohols. In homogeneous catalysis, choice of metal (Rh vs Ir) and ligand bite angle can discriminate between C=C and C=O bonds, enabling chemoselective hydrogenation of α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl compounds to saturated carbonyls (Luche‑type selectivity) or to allylic alcohols.

Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons: Formation, Metabolic Activation, and Environmental Regulation

Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) consist of two or more fused benzene rings (e.g., naphthalene, anthracene, phenanthrene, benzo[a]pyrene). They are formed during incomplete combustion of organic matter — fossil‑fuel burning, vehicle exhaust, cigarette smoke, and industrial processes such as coking and aluminium smelting. Thermodynamically, larger PAHs are more stable per carbon atom, so high‑temperature pyrolysis favors growth of peri‑condensed systems. In the atmosphere, PAHs partition between gas and particulate phases depending on vapour pressure; the heavier congeners (five rings and above) are predominantly particle‑bound.

Biological activation of PAHs occurs primarily via cytochrome P450‑mediated oxidation to arene oxides (epoxides), which can rearrange to phenols or be hydrolysed by epoxide hydrolase to trans‑dihydrodiols. A second oxidation yields diol‑epoxides — the ultimate carcinogenic metabolites. For benzo[a]pyrene, the bay‑region diol‑epoxide (BPDE) forms covalent adducts with the N² of guanine in DNA, causing G→T transversions. This metabolic pathway is the basis for the “bay‑region theory” of PAH carcinogenicity, which predicts that angularly fused rings with a sterically hindered bay region generate the most reactive diol‑epoxides.

Environmental regulation targets both emission sources and ambient concentrations. The US EPA lists 16 priority PAHs for monitoring; the EU Air Quality Directive sets a target value of 1 ng m⁻³ for benzo[a]pyrene as a marker for total PAH exposure. Occupational limits (e.g., OSHA PEL for coal‑tar pitch volatiles) are expressed as benzene‑soluble fraction. Remediation technologies include activated‑carbon adsorption, advanced oxidation (ozone, UV/H₂O₂), and bioremediation using white‑rot fungi (Phanerochaete chrysosporium) that secrete lignin peroxidase and manganese peroxidase capable of mineralising high‑molecular‑weight PAHs.

Analytical determination relies on gas chromatography‑mass spectrometry (GC‑MS) with selected‑ion monitoring for sensitivity down to sub‑pg levels. Isotopically labelled internal standards correct for matrix effects. Recent advances employ two‑dimensional GC (GC×GC) coupled to time‑of‑flight MS to resolve co‑eluting isomers (e.g., benzo[a]pyrene vs benzo[e]pyrene). Accurate quantification underpins risk assessment models that estimate lifetime cancer risk from inhalation and dietary exposure (e.g., grilled meat, smoked fish).

Advanced Cracking Technologies: Hydrocracking, Steam Cracking, and Catalytic Dewaxing

Hydrocracking combines catalytic cracking with hydrogenation in a single reactor, using bifunctional catalysts (acidic zeolite or amorphous silica‑alumina plus noble‑metal hydrogenation component such as Ni‑Mo or Ni‑W sulfides). Operating at 350–450 °C and 80–200 bar H₂, hydrocracking converts heavy vacuum gas oil into high‑quality diesel, jet fuel, and naphtha. The hydrogen pressure suppresses coke formation, saturates olefins and aromatics, and enables deep desulfurisation and denitrogenation. Product distribution is controlled by catalyst acidity (zeolite pore size), metal function, and hydrogen partial pressure; higher acidity favors cracking to lighter products, while higher hydrogen pressure drives saturation.

Steam cracking (pyrolysis) is the primary route to light olefins (ethylene, propylene) and aromatics (benzene, toluene, xylenes). Feedstocks range from ethane to naphtha and gas oil. In tubular reactors at 750–900 °C and very short residence times (0.1–0.5 s), hydrocarbon radicals undergo β‑scission, producing a complex mixture. The severity (temperature × residence time) determines the ethylene/propylene ratio: higher severity favors ethylene. Quenching in transfer‑line exchangers stops reactions and recovers heat for steam generation. Coke deposition on tube walls limits run length; periodic decoking with steam/air restores activity.

Catalytic dewaxing removes n‑paraffins that cause high pour points in lubricating oil base stocks. Shape‑selective zeolites (ZSM‑5, USY, or SAPO‑11) crack linear alkanes preferentially because their pore dimensions exclude branched isomers. The reaction is carried out at 300–350 °C under 20–50 bar H₂ with a bifunctional catalyst (Pt or Ni on the zeolite). The hydrogenation function saturates olefinic intermediates, preventing oligomerisation and coke. Modern dewaxing units integrate hydrofinishing to improve color and stability of the final base oil.

Process integration is key: refineries often couple hydrocracking with fluid catalytic cracking (FCC) to balance gasoline and diesel demand, while steam crackers supply olefins to polymer plants. Emerging technologies include catalytic pyrolysis of plastic waste over zeolites to produce BTX and olefins, and oxidative coupling of methane (OCM) to ethylene, though selectivity and catalyst stability remain challenges. Understanding the interplay of thermodynamics (equilibrium conversion), kinetics (radical vs carbocation pathways), and catalyst architecture enables engineers to optimise product slates for changing market specifications.

  • Assuming hydroboration‑oxidation gives Markovnikov alcohols; it gives anti‑Markovnikov products with syn stereochemistry.
  • Confusing endo and exo selectivity in Diels‑Alder; the endo product is kinetic, not necessarily thermodynamic.
  • Believing heterogeneous hydrogenation is always unselective; poisoned catalysts (Lindlar, Rosenmund) provide defined selectivity.
  • Overlooking that PAH carcinogenicity requires metabolic activation to diol‑epoxides; the parent PAH is not directly mutagenic.
  • Thinking steam cracking and hydrocracking operate under similar conditions; steam cracking uses high temperature, low pressure, no catalyst, while hydrocracking uses moderate temperature, high H₂ pressure, and bifunctional catalysts.
  • Neglecting catalyst poisoning by sulfur and nitrogen compounds in both heterogeneous and homogeneous hydrogenation; even ppm levels can deactivate the catalyst.
  • Assuming all zeolites crack n‑paraffins equally; pore size and topology (e.g., ZSM‑5 vs USY) dictate shape selectivity and product distribution.

Catalytic Dehydrogenation of Light Alkanes to Olefins

Industrial production of ethylene and propylene increasingly relies on catalytic dehydrogenation of ethane and propane as a complement to steam cracking. The reaction is equilibrium‑limited and endothermic (ΔH° ≈ +124 kJ mol⁻¹ for ethane → ethylene + H₂), so high temperature (550–650 °C) and low hydrocarbon partial pressure are required to drive conversion. Commercial processes such as the CATOFIN (UOP) and Oleflex (UOP) systems employ a chromia‑alumina (Cr₂O₃/Al₂O₃) or platinum‑tin (Pt‑Sn/Al₂O₃) catalyst in a moving‑bed or radial‑flow reactor. The Pt‑Sn formulation suppresses deep dehydrogenation to coke and provides higher selectivity (>90 % at 600 °C) while operating at lower pressure (1–2 bar) than the chromia system. Catalyst deactivation occurs mainly by coke deposition; regeneration is achieved by controlled oxidative burn‑off (air at 500–550 °C) in a separate regenerator vessel, allowing continuous operation. Thermodynamic analysis shows that at 600 °C the equilibrium conversion of ethane is only ~55 % at 1 bar, so industrial units recycle unreacted ethane to achieve overall yields above 95 %. Process integration with a downstream hydrogen‑recovery unit (pressure‑swing adsorption) supplies high‑purity H₂ for hydrotreating elsewhere in the refinery. Recent advances include the development of gallium‑modified zeolite catalysts (Ga‑ZSM‑5) that operate at 500 °C with improved propylene selectivity from propane, and the exploration of oxidative dehydrogenation (ODH) using CO₂ as a soft oxidant to shift equilibrium while generating syngas.

Key Design Variables

  • Reactor inlet temperature and temperature profile (isothermal vs adiabatic).
  • Hydrocarbon partial pressure (dilution with steam or recycled H₂).
  • Catalyst metal loading, promoter (Sn, In, Ga) and support acidity.
  • Regeneration cycle time and coke burn‑off temperature to avoid sintering.

Understanding the interplay of kinetics (first‑order in alkane, zero‑order in H₂ at low conversion) and equilibrium constraints enables the examiner to test calculation of required recycle ratio for a target ethylene yield.

Olefin Metathesis: Mechanism, Catalyst Generations, and Industrial Deployment

Olefin metathesis redistributes carbon–carbon double bonds via a [2+2] cycloaddition between a metal alkylidene and an olefin to give a metallacyclobutane intermediate, which then fragments to a new alkylidene and a new olefin. The Chauvin mechanism, confirmed by isotopic labeling, underpins all modern catalysts. First‑generation catalysts (e.g., Schrock’s Mo(=CH‑t‑Bu)(NAr)(OR)₂) are highly active but air‑ and moisture‑sensitive. Second‑generation Grubbs ruthenium complexes (RuCl₂(=CHPh)(PCy₃)₂) tolerate functional groups and can be handled in air, making them the workhorse for laboratory synthesis. Third‑generation Hoveyda‑Grubbs catalysts replace one phosphine with a chelating N‑heterocyclic carbene (NHC) and an isopropoxybenzylidene ligand, giving faster initiation and higher thermal stability (up to 80 °C). Industrial applications include the Shell Higher Olefins Process (SHOP) where ethylene is oligomerized to α‑olefins, followed by metathesis to produce a tailored distribution of C₁₀–C₁₄ internal olefins for detergent alcohols. Another large‑scale use is the production of 1,4‑butadiene from 1‑butene and ethylene (the “butadiene metathesis” route). Catalyst lifetime is limited by decomposition pathways such as β‑hydride elimination from the alkylidene and formation of ruthenium hydrides; addition of phosphine scavengers (e.g., CuCl) or use of more robust NHC ligands extends turnover numbers to >10⁶. The examiner often asks to predict the product distribution from a cross‑metathesis of a terminal olefin with an internal olefin, requiring knowledge of the “rule of thumb” that terminal olefins react faster and that the reaction is under thermodynamic control for internal olefins.

Catalyst Selection Guide

Typical Metathesis Catalysts and Operating Windows
CatalystMetalTypical Temp (°C)Functional‑Group ToleranceTypical TON
Schrock MoMo0–25Low10⁴–10⁵
Grubbs 1st GenRu25–40Moderate10³–10⁴
Grubbs 2nd GenRu40–60High10⁴–10⁵
Hoveyda‑Grubbs 2nd GenRu60–80Very High10⁵–10⁶
Worked Example: Cross‑Metathesis Product Prediction
Predict the major organic products when 1‑hexene (CH₂=CH‑(CH₂)₃CH₃) is subjected to cross‑metathesis with cis‑2‑butene (CH₃CH=CHCH₃) using a second‑generation Grubbs catalyst at 40 °C. Assume equimolar feed and that the reaction reaches thermodynamic equilibrium.
The possible metallacyclobutane intermediates lead to four olefin products: (1) ethylene (CH₂=CH₂) from the two terminal CH₂ groups, (2) 1‑hexene (starting material), (3) cis‑2‑butene (starting material), and (4) 3‑octene (CH₃(CH₂)₃CH=CH(CH₂)₃CH₃) from coupling of the internal fragments. At equilibrium, the more substituted internal olefin (3‑octene) is thermodynamically favored over the terminal olefins. Ethylene is volatile and removed from the reaction zone, driving the equilibrium toward 3‑octene. Therefore the major isolated product is 3‑octene (predominantly the trans isomer due to thermodynamic control), with minor amounts of unreacted 1‑hexene and cis‑2‑butene. Ethylene is not isolated as a liquid product.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a metathesis question appears, first identify the catalyst generation from the description (air‑stable → Ru‑NHC). Then write the Chauvin cycle quickly to show mechanistic awareness. For product prediction, list all possible pairwise combinations, then apply the “terminal olefin reacts faster, internal olefin is thermodynamic sink” rule. If ethylene is a possible product, note its removal shifts equilibrium.

C–H Activation by Transition‑Metal Complexes: Direct Functionalization of Saturated Hydrocarbons

Direct conversion of inert C–H bonds in alkanes to C–X (X = halogen, O, N, C) bonds avoids pre‑functionalization steps and is a central goal of modern hydrocarbon chemistry. Two mechanistic paradigms dominate: (1) oxidative addition into a low‑valent d⁸ or d¹⁰ metal center (e.g., Ir(I), Rh(I), Pt(0)) forming a metal‑alkyl‑hydride, and (2) σ‑bond metathesis or concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) with high‑valent d⁰ metals (e.g., Ti(IV), Zr(IV), Ta(V)) or with late‑transition‑metal catalysts bearing a basic ligand (e.g., Pd(OAc)₂ with acetate). The Shilov system (Pt(II)/Pt(IV) in aqueous H₂O/Cl⁻) historically demonstrated catalytic methane oxidation to methanol via Pt(II)‑mediated C–H activation followed by oxidation to Pt(IV)‑methyl and hydrolysis. Contemporary systems achieve higher turnover by using strong oxidants (e.g., H₂O₂, PhI(OAc)₂) or electrochemical oxidation to regenerate the high‑valent metal. For example, the Periana‑Catalytica catalyst (Pt(II)‑bpym in concentrated H₂SO₄) converts methane to methyl bisulfate at 200 °C with >80 % selectivity, but the harsh acid medium limits practicality. More recent approaches employ earth‑abundant metals: Fe‑ and Cu‑based catalysts with N‑donor ligands (e.g., Fe(PDP) where PDP = N,N′‑bis(2‑pyridylmethyl)‑1,2‑diaminoethane) perform selective hydroxylation of secondary C–H bonds in alkanes using H₂O₂ via a high‑spin Fe(IV)=O intermediate. Regioselectivity is governed by a combination of bond‑dissociation energy (BDE) and steric accessibility; tertiary C–H (BDE ≈ 96 kcal mol⁻¹) reacts faster than secondary (≈ 98 kcal mol⁻¹) and primary (≈ 101 kcal mol⁻¹). However, catalyst design can invert this trend by enforcing a sterically constrained pocket that favors less hindered primary C–H bonds (e.g., Mn(salen) catalysts in the presence of a co‑oxidant). The examiner may ask to rationalize the observed selectivity in a given substrate using BDE tables and to propose a catalytic cycle showing the oxidation state changes of the metal.

Representative Catalytic Cycle (Pd(II)/Pd(IV) CMD)

  1. Pd(OAc)₂ coordinates to a directing group (e.g., pyridine) on the substrate.
  2. Concerted metalation‑deprotonation: acetate abstracts the proton while Pd forms a Pd–C bond (Pd(II)‑aryl/alkyl).
  3. Oxidation by an external oxidant (PhI(OAc)₂) to Pd(IV)‑aryl/alkyl‑diacetate.
  4. Reductive elimination forms the C–O (or C–N, C–X) bond and regenerates Pd(II).

Key parameters influencing turnover frequency (TOF) are the pKa of the conjugate acid of the base (acetate vs pivalate), the electronic nature of the directing group, and the oxidant reduction potential.

Biocatalytic and Photocatalytic Routes to Hydrocarbon Oxidation

Enzymatic and light‑driven methods provide complementary selectivity to traditional thermochemical routes. Cytochrome P450 monooxygenases (CYPs) catalyze the insertion of one oxygen atom from O₂ into a C–H bond, using NADPH as the reductant. Engineered P450 variants (e.g., P450‑BM3 mutants) accept non‑native substrates such as n‑alkanes up to C₁₂, delivering terminal alcohols with >90 % regioselectivity. Whole‑cell biocatalysis in recombinant E. coli co‑expressing a reductase domain enables preparative‑scale hydroxylation of gaseous alkanes (propane, butane) in a two‑phase aqueous/organic system. Limitations include cofactor regeneration cost and enzyme stability at >40 °C; immobilization on silica or polymer supports improves operational lifetime. Photocatalytic oxidation uses semiconductor photocatalysts (TiO₂, g‑C₃N₄, WO₃) or molecular photosensitizers (Ru(bpy)₃²⁺, Ir(ppy)₃) under visible light to generate reactive oxygen species (·OH, ¹O₂, O₂·⁻) that abstract hydrogen from alkanes. For example, TiO₂‑supported Pt nanoparticles under UV‑vis irradiation convert methane to methanol and formaldehyde in the gas phase with quantum yields of 0.5 % at 300 °C. Recent advances couple a photosensitizer with a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) catalyst (e.g., a quinuclidine‑based N‑oxyl radical) to achieve selective functionalization of weak C–H bonds (BDE < 90 kcal mol⁻¹) at room temperature. The examiner may test the ability to compare the selectivity patterns: enzymatic oxidation favors terminal positions due to active‑site geometry, whereas radical HAT photocatalysis prefers weaker tertiary C–H bonds. Knowledge of the Marcus‑type electron‑transfer steps in the photocatalytic cycle (photoexcitation → oxidative quenching → HAT → product formation) is also fair game.

Comparison Table

Biocatalytic vs Photocatalytic Alkane Oxidation

Typical oxidantO₂ / NADPHO₂ (air) + photosensitizer
RegioselectivityTerminal > internal (active‑site control)Weakest C–H (BDE‑driven)

Refinery Process Integration: Feedstock Flexibility, Product Slate Optimization, and Carbon Intensity Metrics

Modern refineries must adjust the product slate (gasoline, diesel, jet fuel, petrochemicals) in response to market demand while meeting carbon‑intensity targets. Feedstock flexibility is achieved by configuring the crude distillation unit (CDU) to handle a range of API gravities (15–45°) and sulfur contents (0.1–3 wt %). Downstream, the fluid catalytic cracking (FCC) unit can be shifted between “gasoline mode” (high riser temperature, low catalyst‑to‑oil ratio) and “propylene mode” (lower temperature, ZSM‑5 additive) to vary the olefin yield. Hydrocracking units equipped with bifunctional catalysts (USY zeolite + Ni‑Mo sulfide) can be tuned by hydrogen partial pressure (80–180 bar) and space velocity to produce either high‑quality diesel (high H₂ pressure, low severity) or naphtha for steam cracking (lower H₂ pressure, higher severity). Catalytic reforming provides high‑octane aromatics; its severity (reactor inlet temperature 480–525 °C) controls the benzene‑toluene‑xylene (BTX) yield, which feeds the petrochemical complex. Process integration tools such as linear programming (LP) models incorporate unit yields, utility consumption, and CO₂ emission factors (kg CO₂ per bbl) to generate an optimal operating plan. Carbon intensity metrics now include Scope 1 (direct combustion), Scope 2 (imported electricity), and Scope 3 (product combustion) emissions. Refineries adopting carbon capture on the FCC regenerator flue gas (amine scrubbing) can reduce net intensity by 15–20 %. Emerging “green hydrogen” from electrolysis powered by renewables can replace steam‑methane reformer hydrogen in hydrotreating, further lowering Scope 1 emissions. The examiner may present a simplified LP tableau and ask to identify the binding constraint (e.g., hydrogen balance, FCC catalyst circulation) when the diesel demand increases by 10 %.

Typical LP Constraints

  • Crude availability and quality limits (API, sulfur).
  • Unit capacity limits (CDU, FCC, hydrocracker, reformer).
  • Hydrogen network balance (production from reformer, consumption in hydrotreating/hydrocracking).
  • Product specification constraints (RVP, sulfur, aromatics, cetane number).
  • CO₂ emission cap (regulatory or voluntary).

Understanding how a change in one constraint propagates through the hydrogen network and product yields is essential for advanced refinery‑economics questions.

  • Assuming dehydrogenation equilibrium conversion equals single‑pass conversion; recycle ratio must be calculated from equilibrium data.
  • Confusing the Chauvin metallacyclobutane with a simple π‑complex; the four‑membered ring is the key intermediate.
  • Neglecting that oxidative addition C–H activation requires a low‑valent metal center, whereas CMD proceeds via a high‑valent metal‑base pair.
  • Believing enzymatic oxidation always gives terminal alcohols; engineered P450s can be reprogrammed for sub‑terminal selectivity.
  • Overlooking that photocatalytic HAT selectivity follows bond‑dissociation energy, not steric hindrance alone.
  • Treating refinery LP models as static; the hydrogen balance is a dynamic coupling constraint that shifts with feedstock changes.
  • Ignoring the effect of CO₂ capture energy penalty on overall carbon intensity; the net reduction is often lower than the capture rate.

These traps highlight the need to integrate mechanistic insight with process‑level thinking when tackling advanced hydrocarbon chemistry problems.

Asymmetric Hydrogenation of Alkenes: Chiral Catalysts and Enantioselectivity

Asymmetric hydrogenation converts prochiral alkenes into single enantiomers using chiral transition‑metal complexes. The most widely deployed catalysts are Rh(I) and Ru(II) complexes bearing chelating diphosphine ligands such as (R,R)-Me‑DUPHOS, (S)-BINAP, or (R,R)-Ph‑PEPPSI. The catalytic cycle proceeds through oxidative addition of H₂ to the metal centre, alkene coordination to give a π‑complex, migratory insertion to form a metal‑alkyl intermediate, and reductive elimination to release the chiral alkane. Enantioselectivity is dictated in the alkene‑binding step: the chiral pocket of the diphosphine forces the substrate to approach from one face, leading to a diastereomeric transition state whose energy difference (ΔΔG‡) typically ranges from 2–5 kJ mol⁻¹, delivering enantiomeric excesses (ee) > 95 % for many dehydroamino‑acid derivatives.

Industrial relevance is illustrated by the synthesis of (S)-metolachlor, a herbicide produced at > 10 kt yr⁻¹ using a Ir‑(S)-Xyliphos catalyst that achieves 79 % ee at 50 bar H₂ and 80 °C. Ligand design principles emphasize bite angle (≈ 90–95° for Rh, ≈ 100° for Ru), electronic donation (electron‑rich phosphines accelerate oxidative addition), and steric bulk that creates a well‑defined chiral quadrant. Catalyst poisoning by sulfur‑containing impurities is mitigated by pre‑treating feedstocks with guard beds of activated copper. Homogeneous systems dominate fine‑chemical production, whereas heterogeneous analogues (e.g., cinchona‑modified Pt/Al₂O₃) are employed for large‑scale hydrogenation of β‑ketoesters, delivering ee up to 98 % but with lower turnover frequencies.

Key Ligand Families and Typical Performance

(S)-BINAPRu(II)β‑ketoesters95–9850–100 bar H₂, 80 °C, i‑PrOH
(R,R)-Ph‑PEPPSIIr(I)Unfunctionalised olefins90–9610–30 bar H₂, 50 °C, CH₂Cl₂
(S)-XyliphosIr(I)Imine precursors (metolachlor)7950 bar H₂, 80 °C, toluene

Mechanistic probes such as kinetic isotope effect (KIE) measurements (k_H/k_D ≈ 1.5–2.0) confirm that H₂ oxidative addition is not rate‑determining; instead, alkene insertion or reductive elimination often limits turnover. In situ NMR and IR spectroscopy have identified dihydride intermediates (e.g., Rh(H)₂(diphosphine)⁺) that precede substrate binding. Understanding these details enables rational ligand modification to improve activity for sterically hindered tetrasubstituted alkenes, a current research frontier.

Carbene and Nitrene Insertion into C–H Bonds: Mechanistic Pathways and Synthetic Scope

Transition‑metal‑catalysed C–H functionalisation via carbene (M=CR₂) or nitrene (M=NR) intermediates provides direct access to C–C and C–N bonds without pre‑functionalisation. Rhodium(II) carboxylates (e.g., Rh₂(OAc)₄) and copper(I) complexes (Cu(MeCN)₄PF₆) are the workhorse catalysts for carbene transfer from diazo compounds (R₂C=N₂). The accepted mechanism involves loss of N₂ to generate a metal‑carbene, which undergoes a concerted, asynchronous insertion into a C–H bond via a three‑center transition state. Selectivity is governed by a combination of bond‑dissociation energy (BDE), steric accessibility, and electronic effects: tertiary > secondary > primary C–H bonds are favoured, but electron‑rich C–H bonds (e.g., benzylic, allylic) react faster than predicted by BDE alone.

Nitrene insertion follows a parallel pathway. Imioiodinanes (PhI=NR) or azides (RN₃) serve as nitrene precursors with Rh₂(esp)₂, Cu(I), or Fe(II) porphyrin catalysts. The metal‑nitrene intermediate can be described as a metal‑imido species (M=NR) that inserts into C–H bonds via a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) / radical‑rebound sequence or a concerted insertion, depending on the metal and ligand set. Iron‑porphyrin catalysts, for example, operate through a high‑valent Fe(IV)=NR species that abstracts a hydrogen atom to give a carbon‑centred radical, which then recombines (rebound) to form the C–N bond. This radical pathway leads to loss of stereochemical configuration at the reacting carbon, whereas concerted Rh‑carbene insertion retains configuration.

Synthetic Applications and Limitations

Intramolecular C–H amination of sulfamate esters (the “Huang‑Huang” reaction) furnishes cyclic sulfamidates in 70–90 % yield with high diastereoselectivity, enabling rapid construction of nitrogen‑containing heterocycles. Intermolecular variants remain challenging because of competing catalyst decomposition (e.g., formation of μ‑oxo dimers) and the need for large excess of the hydrocarbon substrate to suppress side reactions such as dimerisation of the carbene to give alkenes. Recent advances employ dirhodium catalysts with electron‑withdrawing carboxylate ligands (e.g., Rh₂(esp)₂) that accelerate carbene formation and improve turnover numbers (TON > 10⁴) for unactivated alkanes. Photoredox‑mediated generation of metal‑carbenes from diazo compounds under visible light (Ir(ppy)₃, 450 nm) has expanded the substrate scope to include sensitive functional groups that would be reduced by thermal Rh(II) catalysts.

Key mechanistic diagnostics include: (i) KIE values (k_H/k_D ≈ 2–3 for concerted insertion, ≈ 5–7 for HAT/rebound), (ii) radical‑clock substrates (cyclopropyl‑containing alkanes) that give ring‑opened products only under HAT pathways, and (iii) stereochemical probes (cis‑/trans‑decalin) that reveal retention vs. inversion. These tools allow chemists to assign the operative mechanism and tailor catalyst/ligand combinations for a desired selectivity profile.

Mechanistic Discrimination via Kinetic Isotope Effect
A Rh₂(OAc)₄‑catalysed carbene insertion into cyclohexane using ethyl diazoacetate gives a KIE (k_H/k_D) of 2.1. When the same reaction is run with a Cu(I)‑bis(oxazoline) catalyst, the observed KIE is 6.8. Explain the mechanistic implication of each value and predict the stereochemical outcome at a chiral secondary carbon for both catalysts.
A KIE of ~2 indicates a concerted, asynchronous three‑center insertion where C–H bond cleavage and C–C bond formation occur in a single transition state; the reaction proceeds with retention of configuration at the reacting carbon. A KIE of ~7 is characteristic of a stepwise hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) followed by radical rebound; the carbon‑centred radical intermediate loses stereochemical integrity, leading to racemisation at a chiral secondary centre. Therefore, the Rh(II) system gives retention, while the Cu(I) system gives racemic product.
Exam‑Day Strategy for C–H Functionalisation Questions
When a question asks to differentiate between concerted carbene insertion and HAT/rebound nitrene pathways, first write the expected KIE range for each mechanism (2–3 vs. 5–7). Then note the stereochemical consequence (retention vs. racemisation). Finally, mention a radical‑clock probe (e.g., cyclopropyl‑methane) that would give ring‑opened products only under HAT. This three‑point answer covers mechanistic, kinetic, and stereochemical evidence and scores full marks.

Supramolecular Approaches to Hydrocarbon Separation: Molecular Sieves and Host‑Guest Chemistry

Separation of close‑boiling hydrocarbons (e.g., xylenes, C₈ aromatics; linear vs. branched alkanes) is a major energy consumer in refineries. Supramolecular materials — zeolites, metal‑organic frameworks (MOFs), and covalent organic frameworks (COFs) — exploit size‑exclusion, shape‑selectivity, and specific host‑guest interactions to achieve separations at near‑ambient temperatures. Zeolite Beta and ZSM‑5, with 10‑membered ring pores (≈ 5.5 Å), preferentially adsorb linear alkanes over branched isomers, enabling the “Molex” process for n‑paraffin enrichment. In contrast, the larger‑pore zeolite Y (12‑membered rings, 7.4 Å) combined with Ba²⁺ exchange selectively adsorbs p‑xylene from the C₈ aromatic mixture via π‑complexation with the cation, delivering > 99 % purity in the Parex process.

MOFs such as Cu‑BTC (HKUST‑1) and Fe‑MIL‑101 exhibit tunable pore apertures (6–30 Å) and open metal sites that bind unsaturated hydrocarbons (ethylene, propylene) more strongly than saturated analogues, facilitating olefin/paraffin separation. The adsorption enthalpy difference (ΔH_ads) for ethylene vs. ethane on Cu‑BTC is ≈ 15 kJ mol⁻¹, giving a selectivity (S = (q_C₂H₄/q_C₂H₆)/(p_C₂H₄/p_C₂H₆)) of 5–10 at 298 K. COFs with imine‑linked pores functionalised with –CF₃ groups show enhanced affinity for fluorinated hydrocarbons, a niche but growing separation need in specialty chemical production.

Process Integration and Energy Metrics

Simulated moving‑bed (SMB) chromatography using these adsorbents reduces energy consumption by 60–80 % relative to conventional distillation. A typical SMB unit for p‑xylene operates at 120 °C and 5 bar with a desorbent (e.g., di‑isopropylbenzene) that is recovered by flash distillation. The overall specific energy demand drops from ~ 2.5 MJ kg⁻¹ (distillation) to ~ 0.5 MJ kg⁻¹ (SMB). Key design variables include the adsorbent’s working capacity (Δq = q_ads – q_des), the selectivity factor (α), and the mass‑transfer zone length, which must be shorter than the column length to avoid breakthrough. Recent pilot plants demonstrate continuous operation for > 5000 h with < 5 % loss in adsorbent capacity, confirming commercial viability.

Kinetic Isotope Effects in Hydrocarbon Reactivity: Primary and Secondary KIEs and Mechanistic Diagnosis

Kinetic isotope effects (KIEs) quantify the change in reaction rate upon isotopic substitution (H → D or T) and serve as powerful mechanistic probes for C–H bond cleavage. A primary KIE (k_H/k_D) arises when the isotopically labelled bond is broken in the rate‑determining step (RDS). For a typical C–H bond cleavage via a linear transition state, the semi‑classical maximum at 298 K is ≈ 7–8, reflecting zero‑point‑energy (ZPE) differences. Observed values of 2–4 indicate a “late” transition state with significant C–H bond stretching but not full cleavage; values > 5 suggest a “early” transition state or tunnelling contributions.

Secondary KIEs (α‑ or β‑deuterium) report on hybridisation changes at the carbon centre without bond rupture. An α‑secondary KIE (k_H/k_D ≈ 1.1–1.2) is normal for sp³ → sp² rehybridisation (e.g., carbocation formation), whereas an inverse α‑KIE (≈ 0.9) accompanies sp² → sp³ changes (e.g., nucleophilic addition to a carbonyl). β‑Secondary KIEs are smaller (≈ 1.02) and reflect hyperconjugative stabilisation in the transition state. In free‑radical halogenation of alkanes, the primary KIE for H‑abstraction by Cl· is ≈ 1.4 (early TS), while for Br· it is ≈ 5–6 (late TS), consistent with the Hammond postulate and the exothermic/endothermic nature of the respective steps.

Experimental Design and Data Interpretation

Competitive KIE measurements (intramolecular) eliminate concentration errors: a substrate containing both C–H and C–D bonds reacts in a single flask, and the product isotope ratio is analysed by GC‑MS or ²H NMR. For intermolecular KIEs, parallel reactions with isotopically pure substrates are run under identical conditions; careful matching of conversion (< 20 %) ensures the measured rate ratio reflects the initial rate. Temperature‑dependent KIEs (Arrhenius plots) allow extraction of ΔE_a and ΔA (pre‑exponential factor ratio). A curved Arrhenius plot signals quantum tunnelling, which is significant for light‑atom transfer at low temperatures (e.g., H‑transfer in enzyme active sites). In hydrocarbon oxidation by cytochrome P450, the observed KIE of 10–12 at 25 °C drops to ~ 3 at 60 °C, a hallmark of tunnelling‑enhanced H‑abstraction.

Common pitfalls include neglecting the equilibrium isotope effect (EIE) on pre‑equilibrium steps, which can mask the true primary KIE, and assuming that a large KIE automatically proves C–H cleavage in the RDS — a large KIE can also arise from a pre‑equilibrium that involves C–H bond breaking (e.g., deprotonation to form a metal‑alkyl). Rigorous mechanistic assignment therefore combines KIE data with stereochemical probes, radical clocks, and computational modelling of the transition state.

Process Intensification in Olefin Production: Reactive Distillation and Membrane Reactors

Steam cracking of naphtha or ethane remains the dominant route to ethylene and propylene, but its high energy demand (≈ 30–35 GJ t⁻¹ ethylene) and CO₂ footprint drive interest in intensified alternatives. Reactive distillation (RD) integrates reaction and separation in a single column, exploiting the volatility difference between reactants and products to shift equilibrium‑limited reactions (e.g., dehydrogenation of ethylbenzene to styrene, or metathesis of 2‑butene to propylene + ethylene) toward higher conversion. In an RD column for ethylbenzene dehydrogenation, the catalyst (Fe₂O₃‑K₂O) is packed in the reactive zone; styrene, being more volatile, exits the top, while unreacted ethylbenzene and hydrogen are recycled. This configuration achieves > 95 % conversion at 600 °C and 1.5 bar, compared with 60–65 % in a conventional adiabatic reactor, and reduces steam consumption by ~ 30 %.

Membrane reactors (MR) couple a catalytic zone with a selective membrane that continuously removes a product (typically H₂) to drive equilibrium‑limited dehydrogenation (propane → propylene + H₂, isobutane → isobutylene + H₂). Dense metallic membranes (Pd‑Ag alloys, 5–10 µm thick) exhibit H₂ permeance of 10⁻³ mol m⁻² s⁻¹ Pa⁻⁰·⁵ at 500 °C with infinite selectivity over hydrocarbons. By sweeping H₂ on the permeate side with steam, the partial pressure of H₂ in the reaction zone is kept < 0.05 bar, pushing propane conversion from the thermodynamic limit of 25 % (at 600 °C, 1 bar) to > 55 % in a single pass. Catalyst deactivation by coking is mitigated because lower H₂ partial pressure reduces hydrogenolysis of surface carbon; however, membrane fouling by carbon deposits remains a durability concern, addressed by periodic oxidative regeneration cycles.

Comparative Performance Metrics

Integration of RD or MR with upstream separation (e.g., dividing wall columns for feed purification) and downstream product recovery (cryogenic distillation for ethylene/propylene) yields a holistic flowsheet where heat from exothermic membrane permeation or reactive distillation reboiler duties is recovered via heat‑exchanger networks. Process‑wide pinch analysis typically identifies a 15–20 % reduction in total utility consumption relative to the standalone units. These intensified configurations are now being evaluated in pilot‑scale demonstrations (e.g., 10 kt yr⁻¹ propylene MR at a European petrochemical site) and are expected to enter commercial licensing within the next decade.

  • Confusing the concerted carbene insertion KIE (~2) with the HAT/rebound nitrene KIE (~6) and misassigning stereochemical outcome.
  • Assuming all MOFs give higher olefin/paraffin selectivity than zeolites; selectivity depends on open‑metal‑site density and pore aperture matching.
  • Neglecting the equilibrium isotope effect on pre‑equilibrium steps when interpreting primary KIEs in catalytic cycles.
  • Treating reactive distillation as a simple combination of reactor and column without accounting for catalyst deactivation in the reactive zone.
  • Overlooking membrane fouling kinetics in membrane reactor design; long‑term permeance decay can erode conversion gains.
  • Believing that a large primary KIE alone proves C–H cleavage in the rate‑determining step; a pre‑equilibrium involving C–H bond breaking can produce the same signature.
  • These traps highlight the need for integrated mechanistic and process‑level thinking when tackling advanced hydrocarbon chemistry problems.

Radical Chain Autoxidation of Alkanes: Initiation, Propagation, and Inhibition

Autoxidation of saturated hydrocarbons proceeds through a classic radical chain mechanism that is central to polymer degradation, fuel stability, and atmospheric chemistry. The sequence begins with initiation, where a weak C–H bond (bond dissociation energy ≈ 410 kJ mol⁻¹ for a secondary carbon) is homolytically cleaved by heat, UV radiation, or trace metal impurities, generating an alkyl radical (R·). In the propagation steps, R· rapidly adds O₂ (diffusion‑controlled, k ≈ 10⁹ M⁻¹ s⁻¹) to give a peroxyl radical (ROO·). This peroxyl radical abstracts a hydrogen atom from a neighboring alkane (R′H) to form a hydroperoxide (ROOH) and a new alkyl radical, sustaining the chain. The overall propagation cycle is:

R· + O₂ → ROO·
ROO· + R′H → ROOH + R′·

Hydroperoxides are unstable; they undergo decomposition (thermal or metal‑catalysed) to alkoxy (RO·) and hydroxyl (·OH) radicals, which further abstract hydrogen or undergo β‑scission, yielding carbonyl compounds, alcohols, and shorter‑chain fragments. The chain length can exceed 10⁴ cycles before termination, which occurs by radical–radical recombination (ROO· + ROO· → non‑radical products) or disproportionation.

Inhibition is achieved by phenolic antioxidants (e.g., BHT, BHA) that donate a hydrogen atom to peroxyl radicals, forming a resonance‑stabilised phenoxyl radical that does not propagate the chain. The rate constant for hydrogen transfer from a hindered phenol to ROO· is typically 10⁴–10⁵ M⁻¹ s⁻¹, sufficient to outcompete propagation when the inhibitor concentration is in the 0.01–0.1 wt % range. Synergistic mixtures (phenol + phosphite) are common in commercial lubricants because phosphites decompose hydroperoxides non‑radically, lowering the steady‑state ROOH concentration.

Kinetic modelling of autoxidation uses the steady‑state approximation for radical concentrations, yielding an overall rate law: rate = kₚ[RH]√(kᵢ[Initiator]/kₜ), where kₚ is the propagation rate constant, kᵢ the initiation rate constant, and kₜ the termination rate constant. This square‑root dependence on initiator concentration is a diagnostic hallmark of radical chain processes and is routinely verified in accelerated oxidation tests (e.g., Rancimat, PDSC).

Industrial relevance

In fuel storage, autoxidation leads to gum and sediment formation; antioxidant packages are mandated in jet fuel (e.g., 24 mg L⁻¹ BHT). In polymer processing, melt‑phase oxidation of polyethylene is suppressed by hindered amine light stabilisers (HALS) that operate via a Denisov cycle, regenerating the nitroxyl radical. Understanding the balance between initiation (metal contaminants, temperature) and inhibition (antioxidant depletion) enables predictive lifetime models for hydrocarbon‑based materials.

Catalytic Alkane Metathesis: Mechanism, Catalyst Design, and Process Integration

Alkane metathesis converts two molecules of a linear alkane into a higher and a lower homologue (e.g., 2 C₆H₁₄ ⇌ C₅H₁₂ + C₇H₁₆) without external hydrogen. The reaction proceeds via a tandem catalytic cycle that couples (i) dehydrogenation of alkanes to olefins, (ii) olefin metathesis, and (iii) hydrogenation of the metathesis products back to alkanes. The overall transformation is thermoneutral (ΔH ≈ 0) but requires removal of the hydrogen generated in step (i) to drive the equilibrium toward olefins; this is achieved by operating under a hydrogen‑lean atmosphere or by using a hydrogen‑acceptor sacrificial alkene.

Dehydrogenation catalysts are typically pincer‑ligated Ir(III) or Fe(II) complexes (e.g., (PCP)IrH₂) that effect reversible β‑hydride elimination at 150–200 °C with turnover frequencies (TOF) of 10–100 h⁻¹. Olefin metathesis is performed by well‑defined Schrock (Mo, W) or Grubbs (Ru) catalysts; the second‑generation Grubbs catalyst (RuCl₂(=CHPh)(IMes)(PCy₃)) delivers >95 % conversion for internal olefins at 40 °C. Hydrogenation of the resulting olefins is accomplished by the same pincer complex operating in reverse or by a separate heterogeneous Pd/C catalyst.

Key design variables include: (a) matching the dehydrogenation and hydrogenation rates to avoid olefin accumulation, which leads to isomerisation and coking; (b) selecting a metathesis catalyst tolerant to residual hydrogen and alkane solvent; (c) controlling the reaction temperature to balance the endothermic dehydrogenation (ΔH ≈ +120 kJ mol⁻¹) against the exothermic hydrogenation (ΔH ≈ –120 kJ mol⁻¹). Recent advances employ a single bifunctional catalyst (e.g., a Ta‑hydride supported on silica‑alumina) that performs all three steps, simplifying reactor design.

Process integration studies show that coupling alkane metathesis with a downstream isomerisation unit (solid acid, e.g., Pt/USY) can upgrade low‑value n‑paraffins (C₈–C₁₂) into high‑octane branched isomers, improving the gasoline pool. A pilot plant (10 kt yr⁻¹) demonstrated a 30 % increase in research octane number (RON) with a net energy penalty of <5 % relative to conventional catalytic reforming.

Mechanistic Diagnosis Question
A researcher observes that the rate of alkane metathesis of n‑hexane is first order in the concentration of the pincer Ir dehydrogenation catalyst and zero order in the Grubbs metathesis catalyst. Propose which step is rate‑determining and justify your answer using the tandem cycle.
The observed kinetics indicate that the dehydrogenation step (alkane → olefin + H₂) is rate‑determining. In the tandem cycle, the overall rate is limited by the slowest elementary transformation. A first‑order dependence on the Ir catalyst shows that its turnover controls the flux of olefin into the metathesis manifold. Zero‑order dependence on the Grubbs catalyst means the metathesis step is fast relative to olefin supply and does not accumulate intermediates; thus it is not rate‑limiting. Hydrogenation, being the reverse of dehydrogenation on the same Ir centre, will share the same kinetic bottleneck. Therefore, the rate‑determining step is the β‑hydride elimination from the Ir‑alkyl intermediate to generate the olefin.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a tandem catalytic system is described, always write the three sub‑cycles explicitly and assign a provisional rate‑determining step. Use the reaction order with respect to each catalyst component as the primary clue; a first‑order dependence on one catalyst and zero order on the others pinpoints the slow step. Practice deriving the overall rate law from the steady‑state approximation for the intermediate olefin concentration.

Electrochemical Oxidation of Hydrocarbons: Direct Electron Transfer and Mediated Pathways

Electrochemical oxidation offers a route to functionalise C–H bonds under mild conditions, avoiding stoichiometric oxidants. Two principal pathways exist: direct anodic oxidation and mediated oxidation. In direct oxidation, the hydrocarbon adsorbs on the anode surface (Pt, BDD, or dimensionally stable anodes) and loses an electron to form a radical cation (RH·⁺). The oxidation potential correlates with the ionisation energy of the C–H bond; for methane the onset is >2.5 V vs. SHE on Pt, while for more activated substrates (e.g., benzylic positions) it drops to 1.2–1.5 V. The radical cation rapidly deprotonates to give a carbon‑centred radical, which can undergo further oxidation to a carbocation (ECE mechanism) or couple with nucleophiles (water, acetate) to yield alcohols or esters.

Mediated oxidation employs a redox‑active species (mediator) that is electrochemically regenerated at the anode. Common mediators include TEMPO (2,2,6,6‑tetramethylpiperidine‑1‑oxyl), which cycles between the oxoammonium cation (TEMPO⁺) and the hydroxylamine (TEMPOH). The oxoammonium ion abstracts a hydrogen atom from the substrate (k ≈ 10³–10⁴ M⁻¹ s⁻¹ for benzylic C–H), generating the carbon radical and TEMPOH. The mediator is then re‑oxidised at the anode (E₁/₂ ≈ 0.7 V vs. SHE). This strategy lowers the required cell voltage, improves selectivity, and enables catalytic turnover numbers >10⁴.

Other mediators: (i) Mn(salen) complexes for allylic oxidation; (ii) Fe‑porphyrins mimicking cytochrome P450, operating at 0.9 V vs. SHE; (iii) iodide/iodine couples for halogenation‑oxidation sequences. The choice of mediator is dictated by the substrate’s oxidation potential and the desired product distribution.

Cell design considerations: (a) anode material – boron‑doped diamond (BDD) provides a wide potential window (>3 V) and suppresses oxygen evolution; (b) electrolyte – non‑nucleophilic supporting electrolytes (e.g., Bu₄NPF₆ in acetonitrile) avoid side reactions; (c) mass transport – rotating‑disk electrodes or flow cells achieve limiting currents of 10–50 mA cm⁻² for 0.1 M substrate. Faradaic efficiencies for selective hydroxylation of toluene to benzyl alcohol using TEMPO/BDD can exceed 80 % at 1.2 V cell voltage.

Industrial outlook

Electrochemical routes are being piloted for the conversion of methane to methanol (via mediated oxidation with Cu‑exchanged zeolites as co‑catalysts) and for the upgrading of heavy naphtha fractions to oxygenates. The main challenges remain catalyst stability under anodic potentials, separation of products from the electrolyte, and scaling of BDD electrode area to industrial current densities (>500 mA cm⁻²).

Green Chemistry Metrics for Hydrocarbon Conversion: Atom Economy, E‑Factor, and Life‑Cycle Assessment

Quantifying the sustainability of hydrocarbon processes requires metrics that go beyond yield. Atom economy (AE) is defined as (molecular weight of desired product ÷ sum of molecular weights of all reactants) × 100 %. For steam cracking of naphtha to ethylene (C₂H₄), the stoichiometric reaction C₁₀H₂₂ → 5 C₂H₄ + H₂ gives an AE of 56 % because a large fraction of carbon ends up in by‑products (methane, propylene, aromatics). In contrast, catalytic dehydrogenation of ethane (C₂H₆ → C₂H₄ + H₂) has an AE of 93 % (only H₂ as co‑product).

E‑factor (mass of waste per mass of product) captures solvent, catalyst loss, and unrecovered by‑products. Typical values: petroleum refining (overall) 0.1–0.5; fine‑chemical synthesis 5–50; pharmaceutical routes >100. For a hydrocracking unit producing diesel, the E‑factor is ≈0.2 when hydrogen is sourced from steam‑methane reforming with CO₂ capture; it rises to >1 if hydrogen is from unabated natural gas.

Process mass intensity (PMI) = (total mass input) ÷ (mass of product) = E‑factor + 1. PMI is useful for comparing alternative routes to the same product. A life‑cycle assessment (LCA) expands the boundary to include feedstock extraction, energy generation, and end‑of‑life. For ethylene, cradle‑to‑gate GWP (global warming potential) ranges from 1.8 kg CO₂ eq kg⁻¹ (steam cracking with renewable electricity) to 2.5 kg CO₂ eq kg⁻¹ (conventional naphtha cracking).

Key levers to improve metrics: (i) feedstock selection – using bio‑derived ethane (from ethanol dehydration) reduces fossil carbon input; (ii) catalyst lifetime – extending zeolite cycle length from 30 to 60 days cuts catalyst waste by 50 %; (iii) heat integration – pinch analysis on a steam‑cracking furnace can recover 15 % of duty, lowering fuel gas consumption and CO₂ emissions; (iv) product slate optimisation – linear programming (LP) models that maximise high‑value products while constraining carbon intensity drive refinery configuration toward lighter, more hydrogen‑rich outputs.

Reporting standards (e.g., ISO 14040/44 for LCA, ACS GCI Pharmaceutical Roundtable for E‑factor) ensure comparability.
