When evaluating a new hydrocarbon technology, calculate AE, E‑factor, and cradle‑to‑gate GWP side‑by‑side with the incumbent process; a genuine advance must improve at least two of the three without worsening the third.

  • Confusing the rate‑determining step in a tandem catalytic cycle by assuming the metathesis catalyst order reflects its intrinsic activity rather than olefin supply.
  • Neglecting the equilibrium isotope effect on pre‑equilibrium steps when interpreting primary kinetic isotope effects in C–H activation studies.
  • Assuming that a low anodic onset potential on a BDD electrode guarantees selective functionalisation; competing oxygen evolution often dominates above 2.0 V vs. SHE.
  • Using atom economy alone to judge greenness while ignoring solvent recovery and catalyst turnover numbers that dominate the E‑factor.
  • Overlooking the impact of hydrogen source (grey vs. blue vs. green) on the life‑cycle GWP of dehydrogenation and hydrocracking processes.
  • Treating mediated electrochemical oxidation as a single‑step process without accounting for mediator degradation pathways that limit catalyst turnover.
  • Misapplying the steady‑state approximation to radical autoxidation when the initiation rate is time‑dependent (e.g., photoinitiation with decaying light intensity).

These advanced topics integrate mechanistic insight, catalyst design, electrochemical engineering, and sustainability metrics to give a comprehensive view of modern hydrocarbon chemistry.

Photochemical [2+2] Cycloaddition and Cis‑Trans Isomerization of Alkenes

Ultraviolet irradiation of simple alkenes promotes a π→π* transition that creates a diradical or excimer intermediate capable of forming cyclobutane rings through a [2+2] cycloaddition. The reaction is symmetry‑forbidden in the ground state (Woodward–Hoffmann rules) but allowed in the excited singlet state, giving a concerted suprafacial‑suprafacial pathway for ethylene and a stepwise diradical pathway for substituted alkenes. Substituents that stabilize the diradical (aryl, alkoxy) lower the quantum yield for cycloaddition and increase the competing cis‑trans isomerization via rotation about the C=C bond in the excited state. In the laboratory, sensitizers such as benzophenone (triplet energy 69 kcal mol⁻¹) enable triplet‑mediated cycloaddition, which proceeds through a triplet diradical and shows different stereochemical outcomes: the triplet pathway often gives a mixture of head‑to‑head and head‑to‑tail cyclobutanes, whereas the singlet pathway is strictly suprafacial. Quantum yields for cycloaddition of cyclohexene are typically 0.02–0.05, while isomerization quantum yields can reach 0.3. Industrial relevance appears in the synthesis of cyclobutane‑containing fragrances (e.g., exaltolide precursors) and in the photodegradation of polyolefins, where Norrish type II reactions generate chain‑scission radicals that accelerate embrittlement. Control of wavelength (λ < 300 nm for direct excitation, 300–350 nm with sensitizers) and oxygen exclusion are critical because triplet oxygen quenches excited states and initiates autoxidation.

Hydroformylation (Oxo Process) of Alkenes: Mechanism, Catalyst Evolution, and Industrial Application

Hydroformylation converts an alkene, carbon monoxide, and hydrogen into an aldehyde with one additional carbon atom. The catalytic cycle begins with a Rh(I) or Co(I) carbonyl complex that undergoes oxidative addition of H₂ to give a metal‑hydride. Alkene coordination followed by migratory insertion into the M–H bond yields an alkyl‑metal carbonyl. CO insertion into the M–C bond forms an acyl‑metal intermediate, which undergoes reductive elimination of the aldehyde and regenerates the active catalyst. Regioselectivity (linear vs. branched aldehyde) is governed by the steric and electronic properties of the phosphine ligands: bulky, electron‑rich phosphites (e.g., BISBI) favor linear aldehydes (n/iso > 20:1) for propylene, while less hindered trialkylphosphines give lower selectivity. The commercial low‑pressure oxo process (LP‑Oxo) uses Rh/TPPTS (water‑soluble phosphine) in a biphasic system, allowing catalyst recycling and operation at 80–120 °C, 10–30 bar syngas. Cobalt carbonyl catalysts (Co₂(CO)₈) operate at higher pressures (200–300 bar) and temperatures (150–180 °C) but produce more hydrogenation by‑products (alcohols, alkanes). Typical plant capacities range from 200 kt yr⁻¹ to 1 Mt yr⁻¹ of butyraldehyde (from propylene) and 2‑ethylhexanal (from 1‑octene). Catalyst deactivation pathways include phosphine oxidation, Rh leaching, and formation of inactive Rh‑carbonyl clusters; ligand design (chelating diphosphines, sulfonated phosphines) mitigates these issues. The aldehyde products are hydrogenated to alcohols (plasticizers, detergents) or oxidized to acids (precursors for polyesters).

Mechanistic discrimination
A kinetic study of propylene hydroformylation with a Rh/BISBI catalyst shows a first‑order dependence on [Rh] and [CO]⁻¹, zero‑order in [H₂], and a linear‑to‑branched ratio of 25:1 at 100 °C. Propose the rate‑determining step and explain how the observed orders support your assignment.
The inverse first‑order in CO indicates that CO dissociation from the Rh‑carbonyl precursor is required before alkene coordination; thus the rate‑determining step is alkene insertion into the Rh–H bond after CO loss. Zero‑order in H₂ means oxidative addition of H₂ is fast and reversible, occurring before the RDS. The high linear selectivity reflects the steric bulk of BISBI, which favors the less hindered primary alkyl intermediate.
Exam‑day strategy
When a hydroformylation question gives kinetic orders, map each order to a pre‑equilibrium or RDS: negative CO order → CO dissociation before RDS; zero H₂ order → H₂ addition before RDS; first‑order catalyst → single catalytic site. Sketch the catalytic cycle and label the step that matches the orders.

Ziegler‑Natta and Metallocene Catalysis for Olefin Polymerization: Active Site Structure, Tacticity Control, and Process Economics

Ziegler‑Natta (ZN) catalysts are heterogeneous TiCl₄ supported on MgCl₂ with internal (phthalate) and external (alkoxysilane) donors. The active site is a Ti(III)–alkyl species generated by alkylaluminum co‑catalyst (AlEt₃). Propylene inserts into the Ti–C bond via a Cossee‑Arlman mechanism; the chiral environment of the Ti center, imposed by the donor ligands, enforces isotactic placement (mmmm > 95 %). Typical ZN catalysts give broad molecular‑weight distributions (Đ ≈ 4–8) and require hydrogen as a chain‑transfer agent to control Mₙ. Metallocene catalysts (e.g., rac‑ethylene‑bis(indenyl)ZrCl₂ activated by methylaluminoxane, MAO) are homogeneous, single‑site systems. The C₂‑symmetric ansa‑zirconocene creates a well‑defined chiral pocket that yields highly isotactic polypropylene (mmmm > 99 %) with narrow Đ (≈ 2). C₁‑symmetric or Cs‑symmetric metallocenes produce syndiotactic or atactic polymers. Catalyst productivity for modern ZN systems reaches 30–50 kg PP g⁻¹ Ti h⁻¹, while metallocenes achieve 10–20 kg PP g⁻¹ Zr h⁻¹ but command higher ligand cost. Process economics: ZN slurry or gas‑phase reactors (loop, fluidized bed) dominate bulk PP production (> 70 Mt yr⁻¹) because of lower catalyst cost and robustness to poisons. Metallocene plants are justified for specialty grades (high melt‑strength, narrow MWD, tailored comonomer incorporation) where premium pricing offsets catalyst expense. Recent advances include supported metallocenes on silica (single‑site heterogeneous) and post‑metallocene catalysts (e.g., phenoxy‑imine Ni, Fe) that operate with lower MAO loadings.

Direct Partial Oxidation of Methane to Methanol: Catalytic Cycles, Selectivity Challenges, and Emerging Technologies

Direct conversion of methane to methanol avoids the energy‑intensive syngas route (steam reforming → methanol synthesis). The thermodynamic ceiling at 200 °C is ΔG° ≈ + 30 kJ mol⁻¹, so the reaction requires an oxidant (O₂, H₂O₂, N₂O) and a catalyst that stabilizes a high‑valent metal‑oxo or radical intermediate. In the Cu‑exchanged zeolite (Cu‑ZSM‑5) system, a dicopper‑oxo (Cu₂O₂)²⁺ site abstracts a hydrogen atom from methane (C–H BDE ≈ 439 kJ mol⁻¹) forming a Cu‑OH and a methyl radical that rebounds to give a surface‑bound methoxy species. Hydrolysis releases methanol and regenerates the Cu site. Selectivity is limited by over‑oxidation of methanol to formaldehyde and CO₂; typical one‑pass yields are 5–10 % at 200 °C with 50 % methanol selectivity. Homogeneous Periana catalysts (Pt(II)‑bipyrimidine in concentrated H₂SO₄) achieve > 90 % selectivity at 220 °C but suffer from catalyst separation and corrosion. Photocatalytic routes (TiO₂, Cu₂O) use visible light to generate holes that oxidize methane; quantum yields remain < 1 %. Electrochemical oxidation on Pt or BDD anodes in acidic media gives Faradaic efficiencies of 30–40 % at 1.5 V vs. RHE, but competing O₂ evolution dominates at higher potentials. Emerging strategies: (i) tandem catalysis where a methane‑activating metal‑oxo transfers a methyl group to a second catalyst that performs selective hydroxylation; (ii) membrane reactors that continuously extract methanol to suppress over‑oxidation; (iii) bio‑inspired Fe‑porphyrin models that mimic cytochrome P450 rebound mechanism with engineered second‑sphere hydrogen‑bond donors to improve selectivity.

Fischer‑Tropsch Synthesis: Reaction Network, Catalyst Design, and Product Distribution Modeling

Fischer‑Tropsch (FT) synthesis polymerizes surface‑bound CHₓ monomers derived from CO dissociation on Fe or Co catalysts. The Anderson‑Schulz‑Flory (ASF) distribution predicts the weight fraction of hydrocarbon chain length n as Wₙ = (1 − α)² n αⁿ⁻¹, where α is the chain‑growth probability. Typical α values: 0.85–0.95 for Co (high‑temperature FT, 200–240 °C) giving diesel‑range C₁₀–C₂₀; 0.6–0.8 for Fe (high‑temperature FT, 300–350 °C) yielding more olefins and oxygenates. Catalyst design tunes α: Co on SiO₂ or Al₂O₃ with Re promoter enhances CO dissociation and suppresses methane; Fe with K promoter increases CO₂ tolerance and water‑gas‑shift activity, adjusting H₂/CO ratio in situ. Reactor type influences product slate: fixed‑bed (Sasol) gives high α but suffers from heat removal; slurry‑phase (Sasol‑Slurry) improves temperature control and allows higher per‑pass conversion; microchannel reactors achieve near‑isothermal operation, pushing α toward 0.98 for wax‑maximizing modes. Product upgrading: hydrocracking of FT wax (C₂₀⁺) over bifunctional Pt/USY yields high‑quality diesel and jet fuel with low aromatics (< 1 %). Process integration with CO₂ capture and renewable H₂ (green FT) reduces life‑cycle GWP to ≈ 0.5 kg CO₂ eq kg⁻¹ product, compared with 2.5 kg CO₂ eq kg⁻¹ for conventional crude‑derived diesel. Kinetic models combine Langmuir‑Hinshelwood CO adsorption, CHₓ hydrogenation, and chain‑growth steps; parameter estimation uses transient isotopic labeling (¹³CO) to resolve surface coverages.

  • Assuming that a high α value automatically guarantees a narrow molecular‑weight distribution; the ASF distribution is inherently broad (Đ ≈ 2) regardless of α.
  • Confusing the role of K promoter in Fe‑FT (enhances water‑gas‑shift) with that of Re in Co‑FT (improves reducibility); they address different kinetic bottlenecks.
  • Neglecting that methanol selectivity in direct methane oxidation drops sharply above 250 °C because surface methoxy species undergo rapid C–O bond cleavage to formaldehyde.
  • Treating the Cossee‑Arlman insertion step as irreversible in ZN polymerization; chain‑transfer to AlEt₃ and β‑hydride elimination are competitive and determine Mₙ.
  • Overlooking that the inverse CO order in hydroformylation kinetics signals CO dissociation before the RDS, not CO inhibition of the active catalyst.
  • Assuming that metallocene catalysts always give lower productivity than ZN; supported metallocenes can reach comparable kg polymer g⁻¹ metal h⁻¹ with proper activator loading.
  • Failing to account for the equilibrium isotope effect on pre‑equilibrium steps when interpreting primary kinetic isotope effects in C–H activation studies.

Oxidative Coupling of Methane (OCM) and Non‑Oxidative Methane Dehydroaromatization

Oxidative coupling of methane converts two CH₄ molecules into C₂H₆ (ethane) and C₂H₄ (ethylene) using O₂ as a soft oxidant over a solid catalyst. The reaction proceeds via gas‑phase methyl radicals generated at the catalyst surface; the key elementary steps are (1) H‑atom abstraction from CH₄ by a surface lattice oxygen or a metal‑oxo site to give •CH₃, (2) gas‑phase coupling of two •CH₃ to C₂H₆, and (3) oxidative dehydrogenation of C₂H₆ to C₂H₄. Typical catalysts are Li/MgO, Na₂WO₄/Mn/SiO₂, or Sr/La₂O₃, operated at 750–850 °C and 1–5 bar. Single‑pass CH₄ conversion is limited to 15–30 % to avoid deep oxidation to CO/CO₂; C₂ selectivity of 70–80 % is considered state‑of‑the‑art. The main deactivation routes are carbonate formation on basic oxides and sintering of the active phase.

Non‑oxidative methane dehydroaromatization (MDA) uses a bifunctional Mo/HZSM‑5 or Zn/HZSM‑5 catalyst at 700 °C, 1–5 bar, without O₂. The reaction network: (i) CH₄ activation on Mo‑carbide or Zn‑oxo sites to surface CHₓ species, (ii) oligomerization to C₆H₆ (benzene) and C₁₀H₈ (naphthalene) inside the zeolite pores, (iii) hydrogen release. The overall stoichiometry is 6 CH₄ → C₆H₆ + 9 H₂. Benzene selectivity of 60–70 % at 10–15 % CH₄ conversion has been reported. Coke formation on the external surface and dealumination of the zeolite limit catalyst lifetime to 10–20 h on stream; regeneration by oxidative burn‑off restores activity but gradually erodes the Mo/Zn dispersion.

Both processes share a common challenge: the high C–H bond dissociation energy of methane (≈ 439 kJ mol⁻¹) demands either a strong oxidant (OCM) or a highly reactive metal‑carbide/oxo site (MDA). Reactor engineering focuses on rapid quenching to freeze the C₂ product distribution (OCM) or on hydrogen‑permeable membranes to shift the MDA equilibrium toward aromatics. Industrial deployment remains limited; OCM pilot plants (e.g., Siluria, 2015) demonstrated 1.5 t day⁻¹ ethylene but were not commercialized due to separation costs. MDA is pursued primarily as a route to BTX (benzene‑toluene‑xylene) co‑production with H₂ for fuel‑cell applications.

Catalytic Hydrodeoxygenation of Biomass‑Derived Oxygenates to Hydrocarbons

Hydrodeoxygenation (HDO) removes oxygen from lignocellulosic platform molecules (e.g., furfural, 5‑hydroxymethylfurfural, levulinic acid, phenolics) to yield alkanes, cycloalkanes, and aromatics suitable for fuel blending. The reaction network on sulfided CoMo/Al₂O₃ or NiMo/Al₂O₃ at 300–350 °C, 30–80 bar H₂ involves (i) hydrogenation of C=O to alcohol, (ii) C–O bond cleavage via hydrogenolysis to give a hydrocarbon fragment and water, and (iii) possible ring‑hydrogenation or dealkylation. For phenolics, the direct deoxygenation (DDO) pathway (C–O scission on the aromatic ring) competes with the hydrogenation (HYD) pathway (ring saturation followed by C–O cleavage). DDO preserves the aromatic ring, giving benzene, toluene, xylenes (BTX); HYD yields cyclohexanes.

Noble‑metal catalysts (Pt, Pd, Ru) on acidic supports (HZSM‑5, Nb₂O₅, TiO₂) enable HDO at lower temperatures (200–250 °C) and pressures (10–30 bar). Pt/HZSM‑5 shows high DDO selectivity for guaiacol → benzene (≈ 85 % at 250 °C) because the zeolite Brønsted sites stabilize the phenoxy intermediate and promote C–O cleavage. However, coke formation from poly‑condensation of reactive intermediates deactivates the acid sites within 50 h. Bimetallic formulations (e.g., PtRe, PdFe) improve resistance to sulfur poisoning from feed impurities and modulate hydrogenolysis activity.

Process integration couples fast pyrolysis (500 °C, 1 s residence) with in‑situ HDO in a two‑stage reactor: the first stage vaporizes bio‑oil, the second stage passes vapors over a HDO catalyst. This reduces the water content of the product oil from 20–30 wt % to < 2 wt % and raises the heating value from 18 MJ kg⁻¹ to > 40 MJ kg⁻¹. Economic analyses indicate a minimum fuel selling price of 0.8–1.2 USD L⁻¹ for a 2000 t day⁻¹ plant, contingent on catalyst lifetime > 500 h and hydrogen sourced from steam‑methane reforming with carbon capture.

Mechanistic Discrimination in HDO of Guaiacol
A researcher observes that guaiacol conversion over Pt/HZSM‑5 at 250 °C yields 70 % benzene, 20 % cyclohexane, and 10 % methoxy‑cyclohexane. When the reaction is repeated with D₂ instead of H₂, the benzene product shows no deuterium incorporation, while cyclohexane incorporates six deuterium atoms. Explain which pathway (DDO or HYD) dominates for each product and why the isotopic labeling pattern supports your assignment.
Benzene formed without deuterium indicates direct C–O bond cleavage (DDO) on the aromatic ring; the aromatic π‑system does not undergo hydrogenation, so no D atoms are added. Cyclohexane with six deuteriums results from full ring hydrogenation (HYD) followed by C–O cleavage; each of the six ring C–H positions originates from D₂, giving C₆D₁₂. Methoxy‑cyclohexane retains the O‑CH₃ group, showing partial hydrogenation of the ring without C–O scission. Therefore, benzene arises via DDO, cyclohexane via HYD, and the labeling pattern cleanly distinguishes the two routes.
Exam‑Day Strategy for HDO Questions
When a question asks to predict product distribution from a phenolic feed, first identify the catalyst type: sulfided CoMo/NiMo favors HYD at high H₂ pressure, while noble metals on acidic zeolites favor DDO. Next, check temperature and pressure: lower T (≤ 250 °C) and acidic support push DDO; higher T (≥ 300 °C) and sulfided catalysts push HYD. Finally, use isotopic labeling clues (D₂ vs H₂) to confirm the pathway — no D in aromatic product = DDO; full D incorporation in saturated ring = HYD.

Photoredox Catalysis for Hydrocarbon Functionalization

Photoredox catalysis uses visible‑light absorption by a metal complex (e.g., Ir(ppy)₃, Ru(bpy)₃²⁺) or an organic dye (e.g., 4CzIPN) to generate a long‑lived excited state that can act as a strong oxidant or reductant. In hydrocarbon chemistry, the most common mode is oxidative quenching: the excited photocatalyst (*PC) oxidizes a sacrificial electron donor (e.g., DIPEA) to generate PC⁻, which then reduces a suitable precursor (e.g., alkyl halide, sulfonium salt) to a carbon‑centered radical. The radical adds to an alkene, abstracts H from a C–H bond, or undergoes cross‑coupling with a nickel catalyst (dual photoredox/nickel catalysis).

Key mechanistic steps for C–H functionalization of simple alkanes: (1) *PC (E₁/₂* ≈ +1.2 V vs SCE for Ir(ppy)₃) oxidizes a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) catalyst such as quinuclidine or a decatungstate anion (W₁₀O₃₂⁴⁻) to a radical cation; (2) the HAT catalyst abstracts a hydrogen atom from the alkane (BDE 410–440 kJ mol⁻¹) generating an alkyl radical; (3) the alkyl radical is trapped by an electrophilic partner (e.g., an alkene, a N‑hydroxyphthalimide ester) or undergoes nickel‑catalyzed cross‑coupling with an aryl halide. Decatungstate operates via ligand‑to‑metal charge transfer (LMCT) upon 300–350 nm irradiation, directly abstracting H from strong C–H bonds without a separate photocatalyst.

Selectivity is governed by bond‑dissociation energies and steric accessibility: tertiary > secondary > primary C–H bonds. For example, photoredox/HAT functionalization of n‑hexane with decatungstate and an acrylate gives a 3:1 ratio of secondary to primary adducts. In the presence of a chiral Lewis acid, enantioselective radical addition to alkenes can reach > 90 % ee. Industrial relevance is emerging: a pilot plant for the synthesis of 1,6‑hexanediol from adipic acid via photoredox decarboxylative coupling demonstrated 85 % yield at 0.5 mol % Ir catalyst, but catalyst cost and light‑penetration depth in large reactors remain barriers.

Catalytic Dehydrogenative Coupling of Alkenes and Alkynes

Dehydrogenative coupling (DHC) forms C–C bonds between two unsaturated partners with concomitant H₂ evolution, avoiding pre‑functionalized halides or organometallic reagents. A representative system uses a pincer‑ligated Ir or Fe complex (e.g., (iPr₄PNP)IrH₄) that operates via metal‑ligand cooperation: the pincer arm undergoes reversible dearomatization/aromatization, enabling β‑hydride elimination from an alkyl intermediate and reductive elimination to forge the new C–C bond. For terminal alkynes, oxidative coupling (Glaser‑Hay) uses Cu(I)/O₂ to give diynes; however, catalytic DHC of alkenes with alkynes (enyne synthesis) proceeds via a metallacyclopentene intermediate on a Rh(I) or Co(I) catalyst.

Mechanistic sequence for Rh‑catalyzed enyne synthesis from ethylene and phenylacetylene: (i) oxidative coupling of the alkyne and alkene on Rh(I) to a rhodacyclopentene; (ii) β‑hydride elimination from the metallacycle to give a Rh‑hydride and a 1,3‑diene; (iii) reductive elimination of H₂ from the Rh‑hydride (often assisted by a base such as KOtBu) regenerates Rh(I). The overall stoichiometry: C₂H₄ + PhC≡CH → PhCH=CH‑CH=CH₂ + H₂. Turnover numbers (TON) of 500–2000 have been reported at 80–120 °C with 0.1–0.5 mol % Rh. Competing side reactions include alkyne trimerization to benzene derivatives and alkene oligomerization; ligand design (electron‑rich phosphines, NHCs) suppresses these pathways.

Fe‑based DHC catalysts (e.g., Fe(PNP)H₂) operate under milder conditions (50 °C, 1 bar H₂) and are attractive for cost‑sensitive bulk chemical production. A recent Fe‑catalyzed dehydrogenative coupling of 1‑hexene with phenylacetylene gave the corresponding enyne in 78 % isolated yield with 0.2 mol % Fe, TON ≈ 400. The Fe cycle proceeds via Fe(0)/Fe(II) redox rather than metal‑ligand cooperation, and the rate‑determining step is C–H activation of the alkyne. Process integration with in‑situ H₂ removal (membrane reactor) shifts equilibrium toward product and enables continuous operation.

Catalytic C–H Borylation of Alkanes and Arenes

Transition‑metal‑catalyzed borylation converts inert C–H bonds directly into C–B bonds using diboron reagents (B₂pin₂, B₂cat₂). Iridium catalysts bearing bipyridine or phenanthroline ligands (e.g., Ir(OMe)(cod))₂ / dtbpy) achieve high regioselectivity for terminal methyl groups of n‑alkanes (primary C–H) at 80–120 °C, delivering alkyl‑boronates that can be oxidized to alcohols or used in Suzuki couplings. The catalytic cycle: (i) oxidative addition of B–B bond to Ir(I) to give Ir(III)‑boryl; (ii) C–H activation via σ‑bond metathesis or concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) to form Ir‑alkyl‑boryl; (iii) reductive elimination of the C–B product and regeneration of Ir(I). For arenes, the same Ir system borylates the most electron‑rich position (ortho/para to activating groups) with > 95 % regioselectivity.

Recent advances include earth‑abundant metal catalysts: Fe(0) complexes with N‑heterocyclic carbene ligands catalyze borylation of arenes at 100 °C with B₂pin₂, achieving TON up to 1000. Mn(I) carbonyl complexes (Mn(CO)₅Br / phosphine) enable directed borylation of alkylarenes via a Mn‑hydride intermediate that undergoes reversible β‑hydride elimination. Photochemical borylation using Ir(ppy)₃ and B₂pin₂ under blue LEDs proceeds at room temperature, expanding substrate scope to heteroarenes (pyridines, furans) that are prone to thermal decomposition.

Industrial relevance lies in the synthesis of boron‑containing building blocks for pharmaceuticals and agrochemicals. A continuous‑flow process for the borylation of 2‑methylpyridine (Ir/dtbpy, 0.5 mol %, 100 °C, 30 min residence) delivers the 3‑boryl product in 92 % yield, which is subsequently converted to a kinase inhibitor. Catalyst recycling is achieved by immobilizing the Ir complex on a polystyrene‑supported bipyridine resin; leaching < 5 ppm Ir after 10 cycles. Green‑chemistry metrics: atom economy 98 % (only H₂ by‑product), E‑factor ≈ 2 (solvent recovery), and life‑cycle assessment shows a 40 % reduction in CO₂e versus traditional halogenation/Suzuki routes.

  • Assuming OCM and MDA share the same rate‑determining step; OCM is limited by methyl‑radical coupling, MDA by C–C bond formation on the zeolite acid site.
  • Confusing the DDO and HYD pathways in HDO; DDO preserves aromaticity, HYD saturates the ring — isotopic labeling with D₂ is the definitive probe.
  • Overlooking that photoredox HAT catalysts (decatungstate, quinuclidine) operate by distinct mechanisms: LMCT vs proton‑coupled electron transfer.
  • Treating dehydrogenative coupling as a simple oxidative coupling; DHC requires a metal‑hydride intermediate and H₂ release, not just radical dimerization.
  • Believing that Ir‑catalyzed borylation always gives primary selectivity; sterically hindered ligands (e.g., 3,5‑di‑tert‑butyl‑bipyridine) can invert selectivity to secondary C–H bonds.
  • Neglecting catalyst deactivation by boron‑oxide formation in borylation; addition of a sacrificial silane (Et₃SiH) scavenges B‑O species and extends catalyst life.
  • Assuming that all earth‑abundant metal borylation catalysts operate via the same Ir‑type oxidative addition; Fe and Mn often proceed through metal‑hydride or radical pathways.

These advanced topics illustrate how mechanistic insight, catalyst design, and process integration converge to transform simple hydrocarbons into valuable chemicals.

Catalytic Alkane Isomerization for Octane Enhancement

Light n‑paraffins such as n‑butane and n‑pentane have low research octane numbers (RON ≈ 90–92) whereas their branched isomers (isobutane, isopentane) exceed 100 RON. Industrial isomerization units therefore convert normal paraffins to their branched counterparts to upgrade the gasoline pool. The reaction is carried out over bifunctional catalysts that combine a metallic dehydrogenation/hydrogenation function (typically Pt or Pd) with a solid acid function (chlorided alumina, sulfated zirconia, or a medium‑pore zeolite such as USY or Beta). The accepted mechanistic sequence is: (i) dehydrogenation of the n‑alkane to the corresponding olefin on the metal site; (ii) skeletal isomerization of the olefin via a carbenium‑ion intermediate on the Brønsted acid site; (iii) hydrogenation of the iso‑olefin back to the branched alkane on the metal site. Because the overall transformation is thermodynamically limited, an excess of hydrogen (10–30 bar) is maintained to suppress coke formation and to drive the equilibrium toward the desired isomers. Typical operating temperatures lie between 150 °C and 200 °C; higher temperatures accelerate isomerization but also promote cracking, which reduces liquid yield. Commercial processes such as UOP’s Penex (for C₅/C₆) and Butamer (for C₄) employ a chloride‑promoted alumina catalyst that is periodically regenerated by controlled oxychlorination to restore acidity lost to coke deposition. Catalyst life of 2–4 years is common, with activity monitored by the n‑/iso‑paraffin ratio in the product. Recent developments focus on zeolite‑based catalysts (e.g., Pt/USY, Pt/Beta) that offer higher shape selectivity, limiting the formation of cracked C₁–C₃ gases and enabling operation at lower hydrogen partial pressures. Process integration with a downstream reformer allows the hydrogen‑rich off‑gas from isomerization to satisfy the reformer’s hydrogen demand, improving overall refinery hydrogen balance.

Catalytic Alkylation of Light Olefins with Isobutane

Alkylation combines isobutane with C₃–C₅ olefins (primarily butenes) to produce a high‑octane blending component known as alkylate (RON 94–96, MON 90–92). The reaction proceeds via carbocation chemistry on strong Brønsted acid sites. In the traditional liquid‑acid routes, either anhydrous hydrogen fluoride (HF) or concentrated sulfuric acid (H₂SO₄) serves as the catalyst. The mechanism initiates with protonation of the olefin to a secondary or tertiary carbenium ion, which then abstracts a hydride from isobutane to generate a tertiary butyl cation. Successive addition of olefin units and hydride transfers yields a mixture of C₈ isomers dominated by 2,2,4‑trimethylpentane (isooctane) and 2,3,4‑trimethylpentane. Operating conditions are tightly controlled: HF units run at 0–10 °C with an isobutane/olefin molar ratio of 8–12 to minimize polymerization; H₂SO₄ units operate at 10–15 °C with similar ratios but require large acid‑to‑hydrocarbon volumes (≈ 1.5 L acid / L feed) to maintain acid strength. Both liquid acids suffer from severe corrosion, hazardous handling, and spent‑acid disposal issues. Solid‑acid alternatives — such as β‑zeolite, USY, or sulfonated ion‑exchange resins — have been commercialized (e.g., ExxonMobil’s AlkyClean, Albemarle’s SAA). These catalysts operate at higher temperatures (50–80 °C) and lower isobutane/olefin ratios (4–6), delivering alkylate with slightly lower RON (≈ 92) but eliminating liquid‑acid hazards. Catalyst deactivation by coke and oligomer buildup is mitigated by periodic oxidative regeneration or by continuous catalyst circulation in a moving‑bed reactor. Product quality specifications focus on low sulfur (< 1 ppm), low olefin content (< 0.5 wt %), and a narrow boiling range (C₇–C₉) to meet gasoline blending requirements.

Mechanistic Reasoning
A feed containing an equimolar mixture of n‑butane and 2‑butene is passed over a Pt/USY zeolite catalyst at 200 °C and 20 bar H₂. Predict the major hydrocarbon products and rationalize the selectivity.
Under these conditions the Pt sites rapidly dehydrogenate n‑butane to 1‑butene and 2‑butene. The USY acid sites isomerize the butenes to isobutene (2‑methylpropene) via a tertiary carbenium ion. Simultaneously, the acid sites protonate 2‑butene to a secondary butyl cation, which abstracts a hydride from isobutane (generated in situ from n‑butane isomerization) to give a tertiary butyl cation. This cation adds another isobutene molecule, and after a second hydride transfer the dominant C₈ product is 2,2,4‑trimethylpentane (isooctane). Minor products include 2,3,4‑trimethylpentane and cracked C₃–C₅ gases. The high hydrogen pressure suppresses deep cracking and coke formation, steering the network toward the desired alkylate isomers.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When comparing liquid‑acid and solid‑acid alkylation, remember three decisive factors: (1) operating temperature (liquid acids require sub‑ambient cooling), (2) isobutane/olefin ratio (liquid acids need large excess), and (3) product RON (solid acids typically 1–2 points lower). Examiners often ask which catalyst gives the highest octane — answer: HF/H₂SO₄, but note the trade‑off with safety and corrosion.

Catalytic Hydrodesulfurization and Hydrodenitrogenation

Removal of heteroatoms from petroleum fractions is essential to meet environmental specifications and to protect downstream catalysts. Hydrodesulfurization (HDS) and hydrodenitrogenation (HDN) are carried out in trickle‑bed reactors over sulfided CoMo or NiMo catalysts supported on γ‑alumina. The active phase consists of MoS₂ slabs promoted by Co or Ni atoms at the edges, creating “Co‑Mo‑S” or “Ni‑Mo‑S” sites with enhanced hydrogenation activity. For sulfur removal, two parallel pathways operate on refractory molecules such as dibenzothiophene (DBT): direct desulfurization (DDS) cleaves the C–S bonds without prior aromatic ring hydrogenation, while the hydrogenation (HYD) route first saturates one aromatic ring, then eliminates H₂S. CoMo catalysts favor DDS and are preferred for naphtha and light gas oil; NiMo catalysts exhibit higher hydrogenation activity, making them suitable for HDN and for deep HDS of heavy feeds where steric hindrance around the sulfur atom demands ring saturation. Typical conditions: 300–380 °C, 30–100 bar H₂, liquid hourly space velocity (LHSV) 1–3 h⁻¹. Kinetic models employ Langmuir–Hinshelwood rate expressions with competitive adsorption of H₂S, NH₃, and aromatics; inhibition by H₂S is particularly strong for DDS. Catalyst life of 2–5 years is achieved by periodic in‑situ re‑sulfidation and by guarding against metal contaminants (Ni, V, As) that deposit on the support and block pores. Advanced formulations incorporate phosphorus or boron promoters to improve dispersion of the active phase and to increase resistance to coke. Process integration with a hydrogen plant ensures sufficient H₂ partial pressure; the H₂S‑rich off‑gas is sent to a Claus unit for sulfur recovery.

Catalytic Methane Steam Reforming and Dry Reforming

Steam methane reforming (SMR) is the dominant industrial route to synthesis gas (syngas). The overall reaction CH₄ + H₂O ⇌ CO + 3 H₂ is strongly endothermic (ΔH°₂₉₈ = +206 kJ mol⁻¹) and proceeds over Ni‑based catalysts (10–20 wt % Ni on Al₂O₃, often promoted with MgO, CaO, or rare‑earth oxides to enhance metal dispersion and suppress carbon formation). Operating at 750–950 °C and 20–30 bar, the equilibrium favors syngas with an H₂/CO ratio of ≈ 3, ideal for ammonia synthesis after water‑gas shift. Carbon deposition via the Boudouard reaction (2 CO ⇌ C + CO₂) and methane cracking (CH₄ ⇌ C + 2 H₂) is the principal deactivation mechanism; steam‑to‑carbon ratios of 2.5–3.5 are maintained to gasify surface carbon. Dry reforming of methane (DRM), CH₄ + CO₂ ⇌ 2 CO + 2 H₂ (ΔH°₂₉₈ = +247 kJ mol⁻¹), offers a route to utilize CO₂ and produces syngas with an H₂/CO ratio near unity, suitable for Fischer‑Tropsch synthesis. DRM catalysts based on Ni, Rh, or Ru supported on CeO₂, MgO, or La₂O₃ exploit support oxygen mobility to remove surface carbon via the Mars‑van Krevelen mechanism. However, DRM suffers from rapid sintering and coking at the high temperatures (700–850 °C) required for acceptable conversion. Combined reforming (simultaneous steam and CO₂ feed) balances the H₂/CO ratio and mitigates coking by providing both steam and lattice oxygen for carbon gasification. Recent research explores structured catalysts (metallic foams, microchannel reactors) to improve heat transfer and reduce pressure drop, enabling operation at lower temperatures with higher per‑pass conversion.

Catalytic C–H Fluorination of Hydrocarbons

Direct fluorination of C–H bonds provides a powerful strategy to introduce fluorine into pharmaceuticals, agrochemicals, and materials. Three mechanistic classes dominate: electrophilic fluorination, radical hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) fluorination, and nucleophilic fluorination under oxidative conditions. Electrophilic reagents such as Selectfluor (F‑TEDA‑BF₄), N‑fluorobenzenesulfonimide (NFSI), and elemental F₂ deliver “F⁺” equivalents. With F₂, reaction proceeds via a radical chain (initiation by homolysis, propagation through H‑abstraction by F·), but selectivity is poor and over‑fluorination is common. Selectfluor and NFSI enable metal‑catalyzed electrophilic fluorination; for example, Pd(II)/Pd(IV) cycles activate a C–H bond via concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD), followed by reductive elimination from a Pd(IV)‑F intermediate to forge the C–F bond. Radical HAT fluorination employs a catalyst (Mn, Fe, or Cu complex) that generates a high‑valent metal‑oxo or metal‑fluoro species capable of abstracting a hydrogen atom from a strong C–H bond; the resulting carbon radical then captures a fluorine atom from a fluorinated co‑reagent (e.g., NFSI, AgF₂). This approach tolerates a wide range of substrates, including unactivated alkanes, and can be driven photochemically (Ir(ppy)₃, blue LEDs) or electrochemically. Nucleophilic fluorination uses metal fluorides (AgF, CuF₂, CsF) in combination with an oxidant (e.g., MnO₂, electrochemical anodic oxidation) to generate a fluorinating agent in situ; the reaction typically proceeds via a single‑electron oxidation of the substrate to a radical cation, followed by fluoride capture. Key challenges across all methods are controlling regioselectivity (primary vs secondary vs tertiary C–H), avoiding polyfluorination, and managing the toxicity and corrosivity of fluorine sources. Recent advances include the use of supramolecular hosts to pre‑organize substrates for site‑selective fluorination and the development of flow‑chemistry protocols that safely handle F₂ gas at millimolar concentrations.

  • Confusing the DDS and HYD pathways in HDS; DDS preserves the aromatic ring, HYD saturates it — isotopic labeling with D₂ is the definitive probe.
  • Assuming that solid‑acid alkylation gives the same RON as HF/H₂SO₄; solid acids typically produce alkylate 1–2 octane points lower because of higher reaction temperature.
  • Neglecting the steam‑to‑carbon ratio in SMR; insufficient steam leads to rapid Ni sintering and carbon encapsulation, not merely thermodynamic limitation.
  • Treating dry reforming as a simple reverse water‑gas shift; DRM requires simultaneous activation of both CH₄ and CO₂ and is limited by catalyst coking, not equilibrium alone.
  • Believing that electrophilic fluorination reagents (Selectfluor, NFSI) operate by the same mechanism as F₂; they deliver F⁺ via polar pathways and are compatible with transition‑metal catalysis, whereas F₂ proceeds through a radical chain.
  • Overlooking that NiMo catalysts are preferred for HDN while CoMo excels at HDS; swapping them reduces heteroatom removal efficiency.
  • Assuming that all C–H fluorination methods tolerate heteroatoms equally; radical HAT fluorination is more tolerant of oxygen‑ and nitrogen‑containing functional groups than electrophilic fluorination, which can oxidize sensitive moieties.

Catalytic C–H Carboxylation of Alkanes and Arenes with Carbon Dioxide

Direct carboxylation of inert C–H bonds using CO₂ offers an atom‑economic route to carboxylic acids while consuming a greenhouse gas. The transformation proceeds through three mechanistic manifolds that differ in the nature of the C–H activation step and the oxidation state of the metal catalyst. In the first manifold, a low‑valent transition‑metal complex (Ni⁰, Pd⁰, or Cu⁰) undergoes oxidative addition into a relatively acidic sp² C–H bond of an arene or heteroarene, generating a metal‑aryl intermediate. Subsequent insertion of CO₂ into the metal‑carbon bond yields a metal‑carboxylate that, after protonolysis, furnishes the aryl‑carboxylic acid. This pathway is operative for electron‑deficient arenes (e.g., pyridines, quinolines) where the C–H bond acidity lowers the barrier for oxidative addition. Representative catalysts include Ni(cod)₂/PCy₃ and Pd(PPh₃)₄, typically employed with a stoichiometric base such as Cs₂CO₃ to neutralize the generated acid.

The second manifold exploits a concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) mechanism mediated by a high‑valent metal (Pd²⁺, Rh³⁺, Ir³⁺) in the presence of a carboxylate or carbonate base. The base acts as an internal proton shuttle, enabling activation of less acidic sp³ C–H bonds in simple alkanes or alkyl‑substituted arenes. After C–H cleavage, the resulting metal‑alkyl species undergoes CO₂ insertion to give a metal‑carboxylate, which is released by transmetalation with a silyl chloride or by protodemetalation. Notable examples are the Pd(OAc)₂/Ag₂CO₃ system for benzylic carboxylation and the Cp*RhCl₂/AgSbF₆ catalyst for unactivated aliphatic C–H bonds. Reaction temperatures of 80–120 °C and CO₂ pressures of 30–50 bar are typical; higher pressures improve CO₂ solubility and drive the equilibrium toward product.

A third, emerging manifold uses photoredox catalysis to generate a radical cation from the hydrocarbon substrate, which then adds to CO₂ to form a carboxyl‑radical intermediate. Subsequent hydrogen‑atom transfer (HAT) from a thiol or silane quenches the radical, delivering the carboxylic acid. Iridium or copper photosensitizers (Ir(ppy)₃, Cu(dap)₂Cl) under blue‑LED irradiation enable carboxylation of electron‑rich arenes and even simple alkanes at ambient temperature and 1 atm CO₂. The radical pathway tolerates a broader range of functional groups but suffers from lower regioselectivity on poly‑substituted substrates.

Key challenges across all manifolds include competing decarboxylation, over‑carboxylation, and catalyst deactivation by CO₂‑induced carbonate formation. Ligand design (electron‑rich phosphines, N‑heterocyclic carbenes) and additive choice (Lewis acids such as AlMe₃ to sequester carbonate) have been shown to improve turnover numbers from <10 to >200. Recent flow‑chemistry implementations allow safe handling of high‑pressure CO₂ and continuous removal of water, further enhancing catalyst longevity.

Catalytic C–H Silylation of Hydrocarbons: Mechanisms and Synthetic Utility

Transition‑metal‑catalyzed silylation converts C–H bonds directly into C–Si bonds, providing a versatile handle for downstream functionalization (e.g., Hiyama coupling, oxidation to alcohols). The reaction typically employs a hydrosilane (R₃Si–H) as the silicon source and proceeds via either a σ‑bond metathesis pathway (early‑transition‑metal and lanthanide catalysts) or an oxidative addition/reductive elimination sequence (late‑transition‑metal catalysts). In the σ‑bond metathesis manifold, a d⁰ metal center such as Ti⁴⁺, Zr⁴⁺, or a lanthanide (e.g., Cp*₂Sm) coordinates the C–H bond and the Si–H bond simultaneously, effecting a four‑center transition state that exchanges the hydrogen atoms. This pathway is operative for relatively acidic C–H bonds (benzylic, allylic, α‑to heteroatoms) and proceeds under mild conditions (25–60 °C) with catalyst loadings of 1–5 mol %.

Late‑transition‑metal catalysts (Fe, Co, Ni, Cu, Rh, Ir) favor oxidative addition of the Si–H bond to give a metal‑silyl hydride, followed by C–H activation via either oxidative addition (for electron‑rich arenes) or CMD (for aliphatic C–H bonds). The resulting metal‑alkyl‑silyl intermediate undergoes reductive elimination to forge the C–Si bond and regenerate the catalyst. Iron catalysts supported by bis(imino)pyridine ligands have demonstrated remarkable activity for the silylation of unactivated alkanes (e.g., n‑hexane) at 80 °C with turnover numbers exceeding 500. Cobalt complexes bearing pincer ligands enable selective silylation at the terminal position of linear alkanes, a regioselectivity that is difficult to achieve with radical methods.

Regioselectivity is governed by a combination of steric and electronic factors. For arenes, electron‑donating groups direct silylation ortho/para via a Wheland‑type intermediate, whereas electron‑withdrawing groups favor meta‑silylation through a radical cation pathway when photoredox co‑catalysis is employed. In aliphatic substrates, tertiary C–H bonds are generally more reactive under radical HAT silylation (using a silyl radical generated from a silane and a photocatalyst), while primary C–H bonds can be targeted with sterically hindered catalysts that enforce a “terminal‑selective” pocket.

Synthetic utility is amplified by the stability of the C–Si bond toward many reaction conditions and its ready conversion to C–C, C–O, or C–N bonds. For example, aryl‑silanes produced by direct silylation undergo Hiyama‑Denmark coupling with aryl halides under fluoride activation, delivering biaryls without the need for pre‑functionalized boronic acids. Aliphatic silanes can be oxidized to alcohols (Tamao‑Fleming oxidation) or transformed into alkyl fluorides via electrophilic fluorination, showcasing the strategic value of C–H silylation in complex molecule synthesis.

Mechanistic Discrimination Experiment
A researcher observes that silylation of toluene with PhMe₂SiH catalyzed by Fe(PMe₃)₄ proceeds with a kinetic isotope effect (KIE) of 1.0 when the benzylic C–H is replaced by C–D, but a KIE of 3.2 when the aromatic C–H is deuterated. Which C–H activation pathway is operative for each position, and what does this imply about the rate‑determining step?
The unity KIE at the benzylic position indicates that C–H bond cleavage is not rate‑determining; the benzylic C–H activation likely occurs via a fast, reversible σ‑bond metathesis or a pre‑equilibrium oxidative addition, with the turnover‑limiting step being reductive elimination or silane activation. The significant primary KIE (3.2) at the aromatic position shows that aromatic C–H cleavage is rate‑determining, consistent with a concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) or oxidative addition pathway where the C–H bond breaking occurs in the turnover‑limiting transition state. Therefore, the catalyst operates through two distinct manifolds: a non‑rate‑determining benzylic activation and a rate‑determining aromatic C–H activation.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question presents KIE data for multiple positions, map each KIE magnitude to the corresponding elementary step: a KIE ≈ 1 signals a step after C–H cleavage is rate‑limiting; a KIE > 2 signals C–H cleavage itself is rate‑limiting. Pair this with the known catalyst class (early vs late metal) to assign the operative manifold quickly.

Cross‑Dehydrogenative Coupling of C–H Bonds: Oxidative Alkylation and Arylation

Cross‑dehydrogenative coupling (CDC) merges two distinct C–H bonds in a single oxidative step, forming a new C–C bond while releasing hydrogen (or a hydrogen equivalent) as the sole by‑product. The reaction requires an external oxidant (e.g., peroxides, hypervalent iodine, electrochemical anodic oxidation) to re‑oxidize the metal catalyst after each turnover. Two principal mechanistic families dominate: (1) a radical‑mediated pathway in which a metal‑oxo or metal‑peroxo species abstracts a hydrogen atom from the more electron‑rich C–H bond, generating a carbon‑centered radical that adds to a metal‑activated partner; and (2) a polar pathway where the metal catalyst (often Pd²⁺, Cu²⁺, or Ag⁺) activates one C–H bond via CMD to give a metal‑aryl or metal‑alkyl intermediate, which then undergoes transmetalation or nucleophilic attack by the second substrate activated as a nucleophile (e.g., an enolate, a heteroarene).

In the radical manifold, copper‑catalyzed CDC of benzyl C–H bonds with electron‑deficient alkenes (Michael acceptors) proceeds via Cu(II)‑tert‑butylperoxide generating a tert‑butoxy radical that abstracts the benzylic hydrogen. The resulting benzyl radical adds to the alkene, and the adduct radical is oxidized by Cu(II) to a carbocation, which is quenched by a nucleophile (often the peroxide anion) to give the alkylated product. This sequence tolerates a wide range of functional groups and operates at room temperature with 10 mol % Cu(OAc)₂ and 2 equiv tert‑butyl hydroperoxide.

The polar manifold is exemplified by Pd(II)‑catalyzed direct arylation of heteroarenes (e.g., thiophenes, indoles) with simple arenes. The heteroarene undergoes CMD at the C‑2 position (directed by the heteroatom) to give a Pd‑aryl species. The arene partner, often used in excess, coordinates to Pd and undergoes a second CMD, forming a diaryl‑Pd(II) intermediate that reductively eliminates the biaryl product. Oxidant such as benzoquinone or O₂ re‑oxidizes Pd⁰ to Pd²⁺. Regioselectivity on the arene partner is governed by electronic bias (electron‑rich positions react faster) and steric hindrance; ortho‑substituted arenes give lower yields.

Electrochemical CDC eliminates chemical oxidants, using anodic oxidation to generate the active metal‑oxidant in situ. For example, a divided cell with a graphite anode and Pt cathode enables Ni‑catalyzed alkyl‑alkyl coupling of two different aliphatic C–H bonds at 5 mA cm⁻², delivering cross‑coupled products in 60–70 % yield with minimal homocoupling. The key to selectivity lies in the differential oxidation potentials of the two substrates; the more easily oxidized partner forms a radical cation that couples with the metal‑activated partner.

Challenges include controlling chemoselectivity when multiple C–H bonds of similar reactivity are present, suppressing homocoupling, and managing over‑oxidation to carbonyl products. Ligand engineering (bidentate nitrogen ligands for Cu, phosphine‑sulfonate for Pd) and the use of directing groups (e.g., pyridine, amide) on one partner have proven effective in improving cross‑selectivity.

Catalytic C–H Oxidation to Alcohols and Ketones via High‑Valent Metal‑Oxo Intermediates

Direct oxidation of C–H bonds to alcohols or ketones using O₂, H₂O₂, or peracids as terminal oxidants is a cornerstone of green chemistry. The active oxidant in most transition‑metal systems is a high‑valent metal‑oxo species (M=O) that performs hydrogen‑atom transfer (HAT) or oxygen‑atom transfer (OAT) to the substrate. Iron‑ and manganese‑based catalysts with tetradentate ligands (e.g., N4Py, PDP, TPA) generate Fe⁴⁺=O or Mn⁴⁺=O intermediates that abstract hydrogen from strong aliphatic C–H bonds (BDE ≈ 95–100 kcal mol⁻¹) with rate constants up to 10⁴ M⁻¹ s⁻¹. The resulting carbon radical rebounds to the metal‑hydroxo species (the “oxygen rebound” mechanism) to give the alcohol product. This pathway is highly sensitive to ligand electronics: electron‑donating substituents on the ligand raise the redox potential of the M=O unit, accelerating HAT but also increasing the propensity for unproductive decay (e.g., dimerization to μ‑oxo species).

For aromatic C–H oxidation, a different manifold operates: electrophilic aromatic substitution by a metal‑oxo (or metal‑peroxo) species yields a Wheland‑type σ‑complex that rearomatizes by loss of a proton, delivering a phenol. Manganese(salen) catalysts with NaClO as oxidant effect hydroxylation of electron‑rich arenes (anisole, phenol) at the ortho/para positions with >90 % regioselectivity. The reaction proceeds via a Mn⁵⁺=O species that behaves as an electrophile; electron‑withdrawing groups on the arene dramatically reduce reactivity.

Copper‑catalyzed oxidation using H₂O₂ generates a Cu²⁺‑OOH (copper‑hydroperoxo) or Cu³⁺=O intermediate that can perform both HAT and OAT. In the presence of a directing group (e.g., 8‑aminoquinoline), the catalyst achieves site‑selective oxidation of a specific methylene C–H bond in a complex molecule, delivering a single alcohol diastereomer. The directing group chelates copper, positioning the M=O moiety for intramolecular HAT. This strategy has been applied to late‑stage functionalization of terpenes and steroids.

Key performance metrics include turnover number (TON), turnover frequency (TOF), and the alcohol/ketone ratio. For secondary C–H bonds, over‑oxidation to the ketone competes with alcohol formation; controlling the oxidant stoichiometry (1.1 equiv H₂O₂) and temperature (0 °C to 25 °C) favors alcohol. In contrast, tertiary C–H bonds cannot form alcohols via rebound (no α‑hydrogen) and instead yield tertiary radicals that undergo further oxidation or fragmentation. Recent advances employ photocatalytic generation of the M=O species (e.g., Ir(ppy)₃/blue LED with a cobalt‑porphyrin) to operate under milder conditions and with visible light, expanding the substrate scope to oxidation‑sensitive molecules.

  • Assuming that a single catalyst system can universally oxidize all C–H bond types; in practice, catalyst/ligand/oxidant combinations are highly substrate‑specific.
  • Confusing the oxygen‑rebound mechanism (radical HAT + rebound) with a concerted oxygen‑atom insertion; the former shows a primary KIE, the latter does not.
  • Neglecting the role of the directing group in site‑selective oxidation; without it, regioselectivity follows inherent substrate reactivity, not the desired position.
  • Treating electrochemical CDC as a simple replacement for chemical oxidants without considering the need for matched oxidation potentials of the two coupling partners.
  • Overlooking that silylation of unactivated alkanes often requires a radical HAT initiator (e.g., a silyl radical) rather than a pure σ‑bond metathesis pathway.
  • Believing that CO₂ carboxylation of simple alkanes proceeds readily under 1 atm CO₂; high pressure (≥30 bar) and a suitable base are essential to drive the equilibrium.
  • Assuming that all CDC reactions are tolerant of free amines; many oxidants (peroxides, hypervalent iodine) oxidize amines to imines or nitrones, requiring protection.

These advanced topics illustrate how mechanistic insight guides catalyst selection and reaction design for modern hydrocarbon functionalization.

Catalytic C–H Amination via Nitrene Transfer: Mechanisms, Catalysts, and Site‑Selectivity

Nitrene Precursors

Nitrene transfer to C–H bonds relies on precursors that generate a metal‑nitrene (M=NR) intermediate under mild conditions. Common sources include organic azides (R‑N₃), N‑haloamines (R‑NCl₂), iminoiodinanes (PhI=NR), and N‑sulfonyl‑oxycarbamates. Azides decompose thermally or photochemically to give nitrenes, but the reaction is often unselective. Iminoiodinanes, prepared from iodosylbenzene and the corresponding amine, deliver nitrenes cleanly upon coordination to a transition metal. N‑sulfonyl‑oxycarbamates (e.g., N‑tosyloxycarbamate) are bench‑stable and release a sulfonyl‑protected nitrene after activation by a metal catalyst.

Metal‑Nitrene Intermediates

The active species is a high‑valent metal‑nitrene, formally Mⁿ⁺=NR. For copper, a Cu(II)‑nitrene (Cu=NR) is invoked; for rhodium, a Rh(II)‑nitrene (Rh=NR) forms from Rh₂(OAc)₄; for iron, Fe(IV)=NR or Fe(V)=NR species are proposed. The nitrene can undergo two distinct pathways: (1) a concerted insertion into a C–H bond via a three‑center transition state (C–H···N–M) that preserves stereochemistry, and (2) a stepwise hydrogen‑atom transfer (HAT) to generate a carbon radical and a metal‑amido species, followed by radical rebound. The concerted pathway dominates with electron‑rich metals (Rh, Cu) and gives high retention of configuration at chiral centers. The HAT/rebound manifold is favored with more oxophilic metals (Fe, Mn) and shows a primary kinetic isotope effect (KIE ≈ 3–5).

Catalyst Families

Rhodium(II) carboxylates (Rh₂(OAc)₄, Rh₂(esp)₂) are the workhorses for intramolecular amination of sulfamate esters, delivering cyclic sulfamidates with excellent diastereoselectivity. Copper(I) complexes with bis(oxazoline) or phenanthroline ligands enable intermolecular amination of unactivated alkanes using N‑tosyloxycarbamates. Iron(II/III) porphyrins and non‑heme iron complexes (e.g., Fe(PDP)) catalyze amination with organic azides, often requiring an oxidant (PhIO) to reach the high‑valent Fe‑nitrene. Manganese(salen) systems operate with iminoiodinanes and show high activity for benzylic C–H amination. Recent developments include cobalt(III) corrinoids and nickel(II) complexes that expand the scope to heteroaromatic C–H bonds.

Site‑Selectivity Strategies

Intramolecular reactions are guided by tether length: five‑ and six‑membered ring formation is strongly favored. For intermolecular amination, selectivity follows the order tertiary > secondary > primary C–H bonds, modulated by steric hindrance and bond dissociation energies. Directing groups (e.g., pyridine, amide, 8‑aminoquinoline) chelate the metal and position the nitrene for selective functionalization of a proximal C–H bond. Ligand engineering (bulky chiral bisoxazolines) can invert the natural selectivity, enabling primary C–H amination in the presence of weaker secondary bonds. Photoredox generation of nitrenes (Ir(ppy)₃/blue LED) further broadens the substrate scope to oxidation‑sensitive molecules.

Applications

C–H amination provides direct access to nitrogen‑containing motifs prevalent in pharmaceuticals (e.g., β‑amino acids, cyclic amines). The Rh‑catalyzed intramolecular amination of sulfamate esters is a key step in the synthesis of the antiviral agent oseltamivir. Copper‑catalyzed intermolecular amination of simple alkanes furnishes protected amines that can be deprotected to give primary amines, a transformation otherwise requiring multi‑step sequences.

Catalytic C–H Sulfonylation and Sulfoxidation: Radical and Electrophilic Pathways

Sulfonyl Radical Generation

Sulfonyl radicals (R‑SO₂·) are generated from sulfonyl chlorides, sulfonyl azides, or sulfonyl peroxides under thermal, photochemical, or redox conditions. Visible‑light photoredox catalysis (Ir(ppy)₃, 450 nm) reduces sulfonyl chlorides to the radical anion, which fragments to R‑SO₂· and Cl⁻. Alternatively, copper(I) salts mediate single‑electron transfer (SET) to sulfonyl azides, releasing N₂ and the sulfonyl radical. These radicals add to C–H bonds via HAT, forming a carbon radical that couples with a second sulfonyl radical or undergoes oxidation to a carbocation for nucleophilic capture.

Electrophilic Sulfonylation

Electrophilic sulfonylation employs sulfonyl fluorides (SuFEx) or sulfonyl imidazoles as sulfonyl donors. Palladium(II) catalysts activate the S–F bond through oxidative addition, generating a Pd(IV)‑sulfonyl intermediate that undergoes reductive elimination to forge the C–S bond. This pathway is highly selective for electron‑rich arenes and heteroarenes, proceeding via a Wheland‑type σ‑complex. The reaction tolerates a wide range of functional groups and avoids radical side‑products.

Metal‑Catalyzed Sulfoxidation

Direct conversion of C–H bonds to sulfoxides (R‑SO‑R′) is achieved with manganese(salen) or iron(porphyrin) catalysts using peracids (m‑CPBA) or H₂O₂ as oxidants. The high‑valent metal‑oxo species performs oxygen‑atom transfer to a pre‑formed thioether, but tandem C–H thiolation/sulfoxidation sequences have been reported where a thiol adds across a C–H bond followed by in‑situ oxidation. Copper‑catalyzed systems with DTBP (di‑tert‑butyl peroxide) generate a Cu‑oxyl that abstracts hydrogen, and the resulting carbon radical couples with a sulfinate anion to give the sulfoxide after oxidation.

Regioselectivity

Radical sulfonylation follows the stability order of carbon radicals: benzylic ≈ allylic > tertiary > secondary > primary. Electron‑withdrawing groups on the sulfonyl radical increase its electrophilicity, favoring attack at electron‑rich C–H bonds. Electrophilic sulfonylation is directed by the arene’s substitution pattern (ortho/para to activating groups). Directing groups (e.g., 8‑aminoquinoline) enable site‑selective sulfonylation of unactivated aliphatic C–H bonds in complex molecules.

Industrial Relevance

Sulfonylation is a cornerstone of detergent and dye manufacturing. The continuous‑flow photoredox sulfonylation of alkyl chains with sodium sulfinates provides a scalable route to α‑sulfonyl carbonyl building blocks. Electrophilic SuFEx chemistry underpins the production of polysulfone polymers and sulfonamide pharmaceuticals.

Mechanistic Elucidation via Kinetic Isotope Effect
A copper‑catalyzed intermolecular amination of cyclohexane using N‑tosyloxycarbamate shows a rate decrease of 4.2‑fold when cyclohexane‑d₁₂ is used. The reaction proceeds with a primary KIE of 4.2. Does this support a concerted nitrene insertion or a stepwise HAT/rebound mechanism? Justify your answer.
A primary KIE of ~4 indicates that C–H bond cleavage occurs in the rate‑determining step. In a concerted three‑center insertion, the C–H bond breaks synchronously with N–C bond formation, typically giving a modest KIE (1.5–2.5). A stepwise HAT/rebound pathway involves a discrete hydrogen‑atom abstraction by the metal‑nitrene, which is the rate‑determining step and yields a larger primary KIE (3–6). Therefore, the observed KIE of 4.2 supports a stepwise HAT/rebound mechanism.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to distinguish between concerted and stepwise C–H functionalization, look for the magnitude of the kinetic isotope effect and the presence of radical‑clock substrates. A large primary KIE (>3) and radical‑clock ring‑opening are hallmarks of a HAT/rebound pathway; a small KIE and retention of stereochemistry point to a concerted insertion.

Catalytic C–H Phosphorylation and Phosphonylation: Metal‑Catalyzed P–C Bond Formation

Phosphine Oxide vs Phosphonate Coupling

Phosphorylation of C–H bonds can be achieved using phosphine oxides (R₃P=O) or dialkyl phosphonates (HP(O)(OR)₂). Phosphine oxides are thermodynamically stable; their direct use requires activation of the P=O bond. Dialkyl phosphonates possess a P–H bond that can undergo deprotonation to give a nucleophilic phosphonate anion, which adds to electrophilic metal‑activated C–H bonds. The choice of phosphorus reagent dictates the oxidation state of the product: phosphine oxides yield phosphine oxide‑substituted products, while phosphonates give phosphonate esters that can be further elaborated.

Nickel and Palladium Catalysis

Nickel(0) complexes (Ni(cod)₂) with bidentate phosphine ligands (e.g., dppe, dcype) catalyze the cross‑coupling of aryl halides with H‑phosphonates via oxidative addition, transmetalation, and reductive elimination, delivering aryl‑phosphonates. For direct C–H phosphorylation, nickel(II) salts (NiCl₂·glyme) with a photoredox co‑catalyst (Ir(ppy)₃) generate a Ni(III)‑phosphinidene intermediate that inserts into C–H bonds. Palladium(II/IV) cycles operate with phosphine oxide directing groups: a Pd(II) complex undergoes concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) at the ortho position of an aryl phosphine oxide, followed by oxidation to Pd(IV) with an electrophilic phosphorus source (e.g., PhP(O)Cl₂) and reductive elimination to forge the P–C bond.

Directing Group Assistance

Directing groups such as pyridine, oxazoline, and 8‑aminoquinoline chelate the metal and enable remote C–H phosphorylation. For aliphatic substrates, a bidentate directing group (e.g., picolinamide) positions the metal for γ‑C–H activation, delivering γ‑phosphonylated products after functional group removal. The directing group can be cleaved under mild hydrolytic conditions, providing a traceless strategy.

Stereochemical Outcomes

When a chiral center is present at the C–H activation site, the CMD step proceeds with retention of configuration due to the concerted nature of the deprotonation. Subsequent reductive elimination from a Pd(IV) center also occurs with retention, delivering enantioenriched phosphonylated products if the starting material is enantiopure. Nickel‑catalyzed radical pathways, however, can lead to racemization via a planar carbon radical intermediate.

Synthetic Utility

C–H phosphorylation provides direct access to phosphonate‑containing natural products (e.g., fosfomycin analogues) and kinase inhibitor scaffolds. The method avoids pre‑functionalization (halogenation, metalation) and reduces step count. Recent applications include late‑stage phosphorylation of peptide side chains for phosphoproteomics probes.

Catalytic C–H Halogenation: Electrophilic, Radical, and Metal‑Mediated Strategies

Electrophilic Halogenation of Activated Arenes

Electrophilic aromatic halogenation uses Cl₂, Br₂, or I₂ in the presence of Lewis acids (FeCl₃, AlCl₃) or oxidants (H₂O₂, m‑CPBA) to generate the electrophilic halogen species (Cl⁺, Br⁺, I⁺). The reaction follows the classic SEAr mechanism: formation of a σ‑complex (Wheland intermediate) followed by deprotonation. Regioselectivity is governed by the directing effects of substituents; activating groups (–OH, –OR, –NH₂) direct ortho/para, while deactivating groups (–NO₂, –CF₃) direct meta. Iodination of electron‑rich arenes can be achieved with I₂/NaIO₄ in aqueous media, avoiding harsh acids.

Radical Halogenation of Alkanes

Free‑radical halogenation of alkanes employs Cl₂ or Br₂ under UV irradiation or thermal initiation. Chlorination is fast and poorly selective (relative reactivity 1°:2°:3° ≈ 1:3.8:5), giving mixtures. Bromination is slower and highly selective for tertiary C–H bonds (1°:2°:3° ≈ 1:80:1600). The chain mechanism involves initiation (homolysis of X₂), propagation (H‑abstraction by X·, then X‑abstraction by carbon radical), and termination. Industrial chlorination of methane to methyl chloride uses controlled Cl₂/CH₄ ratios and high temperatures to limit polychlorination.

Metal‑Catalyzed Halogenation (Cu, Fe, Mn)

Copper(I/II) catalysts with N‑halosuccinimide (NCS, NBS) enable selective benzylic and allylic halogenation via a Cu‑halogen radical pathway. Iron(porphyrin) complexes with PhICl₂ or PhIBr₂ generate high‑valent Fe‑halogen species that perform electrophilic halogenation of unactivated C–H bonds with moderate selectivity. Manganese(salen) catalysts with NaOCl effect α‑halogenation of carbonyl compounds via enolate formation and subsequent halogen transfer. These systems operate under milder conditions than elemental halogen and can be rendered enantioselective with chiral ligands.

Selectivity Control

Site‑selectivity in aliphatic halogenation is achieved by (i) using a directing group that chelates the metal (e.g., 8‑aminoquinoline for γ‑C–H chlorination), (ii) exploiting the innate reactivity order (tertiary > secondary > primary) with bromination, or (iii) employing a catalyst that discriminates based on steric bulk (bulky Cu‑NHC complexes favor less hindered primary C–H bonds). For aromatic halogenation, regioselectivity is tuned by the choice of Lewis acid and solvent polarity.

Applications in Radiolabeling

Electrophilic radio‑iodination (¹²⁴I, ¹³¹I) of arenes using I₂/oxidant is a standard method for preparing radiotracers for PET and SPECT imaging. Copper‑mediated radio‑fluorination (¹⁸F) of arylboronic esters via Cu(OTf)₂/pyridine enables late‑stage incorporation of fluorine‑18 into drug candidates. The high specific activity and short half‑life demand rapid, high‑yielding halogenation protocols.

Late‑Stage C–H Functionalization in Complex Molecule Synthesis: Directing Groups, Chemoselectivity, and Case Studies

Directing Group Design

Effective directing groups (DGs) must bind the catalyst strongly, be easily installed and removed, and not interfere with other functional groups. Common DGs include 8‑aminoquinoline (AQ), picolinamide, pyridyl‑oxazoline, and N‑acetyl‑amino acids. Bidentate DGs form five‑ or six‑membered metallacycles, positioning the metal for activation of a specific C–H bond (often γ or δ). Recent traceless DGs (e.g., 2‑pyridyl‑sulfoxide) can be cleaved reductively after functionalization, leaving no residual heteroatom.

Chemoselectivity in Polyfunctional Molecules

Complex natural products contain multiple C–H bonds of similar reactivity. Chemoselectivity is achieved by (i) matching the catalyst’s innate selectivity (e.g., Rh(II) prefers electron‑rich C–H bonds), (ii) using a DG that overrides innate reactivity, and (iii) protecting sensitive groups (e.g., silyl ethers) that could coordinate the catalyst. Reaction conditions (solvent, temperature, oxidant) are optimized to suppress side reactions such as over‑oxidation or β‑hydride elimination.

Case Study: Terpene Functionalization

The sesquiterpene caryophyllene oxide was subjected to a Pd(II)/AQ‑directed C–H arylation at the C‑12 position, a sterically hindered tertiary C–H bond. The AQ amide was installed on the carboxylic acid moiety, enabling a six‑membered palladacycle. The reaction delivered a single diastereomer in 68 % yield, which was elaborated to a potent CB₂ receptor ligand. The DG was removed by LiAlH₄ reduction, regenerating the free acid.

Case Study: Peptide Late‑Stage Modification

A cyclic peptide bearing a tryptophan residue was modified at the indole C‑2 position via a Cu‑catalyzed C–H borylation directed by a transient N‑oxide on the adjacent amide. The borylated peptide was subsequently converted to a fluorinated analogue via Suzuki coupling, providing a probe for receptor binding studies. The transient directing group was removed under mild reductive conditions, preserving the peptide backbone.

Future Directions

Emerging strategies include the use of artificial metalloenzymes that embed a metal cofactor in a protein scaffold for exquisite site‑selectivity, and electrochemical C–H activation that replaces chemical oxidants with electrons, enabling precise control over oxidation states. Machine‑learning models trained on high‑throughput experimentation data are beginning to predict optimal DG/catalyst combinations for unseen substrates.

  • Assuming that a single directing group works universally; in practice DG efficiency depends on the metal, oxidation state, and substrate geometry.
  • Confusing the selectivity trends of radical halogenation (tertiary > secondary > primary) with those of electrophilic aromatic substitution (ortho/para to activating groups).
  • Neglecting the primary kinetic isotope effect as a diagnostic tool; a KIE near unity rules out C–H cleavage in the rate‑determining step.
  • Overlooking that phosphine oxides require activation of the P=O bond, whereas phosphonates react via the P–H bond, leading to different catalytic cycles.
  • Treating all metal‑nitrene intermediates as identical; Cu‑nitrenes favor concerted insertion, while Fe‑nitrenes often proceed via HAT/rebound.
  • Believing that late‑stage functionalization always proceeds with complete chemoselectivity; competing reactions at unprotected heteroatoms are common and must be evaluated case by case.
  • Ignoring the impact of catalyst poisoning by sulfur‑containing functional groups in halogenation and amination reactions.

Catalytic C–H Alkylation via Borrowing Hydrogen and Transfer Hydrogenation

The borrowing‑hydrogen (or hydrogen‑autotransfer) strategy exploits the reversible dehydrogenation of an alcohol to the corresponding carbonyl compound, followed by C–C bond formation with a nucleophilic partner, and final hydrogenation of the unsaturated intermediate using the hydrogen stored on the catalyst. In hydrocarbon chemistry this manifold enables the direct alkylation of unactivated alkanes, alkenes, and arenes without pre‑formed organometallic reagents. Typical catalysts are based on ruthenium, iridium, or manganese pincer complexes that operate through a metal‑hydride/metal‑alkoxide cycle. The key mechanistic steps are: (i) oxidative addition of the O–H bond of the alcohol to the metal centre, (ii) β‑hydride elimination to give the carbonyl and a metal‑dihydride, (iii) condensation of the carbonyl with a nucleophile (e.g., an enolate, indole, or amine) to form an imine or α,β‑unsaturated carbonyl, and (iv) transfer of the two hydrides from the metal to the C=C or C=N bond, delivering the alkylated product and regenerating the catalyst. Selectivity is governed by the relative rates of dehydrogenation versus side reactions such as aldol condensation or over‑reduction. For primary alcohols, the equilibrium lies toward the aldehyde, which can undergo rapid imine formation with amines, giving N‑alkylated amines in high yield. Secondary alcohols generate ketones that are less electrophilic; therefore, more nucleophilic partners (e.g., malonates, β‑ketoesters) are required. The reaction is typically run in a high‑boiling solvent (toluene, mesitylene) at 120–150 °C with 0.5–2 mol % catalyst and a base (KOtBu, NaOH) to promote deprotonation of the nucleophile. Recent advances include earth‑abundant manganese‑PNP pincer catalysts that operate under milder conditions (80–100 °C) and tolerate water, expanding the substrate scope to biomass‑derived alcohols such as ethanol and isopropanol. The borrowing‑hydrogen approach is attractive for refinery integration because it converts low‑value alcohols (from fermentation or syngas reduction) into higher‑value alkylated aromatics (e.g., p‑xylene, ethylbenzene) while releasing only water as by‑product.

Photoredox/Nickel Dual Catalysis for C–H Arylation of Unactivated Alkanes

Merging visible‑light photoredox catalysis with nickel catalysis has opened a practical route to forge C(sp³)–C(sp²) bonds directly from simple alkanes and aryl halides. The photoredox catalyst (commonly Ir(ppy)₃, fac‑Ir(ppy)₃, or organic dyes such as 4CzIPN) absorbs blue light and reaches an excited state that can oxidize a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) mediator (e.g., quinuclidine, thiyl radicals, or N‑hydroxyphthalimide esters) to generate a highly reactive radical capable of abstracting a hydrogen atom from a strong C–H bond (BDE ≈ 98–105 kcal mol⁻¹). The resulting alkyl radical adds to a Ni⁰ complex ligated by a bipyridine or phenanthroline ligand, forming a Ni¹‑alkyl intermediate. Oxidative addition of an aryl bromide or chloride to this Ni¹ species yields a Ni³‑aryl‑alkyl complex, which undergoes rapid reductive elimination to deliver the cross‑coupled product and regenerate Ni⁰. The photoredox cycle is closed by reduction of the oxidized HAT mediator (or the Ir⁴⁺ species) by a sacrificial electron donor such as Hünig’s base (DIPEA) or a tertiary amine. Key parameters controlling selectivity are: (i) the HAT mediator’s polarity and steric bulk, which dictate site‑selectivity on the alkane (tertiary > secondary > primary), (ii) the nickel ligand electronics, which influence the rate of oxidative addition versus β‑hydride elimination, and (iii) the light intensity and wavelength, which affect the steady‑state concentration of the excited photocatalyst. Representative conditions: 1 mol % Ir(ppy)₃, 5 mol % NiCl₂·glyme, 10 mol % 4,4′‑dtbpy, 20 mol % quinuclidine, 2 equiv aryl bromide, 5 equiv alkane, DIPEA (3 equiv), MeCN/DMA (4:1), 450 nm LEDs, 25 °C, 12–24 h. Yields for functionalized alkanes (e.g., adamantane, cyclohexane, n‑hexane) range from 45–78 % with excellent regioselectivity for the weakest C–H bond. The method tolerates esters, ethers, and protected amines, making it suitable for late‑stage diversification of petroleum‑derived feedstocks. Recent mechanistic studies using transient absorption spectroscopy confirm that the HAT step is rate‑determining and that the Ni¹‑alkyl species is the resting state of the nickel cycle.

Worked Example: Predicting Site‑Selectivity in Photoredox/Nickel Arylation
A substrate mixture contains n‑octane, 2‑methylbutane, and cyclohexane in equal molar amounts. Under standard Ir/Ni/quinuclidine conditions with 4‑bromoanisole as the aryl partner, which C–H bond of each alkane is most likely to undergo arylation? Rank the three alkanes by expected product yield.
Quinuclidine‑mediated HAT prefers the weakest C–H bond. Bond dissociation energies: tertiary C–H (≈ 96 kcal mol⁻¹) < secondary (≈ 98 kcal mol⁻¹) < primary (≈ 101 kcal mol⁻¹). n‑Octane has only primary and secondary C–H bonds; the secondary positions (C‑2, C‑3, C‑4) are slightly weaker, so arylation occurs mainly at a secondary carbon. 2‑Methylbutane possesses a tertiary C–H at the 2‑position (BDE ≈ 96 kcal mol⁻¹) and several secondary/primary bonds; the tertiary site dominates. Cyclohexane has only secondary C–H bonds (BDE ≈ 98 kcal mol⁻¹). Therefore, the intrinsic reactivity order is 2‑methylbutane (tertiary) > cyclohexane (secondary) > n‑octane (secondary but more hindered). Expected yield ranking: 2‑methylbutane > cyclohexane > n‑octane.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for regioselectivity in a dual catalytic C–H arylation, first identify the HAT mediator and its known selectivity profile (tertiary > secondary > primary for quinuclidine; more electrophilic mediators can invert the order). Then consider steric accessibility of the C–H bonds in the substrate. Finally, check whether the nickel ligand is bulky (e.g., dtbpy) which can further bias toward less hindered sites. Writing the BDE hierarchy and the mediator’s preference side‑by‑side earns full credit.

Continuous‑Flow Reactor Design for Hydrocarbon Cracking and Olefin Production

Continuous‑flow (plug‑flow) reactors have become the preferred platform for high‑temperature steam cracking and catalytic cracking because they provide precise residence‑time control, superior heat‑transfer rates, and facile scale‑out by numbering‑up. In a typical steam‑cracking coil, a dilute hydrocarbon feed (naphtha, gas oil, or ethane) mixed with steam (steam‑to‑hydrocarbon ratio 0.3–0.6 kg kg⁻¹) is heated to 800–880 °C at 0.1–0.3 MPa. The coil diameter (10–30 mm) and length (30–80 m) are chosen to give a residence time of 0.1–0.5 s, which maximizes ethylene and propylene yields while limiting secondary reactions (coke formation, aromatization). Computational fluid dynamics (CFD) coupled with detailed kinetic models (≈ 2000 reactions, 300 species) is used to optimize coil geometry, temperature profile, and quench‑point location. For catalytic cracking (FCC), riser reactors operate in the fast‑fluidization regime (solids flux 200–500 kg m⁻² s⁻¹) with catalyst‑to‑oil ratios of 4–8 wt wt⁻¹. The riser height (30–45 m) and diameter (1.5–2.5 m) give a contact time of 1–3 s. Recent designs incorporate a “downer” section to reduce back‑mixing and a two‑stage regenerator (dense‑phase + dilute‑phase) to control coke burn‑off temperature (700–750 °C) and minimize NOx formation. Heat integration is critical: the hot flue gas from the regenerator preheats the feed and generates high‑pressure steam for the steam‑cracking furnace, achieving overall thermal efficiencies of 85–90 %. Advanced materials (e.g., 25Cr‑20Ni alloys, SiC‑coated tubes) extend coil life under cyclic thermal stress. Process analytical technology (PAT) such as on‑line gas chromatography, laser‑based velocimetry, and infrared thermography provides real‑time data for model‑predictive control (MPC) of coil outlet temperature (COT) and catalyst circulation rate. The shift toward lighter feeds (ethane, LPG) and the integration of renewable hydrogen for steam generation are driving the next generation of flow‑reactor designs that target > 30 % ethylene yield with < 0.5 wt % coke on catalyst.

Catalytic C–H Functionalization in Polymer Upcycling: Depolymerization to Value‑Added Chemicals

Polyolefins (polyethylene, polypropylene) and polystyrene constitute > 60 % of plastic waste. Conventional mechanical recycling degrades properties; chemical upcycling via catalytic C–H activation offers a route to monomers, lubricants, and aromatics. The central challenge is the inertness of saturated C–C and C–H bonds in a solid polymer matrix. Two complementary strategies have emerged: (1) hydrogenolysis over bifunctional metal‑acid catalysts (Pt/SiO₂‑Al₂O₃, Ru/TiO₂) at 200–250 °C and 20–30 bar H₂, which cleaves C–C bonds to give a narrow distribution of liquid alkanes (C₁₀–C₂₀) suitable as diesel‑range fuels or lubricant base oils. The metal sites activate H₂, while the acid sites promote β‑scission of surface alkyl intermediates. (2) Oxidative C–H functionalization using high‑valent metal‑oxo species (e.g., Mn(salen), Fe(PDP), or Cu‑nitrogen‑doped carbon) in the presence of an oxidant (H₂O₂, peracetic acid) at 80–120 °C converts polymer chains into oxygenated products (alcohols, ketones, carboxylic acids) that can be further transformed to diacids (adipic, sebacic) for nylon precursors. Reaction engineering employs swollen polymer gels in supercritical CO₂ or melt‑phase extrusion reactors to ensure catalyst–polymer contact. Kinetic studies show that the rate‑determining step is hydrogen abstraction from a secondary carbon (KIE ≈ 3–5), and that chain‑end initiation is negligible compared with random mid‑chain activation. Selectivity toward terminal versus internal functionalization is tuned by catalyst sterics: bulky porphyrins favor terminal oxidation, giving ω‑hydroxy‑polyethylene that can be cleaved to α,ω‑diols. Techno‑economic analyses indicate that a 100 kt yr⁻¹ upcycling plant based on Ru/TiO₂ hydrogenolysis can achieve a minimum selling price of $0.85 kg⁻¹ for the liquid alkane product, competitive with fossil‑derived naphtha when carbon credits are included. Life‑cycle assessment shows a 70 % reduction in GHG emissions relative to incineration.

Machine‑Learning Guided Catalyst Discovery for Hydrocarbon Transformation

The combinatorial space of ligand–metal–support combinations for hydrocarbon C–H activation exceeds 10⁶ candidates, making experimental screening impractical.
