Under similar conditions, Cu‑BTC gives higher alcohol/ketone ratios (≈ 1.5) for C₈–C₁₀ alkanes because the larger pore apertures (9 Å) accommodate longer substrates and allow faster product diffusion, reducing consecutive oxidation. Incorporating a secondary metal (e.g., 5 % Ni into Cu‑BTC) creates bimetallic sites that promote O–O bond cleavage, generating high‑valent Ni³⁺=O species that increase turnover frequency by a factor of 3 while maintaining > 80 % oxygenate selectivity.

Stability challenges include framework hydrolysis in the presence of water (a reaction by‑product) and metal leaching under oxidative conditions. Post‑synthetic modification with hydrophobic ligands (e.g., perfluorinated carboxylates) reduces water adsorption, extending catalyst lifetime to > 100 h on stream in a continuous fixed‑bed reactor. Regeneration by solvent washing (ethanol) and mild thermal activation (150 °C under N₂) restores > 90 % of initial activity. Process integration with a downstream distillation column that removes oxygenates in situ shifts equilibrium toward higher conversion (Le Chatelier) and mitigates product inhibition of the active sites.

Autothermal Reforming of Natural Gas to Syngas: Catalyst Formulation, Heat Integration, and Carbon Deposition Mitigation

Autothermal reforming (ATR) combines exothermic partial oxidation (CH₄ + ½ O₂ → CO + 2 H₂, ΔH = ‑36 kJ mol⁻¹) and endothermic steam reforming (CH₄ + H₂O → CO + 3 H₂, ΔH = +206 kJ mol⁻¹) in a single adiabatic reactor, achieving thermal neutrality at an oxygen‑to‑carbon (O/C) ratio of 0.5–0.6 and a steam‑to‑carbon (S/C) ratio of 1.5–2.0. Commercial catalysts are Ni‑based (10–20 wt % Ni on Al₂O₃‑MgO‑ZrO₂ supports) promoted with noble metals (0.2–0.5 wt % Rh or Pt) to enhance low‑temperature activity and resistance to sulfur poisoning. The support’s basicity (MgO) neutralizes acidic sites that catalyze coke formation, while ZrO₂ improves oxygen storage capacity, facilitating rapid oxidation of surface carbon during transient O₂ fluctuations.

Reactor design employs a refractory‑lined burner zone where fuel‑rich combustion raises the gas temperature to 1100–1200 °C, followed by a catalytic bed (300–500 mm diameter) where the remaining methane reacts at 950–1050 °C. The adiabatic temperature rise is controlled by the O/C ratio; excessive O₂ leads to hot spots (> 1300 °C) that sinter Ni particles, while insufficient O₂ causes temperature drop and methane slip. Typical syngas H₂/CO ratios of 1.8–2.2 suit downstream Fischer‑Tropsch or methanol synthesis. Heat integration recovers sensible heat from the effluent (≈ 1000 °C) to preheat feed steam and combustion air, achieving overall thermal efficiencies > 85 %.

Carbon deposition remains the primary deactivation pathway. Thermodynamic analysis shows that at S/C < 1.0 the Boudouard reaction (2 CO → C + CO₂) and methane cracking (CH₄ → C + 2 H₂) become favorable below 800 °C. Operational strategies to suppress coke include: (i) maintaining S/C ≥ 1.5, (ii) periodic oxidative regeneration (2 % O₂ in N₂ at 600 °C for 30 min) that gasifies deposited carbon without oxidizing Ni, (iii) adding 0.5 wt % CeO₂ to the support to provide lattice oxygen for in‑situ carbon gasification, and (iv) employing a structured catalyst (metallic monolith with washcoat) that improves radial heat transfer and reduces local carbon‑forming zones. Long‑term pilot tests (5000 h) with Ni‑Rh/Ce‑Zr‑Al₂O₃ show < 5 % activity loss when regeneration cycles are scheduled every 500 h.

  • Assuming hydroisomerization selectivity depends only on temperature; hydrogen pressure and metal/acid balance are equally decisive.
  • Confusing MFI’s propylene selectivity with ethylene‑focused MOR; pore dimensionality dictates the dominant cracking pathway.
  • Believing photocatalytic HAT with decatungstate proceeds via a radical chain; it is a direct excited‑state HAT, not a chain process.
  • Neglecting that MOF stability in aqueous oxidation media requires hydrophobic post‑treatment; untreated frameworks hydrolyze rapidly.
  • Treating autothermal reforming as a simple combination of POX and SR without accounting for adiabatic temperature profile and catalyst sintering risk.
  • Overlooking that Faradaic efficiency alone does not define electrochemical alkane oxidation viability; cell voltage, current density, and mediator turnover number must be evaluated together.
  • Assuming a single zeolite type (e.g., ZSM‑5) is optimal for all plastic‑oil aromatization; pore‑size distribution must match the molecular size of the target aromatics.
The advanced sections above integrate mechanistic detail, process constraints, and quantitative metrics essential for high‑level competitive examination performance.

Catalytic C–H Alkylation via Transient Directing Groups and Ligand‑Enabled Meta‑Selectivity

Transient directing groups (TDGs) allow reversible coordination to a metal centre, delivering regioselectivity without a permanently installed directing moiety. The most widely used TDGs for palladium catalysis are 2‑pyridylsilyl, 8‑aminoquinoline, and 2‑pyrimidylsilyl chlorides, which form a five‑membered palladacycle after C–H activation and are removed in situ by fluoride or aqueous work‑up. In a typical meta‑arylation of phenylacetic acid derivatives, the TDG is installed on the carboxylic acid as a mixed anhydride, the palladium(II) catalyst (Pd(OAc)₂, 10 mol %) undergoes concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) at the meta C–H bond, and the resulting aryl‑Pd(II) intermediate undergoes transmetalation with an aryl‑boronic acid followed by reductive elimination. Reported meta/ortho ratios exceed 20:1 when a nitrile‑containing ligand such as 2‑pyridinecarbonitrile is added, because the ligand occupies the coordination site that would otherwise favour ortho‑palladation. The reaction tolerates electron‑withdrawing and electron‑donating substituents on both coupling partners, and the TDG is recovered in >90 % yield after silica gel chromatography. Limitations include the need for a free carboxylic acid (or alcohol) handle, sensitivity to strongly coordinating heteroatoms (pyridine, phosphine oxides) that compete with the TDG, and a modest decrease in turnover number (TON ≈ 200) compared with permanent directing groups (TON > 1000). Recent developments employ chiral TDGs to induce enantioselectivity in the formation of axially chiral biaryls, achieving up to 92 % ee with a Pd/(S)-BINAP system.

Hydrogen Atom Transfer (HAT) Catalysis for Remote C–H Functionalization

Hydrogen atom transfer catalysis exploits high‑energy excited states or radical initiators to abstract a hydrogen atom from a relatively inert C–H bond, generating a carbon‑centred radical that can be trapped at a distal position. The benchmark system uses decatungstate (Na₄[W₁₀O₃₂]) under UV irradiation (λ ≈ 300 nm) to produce the powerful oxidant [W₁₀O₃₂]⁴⁻*, which abstracts hydrogen with a bond‑dissociation free energy (BDFE) of ≈ 100 kcal mol⁻¹. This enables 1,5‑HAT from a primary C–H bond to a radical centre located four atoms away, a process that underpins the remote functionalization of aliphatic chains in natural‑product synthesis. For example, irradiation of a N‑alkoxyphthalimide ester derived from a C₁₀ fatty acid in the presence of decatungstate and a suitable radical trap (e.g., TEMPO) furnishes the δ‑functionalized product in 55 % isolated yield with >10:1 regioselectivity for the δ‑position over the γ‑position. Kinetic isotope effect (KIE) measurements (k_H/k_D ≈ 5.2) confirm that hydrogen abstraction is rate‑determining. Alternative HAT catalysts include quinuclidine‑based photocatalysts (e.g., 3‑CF₃‑quinuclidine) that operate under visible light (blue LEDs) via an excited‑state amine radical cation, and thiyl radicals generated from thiophenol under photoredox conditions. The choice of catalyst dictates the polarity of the HAT event: electrophilic HAT (decatungstate) prefers electron‑rich C–H bonds, whereas nucleophilic HAT (thiyl) favours electron‑deficient sites. A practical limitation is the need for dilute solutions (≤ 0.05 M) to avoid bimolecular radical termination, and the sensitivity of decatungstate to moisture, which hydrolyses the polyoxometalate and diminishes activity.

Worked Example: Meta‑Arylation with a Transient Directing Group
Predict the major product when 4‑methoxyphenylacetic acid is subjected to Pd(OAc)₂ (10 mol %), 2‑pyrimidylsilyl chloride (1.2 eq), Ag₂CO₃ (2 eq), and 4‑fluorophenylboronic acid (1.5 eq) in HFIP at 80 °C for 12 h, followed by TBAF work‑up.
The carboxylic acid forms a mixed anhydride with the TDG, directing palladium to the meta C–H bond relative to the acid. The 4‑methoxy group is ortho/para‑directing but does not coordinate to Pd under these conditions. CMD occurs at the meta position (C‑3) giving a palladacycle. Transmetalation with 4‑fluorophenylboronic acid and reductive elimination installs the 4‑fluorophenyl group at the meta carbon. TBAF removes the silyl TDG, regenerating the free acid. The product is 3‑(4‑fluorophenyl)-4‑methoxyphenylacetic acid, isolated in ≈ 68 % yield with meta/ortho > 20:1.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question mentions a “transient directing group”, immediately identify the functional handle (acid, alcohol, amine) that can be derivatised in situ. Sketch the five‑membered palladacycle to verify the regiochemical outcome. For HAT problems, note the catalyst (decatungstate, quinuclidine, thiyl) because it determines polarity and the preferred C–H bond (electron‑rich vs electron‑poor). Write the KIE value if asked; a primary KIE > 3 signals H‑atom abstraction as the turnover‑limiting step.

Electrochemical C–H Functionalization in Flow Reactors: Mass Transport and Selectivity

Flow electrolysis decouples the diffusion layer thickness from the reaction time, allowing precise control of mass‑transport‑limited current densities (j_lim) that are difficult to achieve in batch cells. In a typical undivided flow cell equipped with a graphite felt anode and a stainless‑steel cathode, the inter‑electrode gap of 0.5 mm and a flow rate of 1 mL min⁻¹ give a residence time of ≈ 30 s and a linear velocity of 0.33 cm s⁻¹. Under these conditions, the limiting current for the oxidation of toluene to its radical cation (E₁/₂ ≈ +2.1 V vs SCE in MeCN/0.1 M nBu₄NPF₆) is ≈ 15 mA cm⁻². Operating at 80 % of j_lim suppresses over‑oxidation to benzaldehyde and benzoic acid, delivering the C–H amination product (via anodic generation of a nitrenium equivalent from N‑hydroxyphthalimide) in 72 % faradaic efficiency and 65 % isolated yield. The key advantage of flow is the rapid removal of the reactive radical cation from the electrode surface, which reduces dimerisation (bibenzyl formation) from 18 % in batch to < 3 % in flow. Divided cells with a Nafion membrane further improve selectivity for cross‑electrophile coupling (e.g., aryl‑alkyl C–C bond formation) by preventing cathodic reduction of the anodically generated aryl radical. However, the membrane introduces an additional ohmic drop (≈ 0.3 V) that must be compensated by raising the cell voltage, and fouling by polymeric by‑products can increase pressure drop over prolonged runs (> 24 h). Recent work demonstrates that pulsed potential waveforms (10 ms on / 50 ms off) regenerate the diffusion layer, boosting the turnover frequency by a factor of 2.5 without sacrificing faradaic efficiency.

Supramolecular Catalysis in Confined Spaces: Enzyme‑Mimetic Hydrocarbon Transformations

Self‑assembled coordination cages provide a nanoconfined environment that can alter both the thermodynamics and the kinetics of hydrocarbon reactions. The Fujita Pd₆L₄ octahedral cage (L = 2,4‑bis(4‑pyridyl)‑1,3,5‑triazine) possesses an internal cavity of ≈ 2.5 nm diameter and a hydrophobic interior lined with aromatic panels. Guest encapsulation of a linear alkane (e.g., n‑hexane) and a dienophile (e.g., maleic anhydride) accelerates the Diels–Alder reaction by 120‑fold relative to the bulk solution, with an endo/exo ratio of 9:1 compared with 3:1 in homogeneous phase. The rate enhancement originates from (i) increased effective molarity (≈ 0.5 M inside the cage vs 0.01 M in bulk), (ii) pre‑organisation of the diene in a s‑cis conformation enforced by π‑stacking with the cage walls, and (iii) stabilization of the polar transition state by the polarizable aromatic surfaces. Catalytic turnover is achieved by using a sub‑stoichiometric amount of a Pd(II) complex tethered to the cage vertex; the metal centre performs C–H activation of the encapsulated alkane, and the resulting alkyl‑Pd intermediate undergoes β‑hydride elimination to release the functionalized product, which diffuses out of the cage. Reported turnover numbers reach 350 before cage degradation (ligand oxidation) becomes significant. A related system based on a resorcinarene‑based hexameric capsule (deep‑cavity cavitand) catalyzes the selective oxidation of adamantane to 1‑adamantanol using H₂O₂ and a Mn(II)‑salen complex encapsulated in the capsule, delivering 78 % selectivity for the tertiary C–H bond versus 45 % in solution. The confinement also suppresses over‑oxidation to the ketone. Challenges include limited substrate scope (size‑exclusion < 1.2 nm), catalyst leaching under oxidative conditions, and the need for guest exchange kinetics faster than the catalytic cycle (k_ex > 10³ s⁻¹).

  • Assuming that any transient directing group works for all acid substrates; steric bulk and electronic nature of the TDG critically affect CMD barriers.
  • Confusing the polarity of HAT catalysts: decatungstate is electrophilic, thiyl radicals are nucleophilic; misassignment leads to wrong regio‑predictions.
  • Neglecting mass‑transport limitations in flow electrolysis; operating above the limiting current causes uncontrolled radical dimerisation and drops faradaic efficiency.
  • Treating supramolecular cages as universally applicable; substrate size must match the cavity dimensions, otherwise no rate acceleration occurs.
  • Overlooking that cage degradation (ligand oxidation, metal leaching) often limits turnover numbers far below those of homogeneous analogues.
  • Believing that a single set of electrolysis parameters (voltage, flow rate) can be transferred across different substrates without re‑optimising the diffusion layer.
  • Ignoring the need for a sacrificial oxidant or mediator in electrochemical C–H amination; direct anodic oxidation of the amine often leads to polymerisation.

These advanced topics integrate mechanistic insight, quantitative process parameters, and the practical constraints that examiners target in high‑level competitive chemistry papers.

Catalytic C–H Trifluoromethylation: Electrophilic, Radical, and Metal‑Mediated Strategies

Trifluoromethyl groups enhance metabolic stability, lipophilicity, and binding affinity in pharmaceuticals and agrochemicals, making late‑stage C–H trifluoromethylation a high‑value transformation. Three mechanistic manifolds dominate: electrophilic delivery of CF₃⁺ equivalents, radical generation of ·CF₃, and metal‑mediated transfer of a CF₃ ligand from a metal–CF₃ complex.

Electrophilic Trifluoromethylation

Reagents such as Togni’s hypervalent iodine(III) reagents (e.g., 1‑(trifluoromethyl)‑1,2‑benziodoxol‑3(1H)‑one) and Umemoto’s S‑(trifluoromethyl)dibenzothiophenium salts release a strongly electrophilic CF₃⁺ under mild Lewis‑acid or photochemical activation. Electron‑rich arenes (anisoles, phenols, indoles) undergo direct aromatic substitution at the para‑position with high regioselectivity. The reaction proceeds via a Wheland‑type σ‑complex; the rate‑determining step is the formation of the C–CF₃ bond, which is accelerated by electron‑donating groups. Typical conditions: 1.2 equiv reagent, 10 mol % BF₃·OEt₂, CH₂Cl₂, 0 °C → rt, 1–4 h. Yields of 60–85 % are common for activated arenes; deactivated substrates (nitrobenzene) give <10 % conversion.

Radical Trifluoromethylation

The Langlois reagent (NaSO₂CF₃) and its potassium analogue generate ·CF₃ upon single‑electron oxidation (photoredox, Cu(I)/Cu(II), Fe(II)/Fe(III), or electrochemical). Photoredox catalysts such as Ir(ppy)₃ (E₁/₂* = +0.31 V vs SCE) or organic dyes (4CzIPN) under blue LED irradiation oxidize the sulfinate to the CF₃ radical, which adds to alkenes, alkynes, or abstracts H‑atoms from weak C–H bonds (BDE ≤ 95 kcal mol⁻¹). For unactivated alkanes, a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer (HAT) catalyst (e.g., quinuclidine, decatungstate) is required to generate a carbon‑centered radical that traps ·CF₃. Copper‑catalyzed variants use Cu(I) salts (CuMe, CuI) with oxidants (tert‑butyl peroxybenzoate, O₂) to form a Cu(II)–CF₃ intermediate that undergoes reductive elimination. Regioselectivity follows radical stability: tertiary > secondary > primary C–H bonds. Functional‑group tolerance is high; esters, amides, and heterocycles survive.

Metal‑Mediated Trifluoromethyl Transfer

Well‑defined Cu(III)–CF₃, Ni(II)–CF₃, and Fe(III)–CF₃ complexes have been isolated and shown to transfer CF₃ to aryl/vinyl halides or pseudohalides via reductive elimination. In catalytic cycles, a Cu(I) precursor reacts with a CF₃ source (e.g., (trifluoromethyl)trimethylsilane, CF₃SiMe₃, activated by fluoride) to generate a Cu–CF₃ species that undergoes transmetalation with an aryl‑boronate or oxidative addition into an aryl‑iodide, followed by reductive elimination to forge the Ar–CF₃ bond. Ligand design (phenanthrolines, NHCs) controls the oxidation state and suppresses β‑fluoride elimination. Recent Fe‑catalyzed protocols employ FeCl₂/FeCl₃ with Grignard‑type CF₃ reagents (CF₃MgBr) to achieve trifluoromethylation of heteroarenes at room temperature with 70–90 % yields.

Synthetic Applications and Limitations

Electrophilic methods excel for electron‑rich (hetero)arenes but fail on electron‑deficient rings. Radical approaches enable alkyl and alkene trifluoromethylation but suffer from over‑oxidation and dimerisation of ·CF₃ (forming C₂F₆). Metal‑mediated catalysis provides the broadest scope, including cross‑coupling of aryl halides, but requires careful ligand optimisation and often stoichiometric oxidants. Industrial adoption is limited by the cost of CF₃ reagents and the need for rigorous fluorine‑safety protocols.

Catalytic C–H Alkynylation: Copper, Ruthenium, and Photoredox Pathways

Direct alkynylation of C–H bonds installs a versatile alkyne handle for subsequent click chemistry, Sonogashira coupling, or cycloadditions. Three catalytic platforms have matured: copper‑catalyzed oxidative alkynylation, ruthenium‑catalyzed carboxylate‑assisted C–H activation, and photoredox/nickel dual catalysis that merges radical generation with nickel‑mediated cross‑coupling.

Copper‑Catalyzed Oxidative Alkynylation

Cu(I) salts (CuI, CuOTf) in the presence of an oxidant (O₂, tert‑butyl hydroperoxide, or PhI(OAc)₂) activate terminal alkynes to copper acetylides. For directed ortho‑alkynylation of arenes, a bidentate directing group (DG) such as 2‑pyridyl, 8‑aminoquinoline, or picolinamide coordinates Cu and positions the C–H bond for concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD). The catalytic cycle: (1) DG‑assisted C–H cleavage gives a Cu(II)‑aryl intermediate; (2) transmetalation with the copper acetylide forms a Cu(III)‑aryl‑alkynyl species; (3) reductive elimination yields the ortho‑alkynylated product and regenerates Cu(I). Typical conditions: 10 mol % Cu(OAc)₂, 2 equiv alkyne, 2 equiv Ag₂CO₃ (oxidant), DMSO, 100 °C, 12 h. Yields 55–80 % for electron‑neutral and electron‑rich arenes; electron‑deficient substrates require more forcing conditions (CuBr·SMe₂, 120 °C). Regioselectivity is dictated by the DG; meta‑selectivity can be achieved with a nitrile‑based DG that enforces a six‑membered cyclometalation.

Ruthenium‑Catalyzed Carboxylate‑Assisted Alkynylation

Ru(II) complexes such as [Ru(p‑cymene)Cl₂]₂ with carboxylate additives (KOAc, NaOAc) enable C–H alkynylation of weakly coordinating arenes (benzene, toluene, xylenes) without a strong DG. The carboxylate acts as an internal base for CMD, forming a ruthenacycle that undergoes alkyne insertion and β‑hydride elimination to release the alkynylated arene. Catalyst loading 2.5 mol %, 1.5 equiv alkyne, 2 equiv Cu(OAc)₂ (oxidant), 1,2‑dichloroethane, 120 °C, 24 h. The reaction tolerates halides, esters, and nitriles. Ortho/para ratios for toluene are ~3:1, reflecting the electronic bias of the ruthenacycle. Mechanistic studies (KIE ≈ 3.2) confirm C–H cleavage as turnover‑limiting.

Photoredox/Nickel Dual Catalysis for Alkynylation

Visible‑light photoredox catalysts (Ir(ppy)₃, 4CzIPN) generate an alkynyl radical from a hypervalent iodine‑alkynyl reagent (e.g., ethynylbenziodoxolone, EBX). The radical adds to a nickel(0) complex (Ni(cod)₂ with a bipyridine ligand) to form a Ni(I)‑alkynyl species. Simultaneously, a C–H bond of an arene or heteroarene is activated via hydrogen‑atom transfer (HAT) from a thiol or quinuclidine co‑catalyst, giving a carbon‑centered radical that couples with the Ni(I)‑alkynyl to a Ni(II) intermediate. Reductive elimination furnishes the C(sp²)–C(sp) bond and regenerates Ni(0). This manifold tolerates unactivated arenes (benzene, fluorobenzene) and delivers meta‑selectivity when a transient directing group (e.g., 2‑pyridylsulfonyl) is employed. Reaction conditions: 1 mol % Ir(ppy)₃, 5 mol % Ni(cod)₂, 10 mol % dtbbpy, 1.2 equiv EBX, 2 equiv HAT catalyst, MeCN, blue LEDs, rt, 16 h. Isolated yields 45–70 %.

Worked Example: Regioselectivity in Cu‑Catalyzed Alkynylation
Predict the major product when 2‑phenylpyridine and 3‑phenylpyridine are subjected to standard Cu(OAc)₂/Ag₂CO₃ oxidative alkynylation with phenylacetylene. Explain the difference in regioselectivity.
2‑Phenylpyridine possesses a pyridyl nitrogen ortho to the phenyl ring, which chelates Cu(II) and directs metalation to the ortho‑C–H of the phenyl group (five‑membered cyclometalation). The product is the ortho‑alkynylated biaryl. 3‑Phenylpyridine lacks a proximal heteroatom on the phenyl ring; the pyridyl nitrogen is too distant to enforce a five‑membered palladacycle. Instead, a six‑membered cyclometalation occurs at the meta‑position of the phenyl ring relative to the pyridyl nitrogen, giving meta‑alkynylated product as the major isomer. The key is the size of the metallacycle: five‑membered (favoured) vs six‑membered (accessible when five‑membered is geometrically impossible).
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks for regioselectivity in directed C–H alkynylation, first identify the directing group and count the atoms to the target C–H. Five‑membered cyclometalation is kinetically favoured; six‑membered is viable only when five‑membered is blocked. Memorise common DGs (picolinamide, 8‑aminoquinoline, pyridyl, pyrimidyl) and their preferred metallacycle sizes. For undirected Ru catalysis, recall that electronic effects dominate (ortho/para for electron‑donating groups).

Catalytic C–H Alkylation via Borrowing Hydrogen: Alcohol Coupling and Transfer Hydrogenation

Borrowing hydrogen (hydrogen autotransfer) couples alcohols with nucleophiles (amines, active methylene compounds, heteroarenes) without pre‑formed alkyl halides. The catalyst dehydrogenates the alcohol to an aldehyde (or ketone), which condenses with the nucleophile to an imine or enamine; the same catalyst then hydrogenates the C=N or C=C bond using the borrowed hydrogen, delivering the alkylated product and water as the only by‑product.

Catalytic Cycle and Key Intermediates

Typical catalysts are Ru(II) complexes (RuCl₂(PPh₃)₃, [Ru(p‑cymene)Cl₂]₂), Ir(I) complexes ([Ir(cod)Cl]₂), Cp*Rh(III) (Cp*RhCl₂)₂, and earth‑abundant Fe(II)/Fe(III) pincer complexes. The cycle: (1) Alcohol coordinates and undergoes β‑hydride elimination to give a metal‑hydride and the carbonyl compound. (2) Nucleophile adds to the carbonyl, forming an imine (with amines) or an enol/enamine (with active methylene). (3) The metal‑hydride delivers hydride to the imine/enamine (transfer hydrogenation), regenerating the active catalyst. The overall stoichiometry: RCH₂OH + H‑Nu → RCH₂‑Nu + H₂O.

Scope and Selectivity

Primary alcohols give linear alkylated amines; secondary alcohols afford branched products. Tertiary alcohols are inert because they cannot form a carbonyl. Chemoselectivity: amines outcompete alcohols for condensation, so N‑alkylation dominates over O‑alkylation. For C‑alkylation, β‑ketoesters, malonates, and indoles act as nucleophiles; the resulting C‑C bond formation is reversible under the reaction conditions, allowing thermodynamic control. Typical conditions: 1–5 mol % catalyst, 1.2 equiv alcohol, 1 equiv nucleophile, toluene or THF, 110–130 °C, 12–24 h. Yields 60–95 %. Water removal (molecular sieves, azeotropic distillation) drives equilibrium forward.

Asymmetric Borrowing Hydrogen

Chiral Ru/Ir catalysts bearing BINAP, SegPhos, or phosphoramidite ligands achieve enantioselective N‑alkylation of amines with racemic secondary alcohols via dynamic kinetic resolution (DKR). The metal‑hydride reduces the imine faster than the alcohol racemises, delivering enantioenriched amines (up to 98 % ee). Recent Fe‑pincer catalysts (Fe(PNP) complexes) operate at 80 °C with 0.1 mol % loading, demonstrating base‑metal viability.

Industrial Relevance

The borrowing‑hydrogen strategy underpins the commercial synthesis of N‑methylamines (e.g., N‑methyldiethanolamine) from methanol and diethanolamine, avoiding formaldehyde handling. It also features in the production of alkylated heterocycles for pharmaceutical intermediates (e.g., N‑alkylated pyrrolidines). Process advantages: atom economy > 90 %, no halogen waste, mild temperatures, and catalyst recycling via biphasic systems.

Photoredox/Nickel Dual Catalysis for C–H Functionalization: Mechanistic Insights and Scope

Merging visible‑light photoredox catalysis with nickel catalysis enables cross‑coupling of C–H bonds with alkyl, aryl, and acyl electrophiles under exceptionally mild conditions. The photoredox catalyst (PC) generates a radical from a precursor (alkyl halide, redox‑active ester, Katritzky salt), while nickel cycles through Ni(0)/Ni(I)/Ni(II)/Ni(III) manifolds to capture the radical and effect C–C or C–heteroatom bond formation at a C–H site.

Catalytic Cycle

1. Photoexcitation of PC (e.g., Ir(ppy)₃, *E₁/₂ = +0.31 V vs SCE) yields a strong reductant (*PC) that reduces an alkyl bromide (Ered ≈ –0.8 V) to an alkyl radical and Br⁻. 2. Ni(0)L₂ (generated in situ from NiCl₂·glyme and a bipyridine ligand) undergoes oxidative addition into an aryl halide or captures the alkyl radical to form Ni(I)‑R. 3. A C–H bond of the substrate (arene, heteroarene, alkane) is activated via hydrogen‑atom transfer (HAT) from a co‑catalyst (thiol, quinuclidine, or a decatungstate anion) to give a carbon‑centered radical. 4. Radical–radical coupling at nickel yields a Ni(II)‑R‑R' intermediate. 5. Reductive elimination furnishes the coupled product and regenerates Ni(0). The PC is regenerated by oxidation of the reduced PC⁻ by the Ni(II) species or by a sacrificial oxidant (e.g., O₂).

Regioselectivity Control

For arenes, regioselectivity is governed by the HAT catalyst: electrophilic HAT (decatungstate) prefers electron‑rich C–H bonds (benzylic, allylic), while nucleophilic HAT (thiyl radicals) favours electron‑deficient positions. Transient directing groups (e.g., 2‑pyridylsulfonyl amides) can override innate selectivity, delivering meta‑ or para‑functionalization. In alkane functionalization, the combination of a selective HAT catalyst (e.g., 2,3‑dichloro‑5,6‑dicyano‑p‑benzoquinone, DDQ) with a nickel catalyst that tolerates steric bulk enables selective functionalization of secondary over primary C–H bonds (selectivity up to 15:1).

Representative Transformations

  • Alkylation of benzene with alkyl bromides: 1 mol % Ir(ppy)₃, 5 mol % NiCl₂·glyme, 10 mol % dtbbpy, 2 equiv HAT (PhSH), MeCN, blue LEDs, rt, 12 h → 55 % yield, 3:1 linear:branched.
  • Arylation of tetrahydrofuran at the α‑position using aryl iodides: 2 mol % 4CzIPN, 5 mol % NiBr₂·diglyme, 10 mol % phenanthroline, 1.5 equiv Na₂CO₃, DMA, 450 nm, 25 °C → 68 % yield.
  • Acylation of heteroarenes (thiophene, furan) with redox‑active esters (NHPI esters): 1 mol % Ir[dF(CF₃)ppy]₂(dtbbpy)PF₆, 5 mol % NiCl₂·glyme, 10 mol % bathophenanthroline, 2 equiv HAT (quinuclidine), MeCN, rt → 60–75 % yield.

Limitations and Emerging Solutions

Catalyst deactivation via nickel black formation limits turnover numbers (TON < 200). Ligand engineering (bulky NHCs, pyridine‑oxazoline) and continuous‑flow operation mitigate this. Competing homocoupling of radicals is suppressed by using a large excess of the C–H substrate (10–20 equiv). Recent advances include the use of organic photoredox catalysts (e.g., 4CzIPN) to avoid precious metals and the development of nickel‑free dual catalysis (Cu/photoredox) for specific substrates.

Continuous‑Flow C–H Functionalization with In‑Line Analytics: Reactor Design, Residence Time Distribution, and Process Safety

Translating batch C–H functionalization to continuous flow improves heat management, enables precise residence‑time control, and facilitates real‑time monitoring — critical for exothermic radical processes and for reactions generating toxic intermediates (e.g., diazo compounds, nitrenes). The key engineering parameters are reactor geometry, mass‑transfer limitations, and analytical feedback loops.

Reactor Types and Hydrodynamics

Packed‑bed reactors (PBR) loaded with heterogeneous catalysts (e.g., Pd/C, Cu‑MOF, zeolite‑encapsulated Rh) provide high catalyst loading and easy separation. For homogeneous catalysis, tubular reactors (inner diameter 0.5–2 mm) operated in laminar flow (Re < 2000) give a narrow residence‑time distribution (RTD) approximated by the axial‑dispersion model (Péclet number Pe > 100). Microreactors with static mixers (herringbone, staggered herringbone) achieve radial mixing in < 10 ms, essential for fast radical reactions (k > 10⁴ M⁻¹ s⁻¹). A typical C–H borylation of arenes using B₂pin₂ and a Ir‑bipyridine catalyst runs at 0.1 M substrate, 5 mol % Ir, 120 °C, 10 bar H₂, residence time 30 min, delivering 92 % conversion with < 2 % protodeborylation.

In‑Line Analytics

FT‑IR probes (diamond ATR) monitor carbonyl stretches of aldehydes/ketones in borrowing‑hydrogen alkylation. UV‑Vis flow cells track the disappearance of a chromophoric directing group (e.g., 2‑pyridyl absorbance at 260 nm). In‑line ¹H NMR (benchtop 60 MHz) provides quantitative conversion and regioisomer ratios for alkynylation reactions. Mass‑spectrometric sampling (ESI‑MS) detects short‑lived radical cations in electrochemical flow cells. Data are fed to a model‑predictive controller that adjusts pump rates or temperature to maintain target conversion.

Process Safety and Scale‑Up

Exothermic radical chain reactions (e.g., decatungstate‑mediated HAT) can exhibit thermal runaway if the heat‑removal capacity (U·A) is insufficient. Flow reactors excel because the high surface‑to‑volume ratio (10⁴ m² m⁻³) enables heat fluxes > 500 kW m⁻². Pressure‑rated tubing (316 SS, Hastelloy) contains volatile by‑products (H₂, CO₂). For reactions generating diazo intermediates, a quench module (triethylphosphine in MeCN) downstream neutralises residual diazo before product isolation. Scale‑out (numbering‑up) rather than scale‑up preserves RTD and mass‑transfer characteristics; a 10 kg day⁻¹ production of a C–H aminated API was achieved with 24 parallel 1 mm ID tubes.

Case Study: Continuous‑Flow C–H Borylation of Arenes

A 0.5 M solution of benzene, B₂pin₂ (1.2 equiv), and [Ir(OMe)(cod)]₂ (0.5 mol %) in cyclohexane is pumped through a 2 mL stainless‑steel coil at 120 °C, 20 bar. In‑line FT‑IR shows complete consumption of B–B stretch (980 cm⁻¹) within 15 min. The effluent passes through a silica plug to remove Ir, then a liquid‑liquid extractor separates the borylated product (phenyl‑Bpin) in 88 % isolated yield. The process runs for 200 h without catalyst leaching (< 5 ppm Ir in product).

  • Assuming that a single directing group works for all substrate classes; steric and electronic mismatch often leads to no reaction or wrong regioisomer.
  • Confusing the polarity of HAT catalysts: decatungstate is electrophilic (prefers electron‑rich C–H), thiyl radicals are nucleophilic (prefers electron‑deficient C–H).
  • Neglecting mass‑transport limitations in flow electrolysis; operating above the limiting current causes uncontrolled radical dimerisation and drops faradaic efficiency.
  • Treating supramolecular cages as universally applicable; substrate size must match the cavity dimensions, otherwise no rate acceleration occurs.
  • Overlooking that cage degradation (ligand oxidation, metal leaching) often limits turnover numbers far below those of homogeneous analogues.
  • Believing that a single set of electrolysis parameters (voltage, flow rate) can be transferred across different substrates without re‑optimising the diffusion layer.
  • Ignoring the need for a sacrificial oxidant or mediator in electrochemical C–H amination; direct anodic oxidation of the amine often leads to polymerisation.

These advanced topics integrate mechanistic insight, quantitative process parameters, and the practical constraints that examiners target in high‑level competitive chemistry papers.

Catalytic C–H Alkylation via Cross‑Electrophile Coupling

Cross‑electrophile coupling (XEC) has emerged as a powerful strategy to forge C–C bonds between two distinct electrophilic partners without pre‑formed organometallic nucleophiles. In the context of hydrocarbon functionalization, nickel or cobalt catalysts mediate the coupling of an alkyl (pseudo)halide with an aryl or vinyl (pseudo)halide, delivering alkylated arenes or alkenes under mild, often redox‑neutral conditions. The catalytic cycle typically involves oxidative addition of the aryl halide to a Ni(0) species to give an aryl‑Ni(II) intermediate, followed by single‑electron reduction of the alkyl halide to generate an alkyl radical. Radical capture by the aryl‑Ni(II) complex forms a Ni(III) species that undergoes reductive elimination to release the cross‑coupled product and regenerate Ni(I). A stoichiometric reductant such as manganese, zinc, or a sacrificial anode in electrochemical variants closes the catalytic loop by reducing Ni(I) back to Ni(0).

Key selectivity determinants include the relative rates of oxidative addition versus radical generation, the stability of the alkyl radical (primary > secondary > tertiary for capture), and the ligand environment on nickel. Bidentate nitrogen ligands (e.g., 2,2′‑bipyridine, phenanthroline) and N‑heterocyclic carbenes have been shown to accelerate oxidative addition while suppressing β‑hydride elimination from the alkyl‑Ni intermediate. For unactivated alkyl chlorides, the addition of a Lewis acidic additive (MgCl₂, ZnCl₂) can facilitate halogen atom abstraction, improving radical formation. Recent developments employ photoredox/nickel dual catalysis, where an iridium or organic photocatalyst generates the alkyl radical under visible light, allowing the use of redox‑active esters (NHPI esters) as alkyl precursors. This expands the substrate scope to include carboxylic acids derived from biomass.

Industrial relevance is growing: a pilot‑scale continuous‑flow XEC process for the synthesis of a key pharmaceutical intermediate (aryl‑alkyl coupling) demonstrated 85 % isolated yield, 99 % regioselectivity, and a catalyst loading of 0.5 mol % Ni with a residence time of 12 min at 60 °C. The process leverages a packed‑bed reactor containing a heterogeneous Ni‑NHC catalyst, enabling catalyst recycling over 20 cycles with <5 % activity loss. Process safety is enhanced because the radical concentration remains low in the flow regime, mitigating runaway polymerization.

Steam Cracking Kinetics, Reactor Modeling, and Product Distribution

Steam cracking remains the primary route to light olefins (ethylene, propylene) and aromatics (benzene, toluene, xylenes). The reaction network comprises >10⁴ elementary steps, but a reduced kinetic model based on the “lumped‑species” approach captures the essential chemistry for reactor design. Feedstock (naphtha, gas oil, ethane) is diluted with steam (steam‑to‑hydrocarbon ratio 0.3–0.6) and heated to 800–880 °C in tubular reactors (radiant coils) with residence times of 0.1–0.5 s. The primary pyrolysis reactions are free‑radical chain mechanisms: initiation by C–C bond homolysis, propagation via β‑scission of alkyl radicals, hydrogen abstraction, and radical addition to double bonds, and termination by radical recombination or disproportionation.

Key kinetic parameters include the activation energy for C–C bond cleavage (~350 kJ mol⁻¹ for n‑alkanes) and the pre‑exponential factor (~10¹⁶ s⁻¹). The rate of ethylene formation from n‑hexane can be approximated by a first‑order dependence on hydrocarbon partial pressure and an Arrhenius expression k = 1.2×10¹⁶ exp(–350 000/RT) s⁻¹. Coke formation, a major operational constraint, follows a parallel pathway: polycyclic aromatic radicals condense on the coil inner surface, with a rate proportional to the square of the concentration of C₆⁺ aromatics. Reactor models couple 1‑D plug‑flow energy balances (including radiative heat flux from burners) with the kinetic ODE system. Commercial simulators (e.g., Aspen Plus, gPROMS) use these models to optimize coil outlet temperature (COT), steam dilution, and pressure drop to maximize ethylene yield while limiting coke to <0.5 wt % of feed.

Product distribution is highly sensitive to COT: a 10 °C increase raises ethylene yield by ~1.5 % (absolute) but also accelerates coke formation exponentially. Advanced control strategies employ real‑time infrared pyrometry and on‑line gas chromatography to adjust furnace firing rates, maintaining COT within ±2 °C. Recent pilot plants test “short‑contact‑time” reactors (micro‑channel or rotating‑disk) that achieve residence times <0.05 s, suppressing secondary reactions and boosting propylene selectivity by 3–5 % relative to conventional coils.

Mechanistic Diagnosis via Kinetic Isotope Effect
A steam cracking experiment compares the pyrolysis of n‑hexane‑d₁₄ (fully deuterated) with n‑hexane‑h₁₄ at 850 °C, 0.2 s residence time. The observed rate constant ratio k_H/k_D = 2.8. What does this primary kinetic isotope effect indicate about the rate‑determining step?
A primary KIE of ~2.8 signifies that C–H (or C–D) bond cleavage occurs in the rate‑determining step. In steam cracking, the initiation step is homolytic C–C bond scission, which does not involve C–H cleavage. However, the propagation step of hydrogen abstraction by an alkyl radical (R· + RH → RH + R·) does break a C–H bond. The measured KIE therefore implicates hydrogen abstraction as the kinetically significant propagation step under these conditions, consistent with literature values of 2–3 for radical H‑abstraction from alkanes at high temperature.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a question asks to interpret a kinetic isotope effect, first identify whether the isotope is on a bond that breaks in the proposed elementary step. A primary KIE > 2 points to that bond’s cleavage in the rate‑determining step; a secondary KIE (≈1.1–1.3) suggests a change in hybridisation without bond rupture. Always state the mechanistic implication explicitly.

Environmental Fate, Toxicology, and Remediation of Polycyclic Aromatic Hydrocarbons

Polycyclic aromatic hydrocarbons (PAHs) are ubiquitous environmental contaminants formed during incomplete combustion of fossil fuels, biomass, and industrial processes. The US EPA lists 16 priority PAHs, ranging from naphthalene (two rings) to benzo[g,h,i]perylene (six rings). Their persistence stems from high hydrophobicity (log K_ow 3.3–6.5), low volatility, and strong sorption to organic carbon in soils and sediments. In aquatic systems, PAHs partition to suspended particles and settle, creating long‑term reservoirs. Photodegradation, microbial metabolism, and chemical oxidation are the principal natural attenuation pathways. Direct photolysis is efficient for low‑molecular‑weight PAHs (e.g., anthracene) but negligible for high‑molecular‑weight congeners due to rapid internal conversion. Microbial degradation proceeds via dioxygenase‑mediated dihydrodiol formation, followed by ring cleavage; however, the rate drops sharply beyond four rings because of steric hindrance and reduced bioavailability.

Toxicological concern centers on the bay‑region diol‑epoxide metabolites of benzo[a]pyrene (BaP) and dibenz[a,h]anthracene, which form covalent DNA adducts leading to mutagenicity and carcinogenicity. The toxic equivalency factor (TEF) approach normalizes the potency of each PAH to BaP (TEF = 1). For risk assessment, the benzo[a]pyrene equivalent concentration (BaPeq) is calculated as Σ(C_i × TEF_i). Regulatory limits for BaP in drinking water are 0.01 µg L⁻¹ (EU) and 0.2 µg L⁻¹ (US EPA).

Remediation technologies include: (1) Enhanced bioremediation — biostimulation with nitrogen/phosphorus and bioaugmentation with PAH‑degrading consortia (e.g., Mycobacterium, Sphingomonas) can achieve >90 % removal of 2–3 ring PAHs in 60 days under aerobic conditions; (2) Advanced oxidation processes (AOPs) — UV/H₂O₂, ozone, or Fenton reagents generate hydroxyl radicals that attack aromatic rings, effective for dissolved PAHs but limited by soil matrix scavenging; (3) Thermal desorption — ex‑situ heating to 300–500 °C volatilizes PAHs for subsequent catalytic oxidation, achieving >99 % destruction but at high energy cost; (4) Sorbent‑based sequestration — activated carbon, biochar, or organoclays immobilize PAHs, reducing bioavailability; this is a containment rather than destruction strategy. Emerging research explores nano‑zero‑valent iron (nZVI) coupled with persulfate activation for in‑situ chemical oxidation, showing 80 % BaP degradation in 48 h at neutral pH.

Catalytic Conversion of Carbon Dioxide to Light Hydrocarbons: Sabatier and Reverse Water‑Gas Shift

Utilizing CO₂ as a carbon feedstock for hydrocarbon synthesis addresses both greenhouse‑gas mitigation and chemical production. Two principal catalytic routes dominate: the Sabatier reaction (CO₂ + 4 H₂ → CH₄ + 2 H₂O, ΔH° = –165 kJ mol⁻¹) and the reverse water‑gas shift (RWGS, CO₂ + H₂ → CO + H₂O, ΔH° = +41 kJ mol⁻¹) followed by Fischer‑Tropsch synthesis (FTS) to higher hydrocarbons. The Sabatier process is thermodynamically favored at low temperature (200–300 °C) and high pressure (10–30 bar). Industrial catalysts are Ru/Al₂O₃ or Ni/Al₂O₃; Ru offers higher activity at lower temperature but Ni is cheaper. Typical Ni catalysts achieve 85 % CO₂ conversion at 300 °C, 20 bar, GHSV 10 000 h⁻¹ with >99 % CH₄ selectivity. Catalyst deactivation arises from sintering of Ni particles and carbon deposition (Boudouard reaction) at >350 °C; periodic oxidative regeneration restores activity.

RWGS operates at 500–700 °C to overcome the endothermic barrier. Cu‑based catalysts (Cu/ZnO/Al₂O₃) are standard for methanol synthesis but show modest RWGS activity; perovskite oxides (La₀.₈Sr₀.₂FeO₃) and CeO₂‑supported Pt exhibit higher CO selectivity (>90 %) at 600 °C. The produced syngas (H₂/CO ≈ 1) can be fed directly to an FTS reactor (Co or Fe catalyst) to generate C₂⁺ olefins and paraffins. Process integration studies indicate that coupling RWGS‑FTS with renewable H₂ (electrolysis) yields a carbon‑negative diesel substitute when the CO₂ source is biogenic. Techno‑economic analyses estimate a minimum selling price of $1.2 L⁻¹ for synthetic diesel at $2 kg⁻¹ H₂, highlighting the critical role of low‑cost green hydrogen.

Recent advances explore tandem catalysis: a bifunctional catalyst comprising Ni nanoparticles for Sabatier and Fe‑K for FTS in a single reactor can convert CO₂ directly to C₂⁺ hydrocarbons (C₂–C₄ selectivity 45 %) at 300 °C, 30 bar, bypassing separate RWGS. In‑situ DRIFTS reveals that surface formate intermediates on Ni migrate to Fe sites for chain growth. Stability tests show <10 % activity loss over 200 h, with coke formation mitigated by the high H₂/CO₂ ratio (4:1).

Metal‑Organic Framework Mediated C–H Activation: Confinement Effects and Selectivity

Metal‑organic frameworks (MOFs) provide crystalline, porous architectures where catalytic sites (open metal nodes or encapsulated complexes) operate within well‑defined cavities. This confinement alters both thermodynamics and kinetics of C–H activation. For example, Zr‑based MOF‑808 functionalized with a mononuclear Rh(I) complex catalyzes the borylation of arenes with B₂pin₂. The pore aperture (6 Å) excludes substrates larger than biphenyl, delivering perfect size‑selectivity. Turnover frequencies (TOFs) inside the MOF are 3–5× higher than the homogeneous analogue because the local concentration of substrate near the active site is enhanced by adsorption (Langmuir isotherm K_ads ≈ 150 M⁻¹). Moreover, the rigid framework suppresses catalyst decomposition pathways such as Rh–Rh dimerization, extending catalyst lifetime to >10⁴ turnovers.

Confinement also steers regioselectivity in alkane C–H oxidation. A Fe‑MOF (MIL‑100(Fe)) with exposed Fe(III) sites activates peracetic acid to generate high‑valent Fe(V)=O species. Oxidation of n‑octane yields a 2‑octanol/3‑octanol ratio of 4:1, whereas the homogeneous Fe(V)=O complex gives a statistical 1:1 ratio. The preferential secondary C–H oxidation arises from the preferential orientation of the linear alkane within the 1‑nm hexagonal channels, positioning the C‑2 and C‑3 bonds closer to the oxidant. Kinetic isotope effect measurements (k_H/k_D = 3.2) confirm a hydrogen‑atom‑transfer mechanism.

Challenges include framework stability under oxidative or aqueous conditions, mass‑transport limitations at high substrate loading, and difficulty in characterizing the active site under operando conditions. Recent strategies employ post‑synthetic metalation of robust Zr‑MOFs (UiO‑66, UiO‑67) with cationic Ir(III) complexes for C–H borylation, achieving >95 % crystallinity retention after 50 cycles. Computational modeling (DFT on cluster models) predicts that the activation barrier for C–H cleavage drops by 8–12 kJ mol⁻¹ inside the pore relative to the gas phase, quantifying the confinement effect.

  • Assuming that a single cross‑electrophile coupling protocol works for all alkyl halide classes; β‑branched substrates often require tailored ligands or additives.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of coke kinetics in steam cracking models; a 10 °C error in COT can double coke formation rate.
  • Treating all PAHs as equally bioavailable; high‑molecular‑weight PAHs are strongly sequestered in soil organic matter and resist microbial attack.
  • Overlooking the water‑gas‑shift equilibrium when designing RWGS‑FTS integration; excess water can shift the equilibrium back to CO₂, lowering CO yield.
  • Believing that MOF pore size alone dictates selectivity; substrate shape, polarity, and competitive adsorption also govern access to active sites.
  • Ignoring catalyst sintering in Sabatier reactors at >350 °C; periodic oxidative regeneration is essential for long‑term operation.
  • Confusing primary and secondary kinetic isotope effects; a KIE ≈ 1.2 indicates a change in hybridisation, not bond cleavage.

These advanced topics integrate mechanistic insight, quantitative process parameters, and the practical constraints that examiners target in high‑level competitive chemistry papers.

Catalytic C–H Activation in Continuous Flow Reactors: Heat Management, Residence Time Control, and Scale‑Up

Continuous flow reactors have become the platform of choice for translating laboratory C–H functionalization protocols to multi‑kilogram scale because they decouple heat removal from reaction volume. In a packed‑bed flow reactor the catalyst (often a heterogeneous metal on silica, alumina, or a metal‑organic framework) is immobilised, eliminating catalyst separation steps and enabling precise temperature profiling along the axial coordinate. For exothermic C–H oxidation of n‑hexane to 2‑hexanone using a Cu‑exchanged zeolite (Cu‑ZSM‑5) the adiabatic temperature rise in a 10 mm internal‑diameter tube at 0.5 mL min⁻¹ feed is ≈ 45 °C; a co‑current coolant jacket held at 120 °C limits the hotspot to < 165 °C, preserving selectivity (> 85 % to the ketone) and suppressing over‑oxidation to CO₂. Residence time distribution (RTD) measurements with a non‑reactive tracer (acetone in He) show a variance σ²/τ² ≈ 0.02, confirming near‑plug‑flow behaviour that is essential for reactions where product inhibition occurs (e.g., Rh‑catalysed C–H borylation of arenes where the borylated product coordinates to Rh and slows turnover). By segmenting the bed into three temperature zones (120 °C, 140 °C, 160 °C) the effective residence time for the rate‑determining C–H cleavage step can be increased without extending the overall reactor length, delivering a 2.3‑fold productivity gain versus a single‑zone isothermal run. Scale‑up follows the constant‑space‑velocity (SV) rule: a 10‑fold increase in tube diameter (from 10 mm to 100 mm) requires a proportional increase in linear velocity to maintain the same Reynolds number (Re ≈ 200, laminar) and heat‑transfer coefficient (h ≈ 1.2 kW m⁻² K⁻¹). Pilot‑plant data for the Cu‑ZSM‑5 system at 5 kg day⁻¹ feed show < 5 % catalyst deactivation over 200 h on stream when a periodic oxidative regeneration pulse (5 % O₂ in N₂, 300 °C, 30 min every 24 h) is applied, confirming that flow operation mitigates coke accumulation by continuously sweeping away heavy oligomers.

Photoredox/Nickel Dual Catalysis for Cross‑Electrophile Coupling of Alkyl Halides with Arenes

The merger of visible‑light photoredox catalysis (Ir(ppy)₃, λₘₐₓ = 450 nm, E₁/₂* = +0.31 V vs SCE) with Ni(0)/Ni(II) cycles enables the coupling of unactivated alkyl bromides (e.g., 1‑bromooctane) with electron‑deficient arenes (e.g., 4‑CF₃‑C₆H₄‑Br) under mild conditions (25 °C, blue LEDs, DMA solvent). The photoredox catalyst generates a radical anion of the aryl bromide via single‑electron reduction (E₁/₂ ≈ –1.8 V), which fragments to give an aryl radical that adds to Ni(0)L₂ (L = dtbbpy) forming an aryl‑Ni(I) intermediate. Simultaneously, the excited Ir* oxidises a sacrificial amine (DIPEA, Eₒₓ = +0.78 V) to its radical cation, which abstracts a bromine atom from the alkyl bromide, producing the alkyl radical. This alkyl radical undergoes rapid capture by the aryl‑Ni(I) species to give a Ni(II) diaryl‑alkyl complex that reductively eliminates the cross‑coupled product (aryl‑alkyl) and regenerates Ni(0). Key selectivity determinants: (i) the reduction potential of the aryl bromide must be more positive than the excited‑state reduction potential of Ir* to avoid direct reduction of the alkyl bromide; (ii) the Ni ligand bite angle (dtbbpy ≈ 85°) favours reductive elimination over β‑hydride elimination, suppressing alkene side‑products; (iii) the concentration of DIPEA (0.5 M) balances radical generation against Ni catalyst decomposition via amine coordination. Reported turnover numbers (TON) for the Ni catalyst reach 1 200 with 0.5 mol % Ni, 1 mol % Ir, delivering isolated yields of 78 % for the model coupling. Mechanistic studies using time‑resolved IR spectroscopy confirm the Ni(I)–aryl intermediate (ν(CO) ≈ 1970 cm⁻¹) and show that the rate‑determining step shifts from radical generation (at low light intensity) to reductive elimination (at high light intensity), a nuance that examiners often probe via kinetic isotope effect (KIE) experiments on α‑deuterated alkyl bromides (k_H/k_D ≈ 1.1, indicating C–Br cleavage is not rate‑limiting).

Mechanistic Diagnosis Question
In the photoredox/Ni dual catalytic cross‑electrophile coupling described above, a student observes that replacing the alkyl bromide with the corresponding α‑deuterated alkyl bromide (R‑CD₂‑Br) does not change the reaction rate (k_H/k_D ≈ 1.0). However, when the aryl bromide is replaced by its deuterated analogue at the ipso‑position (Ar‑D), a primary KIE of 2.8 is measured. Which step is rate‑determining under the standard conditions, and why does the α‑deuterium substitution not affect the rate?
The primary KIE on the aryl bromide indicates that C–Br bond cleavage of the aryl electrophile (single‑electron reduction and fragmentation to the aryl radical) is the rate‑determining step. The α‑deuterium substitution on the alkyl bromide probes C–H(D) bond cleavage at the α‑position, which occurs only during β‑hydride elimination from a Ni‑alkyl intermediate — a pathway that is not operative because the Ni(II) diaryl‑alkyl complex undergoes rapid reductive elimination. Therefore, the alkyl radical formation (via H‑atom abstraction from DIPEA·⁺) and subsequent capture by Ni are faster than the aryl radical generation, making the aryl C–Br activation the bottleneck.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a dual‑catalysis mechanism is presented, first identify the two catalytic cycles and the electron‑transfer events that link them. Write the half‑reactions with potentials; the step with the largest thermodynamic barrier (most negative reduction potential or most positive oxidation potential) is often rate‑determining. Use KIE data to confirm which bond is broken in the turnover‑limiting step — primary KIE > 2 signals C–H/D cleavage, while KIE ≈ 1 points to a non‑bond‑breaking event such as electron transfer or ligand exchange.

Catalytic Hydrogenolysis of Polyolefins to Liquid Fuels and Chemicals: Mechanism, Catalyst Design, and Process Economics

Polyethylene (PE) and polypropylene (PP) waste streams can be converted to narrow‑distribution diesel‑range alkanes (C₁₀–C₂₀) and valuable waxes via catalytic hydrogenolysis over bifunctional Pt/WOₓ‑ZrO₂ or Ru/TiO₂ catalysts. The reaction proceeds through a “chain‑walking” mechanism: adsorbed polymer chains undergo repeated β‑hydride elimination and re‑insertion at the metal site, effectively moving the active metal centre along the chain until a terminal C–C bond is positioned for hydrogenolytic cleavage. On Pt/WOₓ‑ZrO₂ (Pt = 0.5 wt %, WOₓ = 10 wt %) at 250 °C, 30 bar H₂, the turnover frequency (TOF) for PE (Mₙ ≈ 120 kg mol⁻¹) is 0.12 s⁻¹ per surface Pt atom, delivering a C₁₀–C₂₀ selectivity of 72 % with a Schulz‑Flory α ≈ 0.85. The acidic WOₓ sites promote isomerisation of internal olefins formed during chain walking, suppressing cyclisation and aromatization that would otherwise lower diesel yield. Catalyst stability is limited by coke formation from dehydrogenation of adsorbed oligomers; a periodic oxidative regeneration (1 % O₂ in He, 400 °C, 1 h every 50 h) restores > 95 % activity over 500 h. Process economics hinge on the hydrogen consumption (≈ 0.04 kg H₂ kg⁻¹ polymer) and the value of the product slate: at a crude oil price of $70 bbl⁻¹, the minimum selling price (MSP) for the diesel fraction is $0.68 L⁻¹, assuming a 10 kt yr⁻¹ plant with 85 % on‑stream factor. Sensitivity analysis shows that a 20 % reduction in catalyst cost (from $120 kg⁻¹ to $96 kg⁻¹) lowers MSP by $0.04 L⁻¹, while a 10 % increase in H₂ price ($2 kg⁻¹ → $2.2 kg⁻¹) raises MSP by $0.03 L⁻¹. Integration with a refinery hydrocracker allows the hydrogenolysis effluent to be co‑processed, eliminating a separate distillation train and improving overall carbon efficiency to > 90 %.

Electrochemical C–H Functionalization of Hydrocarbons: Anodic Oxidation, Mediated Pathways, and Cell Design

Direct anodic oxidation of simple alkanes (e.g., n‑butane) on a boron‑doped diamond (BDD) anode in acetonitrile/0.1 M Bu₄NBF₄ requires potentials > +2.8 V vs Ag/AgCl, leading to uncontrolled radical cation fragmentation and low faradaic efficiency (< 15 %). Mediated electrolysis circumvent this by employing a redox mediator such as N‑hydroxyphthalimide (NHPI, E₁/₂ = +1.2 V) which is oxidised to the phthalimide‑N‑oxyl (PINO) radical; PINO abstracts a hydrogen atom from the alkane (BDE ≈ 410 kJ mol⁻¹) generating a carbon‑centred radical that couples with a nucleophile (e.g., acetate) to give the functionalised product (butyl acetate). In a divided flow cell (cation‑exchange membrane, Nafion 117) with a Pt cathode (H₂ evolution) and a BDD anode, operating at 5 mA cm⁻², 25 °C, 0.2 M n‑butane, 0.05 M NHPI, 0.5 M AcONa, the faradaic efficiency for butyl acetate reaches 68 % with a current density of 10 mA cm⁻². The cell voltage is 3.2 V, giving an energy consumption of 185 kWh kg⁻¹ product. Key design variables: (i) mediator concentration — excess NHPI (> 0.1 M) leads to mediator dimerisation and loss of activity; (ii) flow rate — a residence time of 120 s in the anodic compartment ensures > 90 % conversion of the mediator per pass; (iii) membrane selection — anion‑exchange membranes (AEM) reduce crossover of acetate, improving coulombic efficiency by 12 %. Recent advances use a heterogeneous mediator (TEMPO‑functionalised carbon nanotubes) immobilised on the anode surface, eliminating mediator loss and enabling continuous operation for > 200 h with < 5 % decay in selectivity. Scale‑up to a 1 m² electrode area stack (10 cells in series) is projected to produce 50 kg day⁻¹ of butyl acetate at an electricity cost of $0.12 kWh⁻¹, competitive with conventional oxidative esterification when renewable power is used.

Catalytic C–H Activation in the Upgrading of Biomass‑Derived Furans to Aromatic Hydrocarbons

Furfural and 5‑hydroxymethylfurfural (HMF), obtained from lignocellulosic biomass dehydration, serve as renewable platforms for benzene, toluene, and xylenes (BTX) via catalytic C–H activation and deoxygenation. A tandem sequence — (1) Pd/C‑catalysed transfer hydrogenation of furfural to 2‑methylfuran (2‑MF) using isopropanol as H‑donor (80 °C, 4 h, 95 % yield), followed by (2) H‑ZSM‑5 (Si/Al = 25) catalysed Diels‑Alder cycloaddition of 2‑MF with ethylene (generated in‑situ from ethanol dehydration over γ‑Al₂O₃, 300 °C) to give a bicyclic adduct, and (3) subsequent dehydrogenation over Pt/SiO₂ (250 °C, 1 bar H₂) to aromatise to p‑xylene — achieves an overall carbon yield of 48 % from furfural to p‑xylene. The rate‑determining step is the Diels‑Alder cycloaddition (Eₐ ≈ 115 kJ mol⁻¹), which is accelerated by the confined pores of H‑ZSM‑5 that pre‑organise the diene and dienophile; kinetic modelling shows a 3.5‑fold rate enhancement versus the homogeneous reaction in toluene. Catalyst deactivation arises from coke deposition on the Brønsted acid sites; a mild oxidative regeneration (2 % O₂, 450 °C, 30 min) restores > 90 % activity. Process integration with a biorefinery that supplies ethanol and furfural from the same feedstock reduces the minimum selling price of renewable p‑xylene to $1.15 kg⁻¹ at a plant capacity of 50 kt yr⁻¹, assuming a furfural cost of $0.80 kg⁻¹ and electricity at $0.06 kWh⁻¹. Life‑cycle assessment indicates a 62 % reduction in greenhouse‑gas emissions compared to fossil‑derived p‑xylene when the hydrogen for the final dehydrogenation step is sourced from biomass gasification.

  • Assuming that a single cross‑electrophile coupling protocol works for all alkyl halide classes; β‑branched substrates often require tailored ligands or additives.
  • Neglecting the temperature dependence of coke kinetics in steam cracking models; a 10 °C error in COT can double coke formation rate.
  • Treating all PAHs as equally bioavailable; high‑molecular‑weight PAHs are strongly sequestered in soil organic matter and resist microbial attack.
  • Overlooking the water‑gas‑shift equilibrium when designing RWGS‑FTS integration; excess water can shift the equilibrium back to CO₂, lowering CO yield.
  • Believing that MOF pore size alone dictates selectivity; substrate shape, polarity, and competitive adsorption also govern access to active sites.
  • Ignoring catalyst sintering in Sabatier reactors at > 350 °C; periodic oxidative regeneration is essential for long‑term operation.
  • Confusing primary and secondary kinetic isotope effects; a KIE ≈ 1.2 indicates a change in hybridisation, not bond cleavage.

Catalytic C–H Alkylation via Hydrogen Atom Transfer (HAT) Catalysis: Mechanisms, Catalysts, and Scope

Hydrogen atom transfer (HAT) catalysis enables the functionalization of strong, non‑polar C–H bonds by abstracting a hydrogen atom to generate a carbon‑centered radical. The key step is the reaction of a high‑energy radical or excited‑state species (the HAT catalyst) with a substrate C–H bond, producing a substrate radical and a reduced catalyst. The substrate radical then undergoes radical addition to an alkene, radical–radical coupling, or trapping by a suitable electrophile. Because HAT is governed by bond‑dissociation energies (BDEs) and polarity matching, it can discriminate between primary, secondary, and tertiary C–H bonds, and even between electronically distinct positions in complex molecules.

Common HAT catalysts fall into three categories. First, photoexcited transition‑metal complexes such as Ir(ppy)₃ or Ru(bpy)₃²⁺ generate a powerful oxidizing excited state that can abstract hydrogen from relatively weak C–H bonds (BDE ≤ 95 kcal mol⁻¹). Second, organic radicals derived from N‑hydroxyphthalimide esters (NHPI esters) or from thiyl radicals (generated from thiols under light or thermal initiation) effect HAT from stronger C–H bonds (BDE up to 105 kcal mol⁻¹). Third, high‑valent metal‑oxo species (e.g., Fe⁽ᴵⱽ⁾=O, Mn⁽ⱽ⁾=O) produced in situ from peroxides perform HAT via a concerted proton‑coupled electron transfer (PCET) pathway. The choice of catalyst dictates the operational conditions: photoredox HAT typically runs at room temperature under blue‑LED irradiation, while thermal HAT with NHPI esters often requires 80–120 °C and a peroxide initiator.

Selectivity in HAT alkylation is controlled by three factors. (1) BDE hierarchy: weaker C–H bonds (allylic, benzylic, α‑to‑heteroatom) react faster. (2) Polarity matching: electrophilic radicals (e.g., alkoxy, acyl) prefer electron‑rich C–H bonds, whereas nucleophilic radicals (e.g., alkyl) favor electron‑deficient sites. (3) Steric accessibility: bulky catalysts or substrates can shield certain positions, enabling remote functionalization. For example, decatungstate (TBADT) under UV light selectively abstracts hydrogen from the most accessible secondary C–H in n‑alkanes, giving a radical that adds to acrylates to afford linear alkylated products. In contrast, a chiral thiol catalyst can induce enantioselectivity in the HAT step when paired with a chiral Lewis acid that coordinates the alkene trap.

Synthetic scope extends to late‑stage functionalization of pharmaceuticals and natural products. A notable example is the C‑3 alkylation of a steroid scaffold using a photoredox/Ir catalyst and a Michael acceptor, where the tertiary C–H at C‑3 is preferentially abstracted over secondary sites due to its lower BDE and favorable radical stability. Industrial relevance is emerging in the upgrading of petroleum fractions: HAT‑mediated alkylation of light alkanes with olefins can produce branched alkanes for high‑octane gasoline components without the need for strong acid catalysts.

Catalytic C–H Alkynylation via Copper and Ruthenium Catalysis: Regioselectivity and Applications

C–H alkynylation installs a terminal alkyne moiety directly onto an sp² or sp³ carbon, providing a versatile handle for further transformations (click chemistry, Sonogashira coupling, cycloadditions). Copper catalysis dominates the field for heteroarene and simple arene alkynylation, while ruthenium catalysis enables directed ortho‑alkynylation of arenes through carboxylate‑assisted C–H activation.

In copper‑catalyzed alkynylation, a Cu(I) salt (CuOTf, CuI) is oxidized in situ by an external oxidant (e.g., (NH₄)₂S₂O₈, O₂, or electrochemical anodic oxidation) to a Cu(II)–acetylide species. The Cu(II)–acetylide undergoes concerted metalation‑deprotonation (CMD) with the arene C–H bond, often assisted by a directing group (DG) such as pyridine, amide, or ester. The resulting Cu(III) intermediate reductively eliminates to forge the C–C bond and regenerate Cu(I). Regioselectivity is dictated by the DG: pyridine directs ortho, while a carbonyl‑based DG can direct meta via a relay strategy. For undirected arenes, the reaction favors the most electron‑rich position, but yields are modest without a DG.

Ruthenium catalysis employs Ru(II) complexes such as RuCl₂(p‑cymene)₂ or Ru(OAc)₂(p‑cymene) with a carboxylate co‑catalyst (e.g., KOAc). The carboxylate acts as an internal base in a CMD step, enabling ortho‑alkynylation of arenes bearing a weakly coordinating DG (e.g., ester, ketone). The Ru(II)/Ru(IV) cycle proceeds via oxidative addition of the C–H bond to Ru(II), forming a Ru(IV)–aryl intermediate that undergoes transmetalation with a copper acetylide (generated in situ from the terminal alkyne and Cu(I)) and reductive elimination to give the alkynylated product. This dual‑metal system tolerates a broad range of functional groups and delivers high ortho selectivity even on sterically hindered substrates.

Applications include the synthesis of alkynylated natural products (e.g., the C‑7 alkynylation of indole alkaloids) and the preparation of conjugated polymers where alkynyl‑aryl linkages provide rigid, linear backbones. In medicinal chemistry, copper‑catalyzed alkynylation of heterocycles (pyridines, quinolines) enables rapid SAR exploration via click‑chemistry diversification. A practical limitation is the requirement for stoichiometric oxidants in copper systems; electrochemical oxidation offers a greener alternative by generating Cu(II) at the anode, eliminating chemical waste.

Technique: Predicting Regioselectivity in Copper‑Catalyzed C–H Alkynylation
A substrate 2‑phenylpyridine is subjected to CuOTf (10 mol %), phenylacetylene (1.5 eq), (NH₄)₂S₂O₈ (2 eq), and Cs₂CO₃ (2 eq) in DCE at 80 °C. Which carbon of the phenyl ring undergoes alkynylation, and why?
The pyridine nitrogen coordinates to Cu(I), directing the CMD step to the ortho position of the phenyl ring relative to the pyridine linkage (C‑2′). The resulting five‑membered palladacycle‑like copper intermediate is favored over a six‑membered alternative. Therefore, alkynylation occurs at the ortho carbon of the phenyl ring adjacent to the pyridine, giving 2‑(2‑phenylethynyl)pyridine as the major product.
Exam‑Day Strategy
When a C–H functionalization question appears, first identify the metal catalyst and the likely activation mode (CMD, oxidative addition, HAT). Then locate any directing groups on the substrate; they override innate electronic preferences. Sketch the metallacycle size (five‑ vs six‑membered) to predict the regiochemical outcome quickly.

Metal‑Free Organocatalytic C–H Functionalization: Enamine, Iminium, and Radical Pathways

Organocatalysis provides metal‑free alternatives for C–H functionalization, leveraging the reactivity of enamines, iminium ions, and organic radicals generated under mild conditions. Enamine catalysis activates carbonyl compounds (aldehydes, ketones) at the α‑position: a secondary amine condenses with the carbonyl to form an enamine, which acts as a nucleophile toward electrophiles (e.g., alkyl halides, Michael acceptors). The resulting iminium intermediate is hydrolyzed to regenerate the catalyst and deliver the α‑functionalized carbonyl. This mode is widely used for α‑alkylation, α‑amination, and α‑oxygenation of aldehydes and ketones.

Iminium catalysis operates on α,β‑unsaturated aldehydes or ketones. The amine catalyst forms an iminium ion with the carbonyl, lowering the LUMO of the β‑carbon and enabling nucleophilic attack at the β‑position (Michael addition) or cycloaddition reactions. Chiral secondary amines (e.g., MacMillan’s imidazolidinone) induce high enantioselectivity in these transformations. The iminium is subsequently hydrolyzed to release the product and the free amine.

Radical organocatalysis has emerged through visible‑light photoredox organocatalysts such as eosin Y, rose bengal, or acridinium salts. These dyes absorb visible light, reach an excited state that can undergo single‑electron transfer (SET) with a substrate or a sacrificial reagent, generating radical ions. For C–H functionalization, a common strategy is hydrogen atom transfer (HAT) from a thiol co‑catalyst (e.g., thiophenol) to the substrate, producing a carbon radical that adds to an alkene or undergoes radical–radical coupling. The thiyl radical is regenerated by SET from the excited photocatalyst, closing the catalytic cycle. This metal‑free HAT manifold tolerates a wide range of functional groups and operates at ambient temperature.

Scope and limitations: enamine/iminium catalysis is restricted to carbonyl‑containing substrates and generally requires an α‑hydrogen. Radical organocatalysis can address unactivated alkanes but often suffers from low site‑selectivity unless a directing group or polarity‑matching strategy is employed. Recent advances combine enamine catalysis with photoredox to achieve α‑functionalization of aldehydes via radical addition to enamine‑derived radicals, merging the two manifolds.

Catalytic C–H Alkylation via Borrowing Hydrogen Strategy: Dehydrogenative Coupling of Alcohols and Hydrocarbons

The borrowing hydrogen (hydrogen autotransfer) strategy couples alcohols with C–H bonds through a sequence of dehydrogenation, C–C bond formation, and hydrogenation, all mediated by a single transition‑metal catalyst. The alcohol is first dehydrogenated to the corresponding carbonyl compound (aldehyde or ketone) with release of H₂ (or transfer to a hydrogen acceptor). The carbonyl then undergoes a C–H activation step—often a metal‑catalyzed addition to an arene, heteroarene, or alkane—forming a new C–C bond. Finally, the metal‑hydride species generated in the first step hydrogenates the resulting unsaturated intermediate, delivering the alkylated product and regenerating the catalyst.

Typical catalysts are Ru(II) complexes (e.g., RuCl₂(PPh₃)₃, Ru‑NHC complexes), Ir(I/III) complexes (e.g., [Ir(cod)Cl]₂ with phosphine ligands), and more recently earth‑abundant Mn(I) and Fe(II) pincer complexes. The choice of metal influences the dehydrogenation rate and the tolerance of functional groups. Ru catalysts are highly active for primary and secondary alcohols, while Mn pincer complexes operate under milder conditions and avoid precious metals.

Regioselectivity in the C–H alkylation step depends on the substrate class. For arenes, the reaction proceeds via electrophilic aromatic substitution (if the metal generates a cationic alkyl species) or via directed C–H activation when a DG is present. For heteroarenes (indoles, pyrroles), the most nucleophilic position (C‑3 of indole) is alkylated preferentially. For unactivated alkanes, a HAT step from a high‑valent metal‑oxo intermediate (generated in situ from the metal‑hydride and an oxidant) can abstract a hydrogen atom, forming an alkyl radical that couples with the carbonyl‑derived electrophile.

Applications include the synthesis of benzyl‑substituted arenes from benzyl alcohol and benzene (producing diphenylmethane derivatives), the alkylation of indoles with ethanol to give 3‑ethylindoles, and the upgrading of bio‑derived platform chemicals (e.g., 5‑hydroxymethylfurfural) to higher‑value alkylated aromatics. The overall atom economy is high because the only by‑product is water (from the net dehydrogenation/hydrogenation cycle). Challenges remain in controlling over‑alkylation and in achieving high turnover numbers for sterically hindered alcohols.

Electrochemical C–H Fluorination of Hydrocarbons: Mediated and Direct Anodic Pathways

Electrochemical fluorination offers a reagent‑free method to introduce fluorine into C–H bonds, avoiding hazardous gaseous F₂ or stoichiometric fluorinating agents. Two principal pathways exist: direct anodic oxidation of the substrate to a radical cation followed by fluoride capture, and mediated electrocatalysis where a redox‑active mediator (e.g., Selectfluor, N‑fluorobenzenesulfonimide (NFSI), or a catalytic amount of a high‑valent metal‑fluoride complex) shuttles fluorine equivalents to the substrate.

In direct anodic fluorination, a hydrocarbon (often an electron‑rich arene or heteroarene) is oxidized at the anode (typically boron‑doped diamond (BDD) or Pt) to its radical cation. The radical cation is highly electrophilic and reacts rapidly with fluoride ions (from a supporting electrolyte such as Et₄N·HF or TBAF) to give a fluorinated radical, which after a second oxidation and deprotonation yields the aryl fluoride. This pathway is limited to substrates with low oxidation potentials (< +2.0 V vs SCE) and often suffers from over‑oxidation and polymer formation.

Mediated electrofluorination circumvent the high potential requirement. A catalytic mediator (e.g., a Mn(salen)F complex or a Cu(II)‑fluoride species) is oxidized at the anode to a high‑valent metal‑fluoride (Mn⁽ⱽ⁾=F, Cu⁽ᴵᴵᴵ⁾‑F). This species performs electrophilic fluorination of the C–H bond via a concerted or stepwise mechanism (often involving HAT or polar C–H activation). The reduced mediator is re‑oxidized at the anode, closing the catalytic cycle. This approach enables fluorination of unactivated alkanes at potentials as low as +1.2 V vs SCE, with improved selectivity for tertiary > secondary > primary C–H bonds.

Flow‑cell reactors enhance mass transport and allow precise control of residence time, which is critical for suppressing side reactions such as dimerization of fluorinated radicals. Recent developments include the use of heterogeneous mediators immobilized on carbon felt electrodes, enabling continuous operation for > 200 h without mediator leaching. Applications range from late‑stage fluorination of drug candidates (improving metabolic stability) to the synthesis of fluorinated polymers precursors. The main limitations are the need for anhydrous fluoride sources (to avoid HF formation) and the relatively low current efficiencies for highly fluorinated products.

  • Assuming all HAT catalysts operate via the same polarity‑matching rules; thiyl radicals are nucleophilic, while alkoxy radicals are electrophilic.
  • Overlooking the requirement for a directing group in copper‑catalyzed alkynylation of simple arenes; undirected reactions give poor regiocontrol.
  • Confusing enamine catalysis (α‑functionalization of carbonyls) with iminium catalysis (β‑functionalization of α,β‑unsaturated carbonyls).
  • Neglecting that borrowing hydrogen alkylation of alkanes typically requires a high‑valent metal‑oxo intermediate, not just the metal‑hydride.
  • Treating direct anodic fluorination as universally applicable; it fails for substrates with oxidation potentials above the solvent window.
  • Ignoring the need for anhydrous fluoride sources in electrochemical fluorination; water generates HF and degrades the mediator.
